# Blood Glucose Regulation in Type 1 Diabetes — A Mechanistic and Clinical Reference

## Scope and rationale

Type 1 diabetes (T1D) is characterized by autoimmune destruction of pancreatic β-cells and the consequent absolute or near-absolute deficiency of endogenous insulin. The clinical sequelae — life-long dependence on subcutaneously delivered exogenous insulin, recurrent hypoglycemia, and a residual lifetime risk of microvascular and macrovascular complications — are well established. The *physiology* by which exogenous insulin replacement only imperfectly substitutes for the native β-cell, however, remains incompletely articulated outside the specialist literature. Subcutaneous insulin pharmacokinetics, the loss of paracrine α-cell regulation, the disruption of the portal-peripheral insulin gradient, and the second-order effects of antecedent hypoglycemia on autonomic counterregulation each contribute to a glycemic phenotype that cannot be predicted from a "simple insulin replacement" model. This document is a mechanistic and quantitative reference for the physiological factors that govern blood glucose (BG) in established T1D, written for clinicians, researchers, and modellers who require a citable synthesis at peer-review depth.

## Clinical relevance

Glycemic management in T1D operates on a substrate of intersecting physiological systems that vary substantially within and between individuals. A given insulin dose, carbohydrate intake, or exercise bout produces a different glycemic trajectory depending on insulin on board, antecedent glycemia, hormonal milieu, hepatic glycogen status, gastric emptying, time of day, menstrual phase, and dozens of additional modulators detailed in the chapters that follow. Quantitative knowledge of these modulators — typical magnitudes, time courses, inter-individual variability, and T1D-specific deviations from healthy physiology — underpins evidence-based dose adjustment, structured patient education, technology development (closed-loop control, decision-support algorithms, simulation platforms), and meaningful interpretation of continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) data. The document is structured to support all four use cases.

## Document roadmap

The 29 sections are organized into eight parts that progress from physiological fundamentals to applied technology:

- **Part 1: Glucose homeostasis** (§1–§4) — distribution kinetics across plasma, interstitial, and intracellular pools; hepatic glucose production; renal glucose handling; and brain glucose consumption. The physiological substrate on which all subsequent perturbations act.
- **Part 2: Macronutrient handling** (§5–§6) — digestion, absorption, and postprandial appearance kinetics for carbohydrate, fat, and protein, including the often-overlooked glycemic effects of the latter two.
- **Part 3: Insulin pharmacology** (§7) — pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of subcutaneous insulin analogs from injection-site depot to receptor binding to systemic effect.
- **Part 4: Physical activity** (§8–§10b) — aerobic, anaerobic, and inflammatory exercise responses, plus the mirror-image relaxation/meditation effects on glycemic variability and counterregulation.
- **Part 5: Hormones and circadian rhythm** (§11–§16) — counterregulatory hormone biology, the dawn phenomenon, the Somogyi effect (with modern CGM-era reframing), diurnal insulin-sensitivity variation, menstrual cycle effects, and seasonal patterns.
- **Part 6: Lifestyle and external factors** (§17–§22) — acute illness and infection, sleep deprivation, alcohol, psychological stress, temperature and climate, and injection-site and lipohypertrophy biology.
- **Part 7: Complications and warning signs** (§23–§26) — ketone-body biochemistry and diabetic ketoacidosis; insulin overdose and the structural limits of counterregulation; non-linear insulin dose-response; and glucotoxic insulin resistance.
- **Part 8: Technology and research models** (§27–§29) — CGM sensor technology and signal processing; mathematical modeling frameworks used in T1D research; and a comparative evaluation of the major glucose-insulin models.

Each section provides the underlying biochemistry and kinetics, quantitative parameters with primary citations, the T1D-specific phenotype, and identified controversies or knowledge gaps. Glossary-style inline parentheses introduce specialist terminology at first use within each section so that individual chapters remain readable in isolation. A final per-section *Implementation* footer flags which aspects of the section's physiology are reproduced in the companion T1D simulator and points to the relevant entries in `MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md` for readers concerned with computational reproduction.

## Out of scope

This document focuses on the physiology and pharmacology of blood-glucose regulation in established T1D. It does *not* cover: type 2 diabetes-specific mechanisms (β-cell exhaustion, primary insulin resistance) except where T2D evidence is the only available proxy for T1D; the immunology and prevention of T1D (autoantibodies, HLA risk, disease-modifying therapies); the operational details of pediatric versus adult dosing strategy beyond what is needed to interpret physiology (referenced in §17 sick-day rules but not given dedicated treatment); psychosocial determinants of self-management and adherence; the comparative effectiveness of insulin delivery devices beyond their pharmacokinetic differences; and cost-effectiveness or healthcare-system considerations. Topics where the underlying physiology is described here but the simulator implementation differs — typically by deliberate simplification for tractability — are flagged in the per-section Implementation footers and detailed in `MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md`.

## A note on quantitative claims and uncertainty

Numerical values throughout the document are reported with their primary source. Where the underlying study was small, methodologically heterogeneous, or restricted to specific populations (healthy young-adult volunteers, narrow age range, single-ethnicity cohort), the limitation is flagged inline at the point of citation rather than collected in a separate caveats section. Many of the canonical T1D dose-response and counterregulation studies were conducted in young-adult cohorts of limited demographic diversity; pediatric, elderly, pregnant, and non-European populations remain comparatively under-studied. Readers should treat published thresholds — insulin EC50 values, hypoglycemic counterregulatory thresholds, gastric-emptying kinetics, and similar — as population descriptors rather than individual parameters. Where the literature is genuinely contested (the Somogyi effect's clinical frequency, the relative contributions of insulin, GABA, and zinc to α-cell paracrine suppression, the dominance of any single hyperglycemic damage pathway in Brownlee's framework), the controversy is presented explicitly rather than resolved.

# Part 1: Glucose homeostasis

<a name="glucose-distribution"></a>
## 1. How glucose moves through the body

Plasma glucose is the regulated variable in glucose homeostasis, but it is only one of three operational pools through which glucose distributes: the intravascular plasma compartment, the interstitial fluid that bathes peripheral tissues, and the intracellular space. Equilibration kinetics across these pools, together with the molecular machinery that gates each interface, determine how rapidly a meal-derived glucose load or an exogenous insulin dose translates into a change in measured (capillary, venous, or sensor) blood glucose. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), the loss of endogenous insulin secretion preferentially disrupts insulin-dependent transport into skeletal muscle and adipose tissue, redistributing the glucose load toward the plasma and interstitial compartments and underwriting much of the postprandial hyperglycemia observed clinically.

### Mechanism

**Transmembrane transport is GLUT-isoform-specific.** Glucose, a polar hexose, cannot cross plasma membranes by passive diffusion and depends on facilitative GLUT (SLC2) carriers. The four isoforms relevant for whole-body glucose distribution have distinct kinetic properties and tissue distributions ([Chadt & Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591907/); [Sun et al., 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37358764/)):

- **GLUT1** (ubiquitous; erythrocytes, blood-brain-barrier endothelium, basal expression in most tissues): high affinity (Km ≈ 1–2 mmol/L); supports basal glucose uptake at concentrations well below normoglycemia.
- **GLUT2** (hepatocytes, pancreatic β-cells, intestinal/renal basolateral membranes): low affinity (Km ≈ 17 mmol/L), high capacity, and operationally **bidirectional**. Sun et al. (2023) note that GLUT2 "allows the glucose concentration to reach balance on the bilateral sides of the cellular membrane," underpinning its role in hepatic glucose efflux during fasting and uptake during the postprandial period.
- **GLUT3** (neurons, brain parenchyma): very high affinity (Km ≈ 1.4 mmol/L; values derive from heterologous expression-system kinetics and rodent studies — direct in vivo human Km measurement is not feasible), permitting near-saturated glucose uptake even when plasma glucose falls into hypoglycemic territory.
- **GLUT4** (skeletal muscle, cardiac muscle, adipose tissue): Km ≈ 5 mmol/L. In the basal state, ~95% of GLUT4 resides in intracellular GLUT4 storage vesicles (GSVs); insulin signaling (via PI3K (phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase, generates PIP3 for downstream kinase activation) → Akt (protein kinase B, central insulin signaling node) → AS160/TBC1D4 (Rab-GAP proteins gating GLUT4 vesicle exocytosis)) and contraction signaling (via AMPK (AMP-activated protein kinase, energy-sensing kinase activated by contraction), Rac1 (small Rho-family GTPase driving actin remodeling for GLUT4 docking)) trigger exocytotic translocation to the sarcolemma/T-tubular membrane, increasing surface GLUT4 by 5- to 10-fold ([Chadt & Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591907/); [Sylow et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27739515/)).

**Phosphorylation traps glucose intracellularly — except in liver and kidney.** Once across the membrane, glucose is phosphorylated to glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) by hexokinase II (muscle, adipose) or glucokinase (hexokinase IV; hepatocytes, β-cells). G6P is anionic and cannot exit through any GLUT isoform. Whether glucose can return to the circulation therefore depends on whether the cell expresses **glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase)**, the gluconeogenic enzyme that hydrolyzes G6P back to free glucose. G6Pase is robustly expressed in hepatocytes and renal proximal tubule cells, but is essentially absent from skeletal muscle and adipose tissue. As a consequence:

- In muscle and adipose tissue, the GLUT4-hexokinase couple constitutes a near-unidirectional glucose sink under physiological conditions. Glucose imported via GLUT4 commits to local glycolysis, glycogen synthesis (the dominant fate, ~75–80% of insulin-stimulated muscle glucose disposal — [DeFronzo et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7026740/)), or lipogenesis. There is no significant counterflux of free glucose to plasma.
- In liver and kidney, GLUT2-mediated transport is bidirectional and the G6Pase pathway permits net glucose release. The liver thus alternates between net uptake (postprandial, high portal-vein glucose and insulin) and net release (fasting, low insulin, counterregulatory hormone activation).

**The two-compartment plasma–tissue model abstracts this asymmetry.** Tracer-based glucose kinetic models (e.g., the Steele single-pool, the Cobelli–Bergman two-compartment minimal model, and the [Hovorka 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15382830/) glucoregulatory model used in this simulator) describe glucose dynamics with an "accessible" plasma pool Q1 and a "non-accessible" peripheral pool Q2, exchanging at rates defined by k12 and the insulin-modulated rate x1. Mechanistically, Q2 is best interpreted as the rapidly equilibrating extracellular interstitial pool plus a model-fitted contribution from the slowly turning over intracellular pool. The k12·Q2 backward flux in such models is therefore not literal release of glucose from muscle myocytes; rather, it is the equilibration of interstitial glucose with plasma down a concentration gradient ([Cengiz & Tamborlane, 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19469670/)).

### Quantitative characterization

| Quantity | Value | Notes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Plasma glucose distribution volume (initial, V₁) | ~0.10 L/kg (~7 L for 70 kg adult) | Reflects central extracellular fluid; estimated from 3-min IV glucose increment | [Ishihara et al., 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10233198/) |
| Total glucose distribution volume (Vₘ, used in two-compartment models) | 0.16 L/kg | Tracer-derived parameter used in the Hovorka 2004 model; encompasses plasma plus rapidly equilibrating extracellular pool. Primary IVGTT identification ([Hovorka et al., 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11934663/)) is paywalled — see wishlist | [Hovorka et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15382830/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

In T1D, β-cell loss eliminates endogenous insulin secretion, so plasma insulin is determined entirely by exogenous administration (subcutaneous, intraperitoneal, or — in research settings — intravenous routes). Because GLUT4 surface density is insulin-gated, the absence of postprandial portal insulin pulses produces three quantitatively important distribution shifts:

1. **Reduced peripheral glucose disposal capacity.** Skeletal muscle glucose uptake is the largest insulin-sensitive sink (~75–85% of insulin-mediated disposal; [DeFronzo et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7026740/)). Without sufficient circulating insulin, GLUT4 translocation is blunted, and the ingested carbohydrate load accumulates in the plasma–interstitial pool rather than partitioning into muscle glycogen. This is the dominant mechanism of postprandial hyperglycemia in under-insulinized T1D.

2. **Preserved non-insulin-dependent uptake.** GLUT1 (erythrocytes, brain endothelium), GLUT2 (liver, β-cells now lost, intestine, kidney), and GLUT3 (neurons) remain insulin-independent. Cerebral glucose extraction is therefore largely preserved across normoglycemic-to-mildly-hyperglycemic ranges, but becomes rate-limited when plasma glucose falls below ~3.0 mmol/L because GLUT1 transport at the blood-brain barrier becomes substrate-limited. This kinetic boundary defines the neuroglycopenic threshold rather than any insulin signaling event.

3. **Preserved hepatic glucose efflux capacity.** GLUT2 and G6Pase remain expressed and functional, so the liver can still release glucose during fasting or counterregulatory activation. In under-insulinized T1D, however, hepatic glucose production is also pathologically elevated because insulin's normal restraint on glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis is absent — a topic developed in §2.

4. **Exercise as an insulin-independent disposal route.** Muscle contraction translocates GLUT4 via an AMPK/Rac1-dependent pathway that does not require insulin signaling ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27739515/)). This is the mechanistic basis for exercise-induced glucose lowering in T1D and explains why the same exercise bout that normalizes blood glucose in a person with adequate insulin on board can precipitate hypoglycemia when residual circulating insulin is high.

5. **Sensor-to-blood-glucose lag.** Continuous glucose monitors (CGMs) measure interstitial glucose in subcutaneous tissue. The intrinsic plasma–interstitial physiological delay is 5–6 min in healthy adults ([Basu et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24009261/)) and modestly longer (7–10 min) in fasting T1D, with additional sensor-electrochemistry lag of 1–5 min. This is clinically relevant during rapid glucose excursions: during a fast fall, sensor glucose overestimates true plasma glucose, which is the kinetic basis for treating impending hypoglycemia on the trend rather than the absolute reading.

NB: only the two-compartment plasma–tissue exchange (Q1, Q2 with k₁₂ = 0.066 min⁻¹) and distribution volume (V_G = 0.16 L/kg body weight) are modeled. The Q2 pool conflates interstitial and intracellular kinetics, the asymmetric phosphorylation trap (G6Pase present in liver/kidney, absent in muscle/adipose) is not represented, and individual GLUT transporter dynamics (GLUT1/2/3 vs insulin-regulated GLUT4) are abstracted into a single insulin-dependent disposal rate via the x1 and x2 insulin-action states.*

<a name="hepatic-glucose-production"></a>
## 2. Hepatic glucose production

The liver is the principal organ of endogenous glucose production (EGP) in the post-absorptive state, contributing approximately 75–85% of systemic glucose flux, with the renal cortex accounting for the remainder via gluconeogenesis ([Petersen et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/); [Gerich, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20546255/)). EGP is the sum of two distinct fluxes — glycogenolysis (breakdown of stored glycogen to release free glucose) and gluconeogenesis (de novo glucose synthesis from non-carbohydrate precursors) — whose relative contributions shift dynamically as a function of feeding state, hormonal milieu, and substrate availability. In healthy adults, basal EGP is tightly clamped at 1.8–2.2 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ (≈ 130–160 mg/min for a 70 kg subject), matching peripheral glucose disposal and stabilising fasting plasma glucose at 4–6 mmol/L.

In type 1 diabetes (T1D), EGP regulation is disturbed on three axes: (i) the physiological portal-peripheral insulin gradient is abolished by subcutaneous delivery, leaving the hepatocyte relatively insulin-deficient at any given peripheral insulin level; (ii) the alpha-cell counterregulatory glucagon response to hypoglycaemia is lost within months to years of diagnosis; and (iii) hepatic glycogen stores are reduced and gluconeogenesis is paradoxically elevated in poor glycaemic control. These features together explain the high inter-meal glucose variability, the dawn phenomenon, and the limited endogenous defence against insulin-induced hypoglycaemia that characterise T1D.

### Mechanism

#### Glycogenolysis

Hepatic glycogen is degraded along a three-enzyme cytoplasmic pathway. **Glycogen phosphorylase** (PYGL, liver isoform) cleaves α-1,4-glycosidic bonds at the non-reducing termini of glycogen, releasing **glucose-1-phosphate**. **Phosphoglucomutase** (PGM1) interconverts G1P and **glucose-6-phosphate**. The endoplasmic-reticulum membrane enzyme **glucose-6-phosphatase** (G6Pase, encoded by *G6PC*), in concert with the G6P transporter SLC37A4, hydrolyses G6P to free glucose, which is exported via GLUT2. Hepatic glycogen content in well-fed adults is approximately 80–100 g (≈ 6 g·kg⁻¹ liver, equivalent to liver glycogen concentrations of 200–300 mmol/L by ¹³C-MRS), depleting by ~32% over a 12-hour overnight fast and approaching exhaustion by 24–36 hours ([Petersen et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/); [Rothman et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1948033/)).

The activation cascade is: glucagon → GCGR (glucagon receptor, Gαs-coupled seven-transmembrane protein) → adenylyl cyclase → ↑ cAMP → **PKA** → phosphorylation of **phosphorylase kinase** (Ser residues on β-subunit) → phosphorylation of glycogen phosphorylase b at **Ser14** → catalytically active phosphorylase a. Adrenaline activates the same axis via β2-adrenergic receptors, with a parallel α1-adrenergic IP3/DAG/Ca²⁺ branch contributing to phosphorylase kinase activation through its calmodulin γ-subunit. Liver phosphorylase a is allosterically inhibited by free glucose, providing a feedback brake when plasma glucose rises.

#### Gluconeogenesis

De novo glucose synthesis proceeds from three-carbon precursors: **lactate** (Cori cycle, the lactate → liver → glucose recycling pathway, dominant in the post-absorptive state), **alanine** and other glucogenic amino acids (Cahill cycle, alanine-based gluconeogenesis from muscle proteolysis), and **glycerol** released by adipose lipolysis. Pyruvate enters the mitochondrion and is carboxylated by **pyruvate carboxylase** (PC, biotin-dependent) to oxaloacetate — the anaplerotic entry step. Oxaloacetate is then decarboxylated and phosphorylated by **phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase** (PEPCK; cytosolic *PCK1* and mitochondrial *PCK2* isoforms) to phosphoenolpyruvate, which traverses a series of glycolytic enzymes in reverse before encountering the second rate-limiting step, **fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase** (FBP1). The pathway terminates at the same G6Pase step as glycogenolysis. Pyruvate carboxylase activity is allosterically driven by **acetyl-CoA** generated from hepatic β-oxidation, providing a direct mechanistic link between insulin deficiency, lipolysis, ketogenesis and accelerated gluconeogenesis — central to the pathophysiology of diabetic ketoacidosis.

After an overnight fast, gluconeogenesis contributes ~50% of EGP in healthy humans, rising to ~64% at 22 hours, ~82% at 36 hours and ~96% by 64 hours of fasting ([Rothman et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1948033/)).

#### Hormonal regulation

**Insulin** is the dominant suppressor of EGP and acts through both direct hepatic and indirect (extra-hepatic) routes. Direct effects predominate acutely ([Edgerton et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28352665/)): insulin binds the hepatic insulin receptor (INSR) → recruits IRS-1/IRS-2 (insulin receptor substrate adaptor proteins, obligate signaling scaffolds) → activates PI3K (phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase, generates PIP3 for Akt activation) (p110α/p85) → generates PIP3 → activates PDK1 → phosphorylates **Akt** (protein kinase B, central insulin signaling node) (PKB) at Thr308 and Ser473. Akt phosphorylates the transcription factor **FoxO1** (transcription factor controlling gluconeogenic gene expression) at Thr24, Ser256 and Ser319, triggering 14-3-3 binding, cytoplasmic sequestration and proteasomal degradation. Because FoxO1 binds insulin-response elements in the *PCK1* and *G6PC* promoters and recruits PGC-1α, its exclusion from the nucleus abrogates transcription of the rate-limiting gluconeogenic enzymes ([Hatting et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28868790/)). In parallel, Akt activates glycogen synthase via inhibition of GSK3, redirecting G6P from output to storage. Indirect effects include insulin-mediated suppression of adipose lipolysis (reducing glycerol and FFA flux), reduced alpha-cell glucagon secretion, and CNS-mediated vagal signals.

**Glucagon** is the principal acute counter-regulator. GCGR (Gαs-coupled) → adenylyl cyclase → cAMP → PKA → CREB phosphorylation → transcriptional upregulation of *PCK1* and *G6PC* via the CRTC2 (CREB-regulated transcription coactivator 2, mediates glucagon-driven gluconeogenic gene activation) coactivator. PKA simultaneously activates phosphorylase kinase (acute glycogenolysis, onset within minutes) and inhibits glycogen synthase. **Cortisol** acts genomically via the glucocorticoid receptor on glucocorticoid-response elements in *PCK1* and *G6PC*, and is permissive for glucagon and adrenaline action; its effects develop over 30–60 minutes. **Adrenaline** drives glycogenolysis acutely (β2 → cAMP/PKA; α1 → IP3/DAG/Ca²⁺) and inhibits insulin secretion. **Growth hormone** acts indirectly by inducing post-receptor hepatic insulin resistance over hours, and is a principal driver of the dawn phenomenon ([Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24265365/)).

### Quantitative characterization

| Parameter | Healthy adults | Type 1 diabetes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Basal EGP, post-absorptive | 1.8–2.2 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ (≈ 11–12 µmol·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹) | 13–17 µmol·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ (poorly controlled ~60% above control) | [Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/); [Petersen et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15356077/) |
| Hepatic vs renal contribution | Liver 75–85% / kidney 15–25% | Similar split, with elevated total | [Gerich, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20546255/) |
| Hepatic glycogen pool (fed) | 80–100 g (200–300 mmol/L liver) | Reduced; postprandial synthesis ~70% lower in poorly controlled T1D | [Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/); [Petersen et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28731034/) |
| Glycogen depletion timeline | ~32% loss over overnight fast; near-exhaustion by 24–36 h | Faster; lower starting pool | [Rothman et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1948033/) |
| GNG : GLY split (overnight fast) | ~50:50 | ~74:20 in poor control; 41:35 with intensified therapy | [Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/); [Petersen et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15356077/) |
| GNG share with prolonged fasting | 64% at 22 h; 82% at 36 h; 96% at 64 h | Not quantitatively characterised in T1D | [Rothman et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1948033/) |
| Portal : peripheral insulin gradient | ~2.5–3 : 1 in basal/secretory state | ~1 : 1 with subcutaneous delivery | [Edgerton et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28352665/); [Edgerton et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33813879/) |
| Time constant — direct hepatic insulin suppression of EGP | ~15 min to near-maximal effect | Blunted by absent portal gradient | [Edgerton et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28352665/) |
| Time constant — indirect insulin effects (lipolysis, glucagon) | 60+ min | Delayed | [Edgerton et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28352665/) |
| Insulin EC50 for EGP suppression | Not robustly established as a single value; physiological hyperinsulinaemia (~30–60 µU/mL peripheral) suppresses GNG by ~20% and GLY almost completely | Right-shifted by portal-peripheral gradient loss | [Hatting et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28868790/) |
| Dawn-phenomenon BG rise (T1D) | n/a | 15–25 mg/dL (0.8–1.4 mmol/L) above nocturnal nadir | [Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24265365/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

**Loss of the portal-peripheral insulin gradient.** In intact endocrine pancreas, ~50% of secreted insulin is extracted on first hepatic pass, producing portal-vein concentrations ~2.5–3-fold higher than peripheral plasma ([Edgerton et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33813879/)). Subcutaneous insulin delivery enters the systemic circulation directly, so peripheral and portal levels equilibrate. To achieve adequate hepatic insulin signalling — and hence adequate EGP suppression — patients must run peripheral hyperinsulinaemia. This drives skeletal-muscle and adipose-tissue glucose disposal beyond physiological limits, contributing to glycaemic variability, weight gain, lipogenesis and a 17–53% reduction in measured insulin sensitivity in long-term peripherally-treated cohorts ([Edgerton et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33813879/)). The corollary: at any given peripheral insulin concentration, hepatic EGP is less well suppressed in T1D than in non-diabetic controls.

**Reduced and dysregulated hepatic glycogen.** Postprandial hepatic glycogen synthesis is up to 70% lower in poorly controlled T1D than in matched controls, with a reciprocal 60% increase in fasting EGP almost entirely attributable to elevated gluconeogenesis ([Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/); [Petersen et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15356077/)). Intensified subcutaneous therapy partially restores both glycogen synthesis and EGP towards control values, indicating reversibility. The smaller mobilisable glycogen pool limits acute glycaemic defence during exercise or fasting and shifts counter-regulation onto the slower gluconeogenic axis.

**Impaired alpha-cell glucagon counterregulation.** The glucagon response to hypoglycaemia is selectively lost in T1D, often within months of diagnosis and essentially universal by 5 years ([Bisgaard Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/)). Proposed mechanisms include (a) loss of paracrine signalling from depleted beta cells (the "intra-islet insulin switch-off" required to disinhibit alpha cells), (b) intrinsic alpha-cell glucose-sensing defects, and (c) excess somatostatin tone from delta cells. Glucagon responses to non-glycaemic stimuli (amino acids, exercise, insulin withdrawal) are preserved but blunted. The defect appears irreversible and is a principal substrate for hypoglycaemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF; progressive blunting of counterregulatory responses to repeated hypoglycaemia).

**Paradoxically elevated EGP in poor control.** Despite chronic hyperglycaemia, fasting EGP in poorly controlled T1D is ~60% higher than in non-diabetic controls — driven by inadequate hepatic insulin signalling (peripheral subcutaneous route), elevated counterregulatory tone (cortisol, GH), increased FFA flux from uninhibited lipolysis (acetyl-CoA-driven pyruvate carboxylase activation), and loss of insulin-induced glycogen storage ([Petersen et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15356077/); [Kacerovsky et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21562079/)).

**Dawn phenomenon.** Nocturnal pulsatile growth-hormone secretion between approximately 03:00 and 07:00 induces hepatic and peripheral insulin resistance with a 4–8 hour lag, raising EGP and producing a 15–25 mg/dL (0.8–1.4 mmol/L) rise from nocturnal nadir to pre-breakfast in T1D when basal insulin replacement is adequate ([Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24265365/)). Cortisol's morning peak adds a permissive component. Healthy individuals neutralise this through a small endogenous insulin pulse; T1D patients without that pulse show the rise as morning hyperglycaemia.

### Sources

Glycogen depletion limits acute counterregulation in the simulator.*

<a name="renal-glucose-handling"></a>
## 3. The kidneys in glucose homeostasis

The kidneys contribute to systemic glucose homeostasis through three quantitatively distinct mechanisms: glomerular filtration of glucose with near-complete tubular reabsorption, gluconeogenesis in the renal cortex, and direct glucose uptake by the renal medulla. All three are altered in T1D in ways that affect both acute glycemic management and long-term renal outcomes.

### Mechanism

**Filtration and reabsorption.** With a glomerular filtration rate (GFR) of approximately 180 L/day, the healthy kidney filters approximately 180 g of glucose daily, essentially all of which is reabsorbed under euglycemic conditions ([Gerich, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20546255/); [Ghezzi et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30132032/)). Reabsorption occurs in the proximal tubule via two apical sodium-glucose cotransporters arranged in series, with basolateral exit via a facilitative uniporter:

- **SGLT2** (sodium-glucose cotransporter 2, the dominant renal glucose reabsorber; gene *SLC5A2*) is expressed in the early proximal tubule (S1/S2 segments) and mediates approximately 90% of filtered glucose reabsorption. It is a low-affinity, high-capacity transporter (Km ≈ 5 mmol/L) operating with a 1:1 Na⁺:glucose stoichiometry.
- **SGLT1** (sodium-glucose cotransporter 1, high-affinity low-capacity reabsorber; gene *SLC5A1*) is expressed in the late proximal tubule (S3 segment) and reabsorbs the remaining ~10% under physiologic conditions. It is high-affinity, low-capacity (Km ≈ 0.4 mmol/L), with 2:1 Na⁺:glucose stoichiometry.
- **GLUT2** (gene *SLC2A2*) on the basolateral membrane returns reabsorbed glucose to peritubular blood by facilitative diffusion ([Ghezzi et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30132032/)).

**Renal gluconeogenesis.** In the post-absorptive state, the renal cortex contributes approximately 20–25% of total endogenous glucose production via gluconeogenesis; the remainder is hepatic. The renal cortex uses lactate, glutamine, and glycerol as principal substrates and is stimulated by epinephrine and inhibited by insulin ([Gerich, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20546255/)). The renal medulla, in contrast, is a net glucose consumer. Renal gluconeogenesis is upregulated severalfold in type 2 diabetes; T1D-specific data are sparse, but the same regulatory architecture (insulin suppression) implies that endogenous glucose production from the kidney is inadequately suppressed during insulin deficiency.

**Glucosuria.** Urinary glucose excretion begins when the filtered load exceeds the tubular maximum (TmG; the reabsorptive saturation point, ~260–350 mg/min/1.73 m²). The plasma concentration at which urinary glucose first appears is the renal threshold. Because individual nephrons saturate at slightly different concentrations ("nephron heterogeneity"), the threshold is splayed rather than abrupt — excretion increases gradually over a range of plasma glucose concentrations rather than as a sharp step.

### Quantitative characterization

| Parameter | Healthy adults | Source |
|-----------|---------------|--------|
| GFR | ~180 L/day | Gerich (2010) |
| Daily filtered glucose | ~180 g | Gerich (2010) |
| Renal threshold (population mean) | ~10–11 mmol/L | Gerich (2010); Poudel (2013) |
| Tubular maximum (TmG) | 260–350 mg/min/1.73 m² (~375 mg/min absolute) | Gerich (2010); Poudel (2013) |
| SGLT2 share of reabsorption | ~90% | Ghezzi et al. (2018) |
| SGLT1 share of reabsorption | ~10% | Ghezzi et al. (2018) |
| Renal share of post-absorptive endogenous glucose production | 20–25% (~2.0–2.5 μmol/kg/min) | Gerich (2010) |
| Renal glucose uptake (post-absorptive) | 103 ± 10 μmol/min | Gerich (2010) |

Inter-individual variation is substantial. In a controlled glucose-infusion study of 23 T1D patients, the renal threshold varied two-fold (range 6.0–14.3 mmol/L) and the TmG varied two-fold (range 0.93–1.98 mmol/min); the renal threshold was inversely correlated with creatinine clearance, and TmG was inversely correlated with both age and diabetes duration ([Johansen et al., 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6714538/)). This degree of variation means glycosuria is a poor surrogate for blood glucose at the individual level.

### T1D-specific aspects

**Glomerular hyperfiltration.** Whole-kidney GFR is elevated (up to ~162 mL/min/1.73 m²) in 10–67% of patients early in T1D, with mean GFR increased by approximately 27% relative to healthy controls in recently diagnosed disease ([Tonneijck et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28143897/)). Three overlapping mechanisms have been proposed:

1. *Tubular hypothesis* — chronic hyperglycemia upregulates SGLT2-mediated reabsorption in the proximal tubule, reducing sodium delivery to the macula densa and suppressing tubuloglomerular feedback (macula-densa-mediated GFR self-regulation via afferent arteriolar tone); the resulting decrease in afferent arteriolar resistance raises GFR.
2. *Vascular hypothesis* — imbalance in vasoactive factors (angiotensin II, prostaglandins, nitric oxide) increases glomerular capillary pressure.
3. *Ultrastructural hypothesis* — proximal tubular hypertrophy from disease onset, preceding functional changes.

Hyperfiltration is reversible with improved glycemic control: in T1D, lowering HbA1c from 10% to 7% reduced measured GFR from 149 to 129 mL/min/1.73 m² (16% decrease) ([Tonneijck et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28143897/)). Hyperfiltration is an independent risk factor for the initiation and progression of diabetic nephropathy.

**Increased TmG and renal threshold in poor control.** Both SGLT2 mRNA and protein expression are upregulated in proximal tubules in poorly controlled diabetes, raising TmG by approximately 20% in both T1D and T2D ([Poudel, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24014904/)). This delays the onset of glucosuria during hyperglycemic excursions, removing one of the body's natural restraints on glucose accumulation and contributing to sustained hyperglycemia.

**Osmotic diuresis.** Glucose is osmotically active in the tubular lumen; each gram excreted obligates approximately 15 mL of free water. At sustained plasma glucose concentrations of 25 mmol/L, urinary glucose loss can reach 200–500 g/day with corresponding fluid loss of 3–7 L/day. Clinically: polyuria, polydipsia, and dehydration — the classic presenting triad of decompensated T1D, alongside ketoacidosis.

**SGLT2 inhibitors as adjunct therapy.** The SGLT2 inhibitor class (dapagliflozin, empagliflozin, sotagliflozin) lowers plasma glucose by inducing therapeutic glucosuria of approximately 52–85 g/day, reducing fasting plasma glucose by 0.9–1.7 mmol/L and HbA1c by ~0.5%, with concomitant weight loss and blood pressure reduction ([Poudel, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24014904/)). Despite achieving >80% transporter inhibition in vitro, only 30–50% of filtered glucose is excreted in vivo; the discrepancy is attributed to compensatory upregulation of SGLT1 in the distal proximal tubule.

In T1D, SGLT2 inhibitors are not approved by the FDA. The DEPICT trials (dapagliflozin) and inTandem trials (sotagliflozin, a dual SGLT1/SGLT2 inhibitor) demonstrated HbA1c reductions of approximately 0.5% with weight loss and blood pressure reduction, but with a 2- to 4-fold increase in DKA risk relative to placebo. European approvals as adjunct therapy in T1D were subsequently withdrawn.

**Euglycemic ketoacidosis.** SGLT2 inhibition can precipitate ketoacidosis at plasma glucose <11 mmol/L (200 mg/dL) in approximately 30–40% of cases — atypical for classic DKA. The mechanism is a three-step pathway: (1) urinary glucose loss reduces the glucose stimulus for endogenous insulin secretion (in T2D) or facilitates exogenous insulin dose reduction (in T1D); (2) the resulting decrease in the insulin/glucagon ratio activates hepatic carnitine palmitoyltransferase-1 and accelerates ketogenesis; (3) the absence of frank hyperglycemia delays clinical recognition ([Ogawa & Sakaguchi, 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27042271/)). Triggers include surgery, infection, fasting, dehydration, and unilateral insulin dose reduction. T1D — characterized by absolute insulin deficiency — confers the highest risk.

**Long-term renal outcomes.** Diabetic nephropathy develops in approximately 30% of T1D patients over 20–30 years and is the leading cause of end-stage renal disease in this population. Early hyperfiltration is a predictor of subsequent albuminuria and GFR decline. SGLT2 inhibition reduces albuminuria and slows GFR decline in both T1D and T2D, with renoprotective effects that appear largely independent of the magnitude of glycemic improvement; the proposed mechanism is restoration of tubuloglomerular feedback and reduction in glomerular capillary pressure.

### Sources

<a name="brain-glucose-consumption"></a>
## 4. The brain's glucose consumption

The adult human brain weighs ~1.4 kg (≈2% of body mass) yet accounts for roughly 20–25% of resting whole-body glucose disposal — making it the single largest obligate glucose consumer at rest ([Mergenthaler et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23933069/)). Unlike skeletal muscle and adipose tissue, cerebral glucose uptake is essentially insulin-independent: it is governed by the activity of facilitative GLUT-family transporters at the blood–brain barrier (BBB) and on neuronal/glial membranes, whose kinetics are sensitive to plasma glucose concentration but not to plasma insulin. This places the brain at the centre of T1D pathophysiology: every aspect of hypoglycemia symptomatology, counterregulation, and clinical risk follows from the brain's near-total dependence on a substrate the patient must titrate exogenously.

In T1D specifically, three brain-related vulnerabilities dominate clinical outcomes: (i) acute neuroglycopenia during exogenous-insulin-induced hypoglycemia, with seizure and coma risk below ~2.0 mmol/L; (ii) hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF), in which recurrent hypoglycemia shifts symptom thresholds downward and impairs the patient's ability to recognize and treat falling glucose ([Cryer, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24095133/)); and (iii) cerebral edema during DKA treatment, predominantly in children, which carries a reported mortality of 20–25% in affected cases (figure derived from heterogeneous case series with substantial ascertainment variability across registries) ([Glaser et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11172153/)). Each of these is mechanistically rooted in the transport and metabolic biology described below.

### Mechanism

#### Cerebral glucose uptake

Glucose enters the brain by sequential facilitative transport across two membranes. At the BBB, glucose crosses the cerebral capillary endothelium via the **55-kDa heavily glycosylated isoform of GLUT1**, expressed at both the luminal (blood-facing) and abluminal (brain-facing) membranes of brain microvascular endothelial cells, with abluminal density approximately 2.5× luminal density ([Vannucci et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21910135/)). Once in the brain interstitium, glucose is taken up by neurons via **GLUT3** (high affinity, Km ≈ 1–2 mmol/L; values from heterologous expression-system kinetics and rodent studies, not directly measured in human brain in vivo) and by astrocytes via the **45-kDa lightly glycosylated GLUT1 isoform** (intermediate affinity).

The aggregate apparent half-saturation constant for unidirectional brain glucose transport (Kt) measured in vivo is **1.5–3.5 mmol/L** in humans, depending on the kinetic model used ([Barros et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19890463/)). Because Kt lies well below the physiological plasma glucose range, BBB transport is operating near saturation at euglycemia (5–7 mmol/L), and brain glucose concentration is approximately a linear function of plasma glucose under steady-state conditions in this range, with a brain:plasma ratio of ~0.2–0.3 (the wider literature range is 0.15–0.30 depending on technique; ¹³C-MRS, microdialysis and PET give somewhat different point estimates). As plasma glucose falls below ~4 mmol/L, transport becomes increasingly limiting; below ~3 mmol/L, brain interstitial glucose drops disproportionately, and intracellular glucose concentration approaches the Km of hexokinase, beginning to limit glycolytic flux. The high affinity of neuronal GLUT3 (Km ≈ 1–2 mmol/L) provides a final safety margin that maintains neuronal glucose uptake even when interstitial concentrations have fallen well below plasma levels.

#### Glucose metabolism in brain

The cerebral metabolic rate of glucose (CMRglc) in awake healthy adults averages **~25–30 μmol/100 g/min** (equivalent to ~5 mg/100 g/min, ~110–120 g/day for a 1.4 kg brain) ([Mergenthaler et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23933069/)). Cerebral glucose utilization is overwhelmingly oxidative: the resting brain consumes O₂ and glucose in a near-stoichiometric ratio (oxygen-glucose index ≈ 5.5, close to the theoretical maximum of 6 for complete oxidation), with only ~10% of glucose released back as lactate at rest. Complete oxidation of one glucose molecule yields ~30–32 ATP, fueling ion pumping (predominantly Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase restoring postsynaptic membrane potentials) which accounts for the majority of neuronal ATP turnover.

Regional CMRglc varies markedly: gray matter consumes glucose at approximately 3–4× the rate of white matter, with thalamus and posterior cingulate among the highest-consuming regions in the resting awake state. Brain glycogen, stored almost exclusively in astrocytes, totals only ~1–2 g per kg brain wet weight — sufficient to support normal CMRglc for less than 10 minutes of complete substrate cutoff, providing buffering for transient hypoglycemic dips but no protection against sustained hypoglycemia.

#### Astrocyte–neuron coupling

The astrocyte–neuron lactate shuttle (ANLS) hypothesis ([Pellerin & Magistretti, 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22027938/)) proposes that synaptic glutamate uptake by astrocytes triggers astrocytic glycolysis, with lactate exported via monocarboxylate transporters MCT1/MCT4 (monocarboxylate transporters mediating lactate and ketone exchange across cell membranes) to neurons (which take it up via MCT2) and oxidize it via lactate dehydrogenase. This model couples local neural activity to glucose uptake in a way that influences the BOLD/FDG-PET signals used in functional imaging.

ANLS remains contested. Critics have argued that direct neuronal glucose uptake is sufficient to meet activity-driven energy demand, and that the magnitude of the postulated lactate flux is inconsistent with measured astrocytic GLUT1 transport capacity ([Dienel, 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28028821/)). The current consensus is that lactate exchange between astrocytes and neurons does occur and contributes to brain energy metabolism, but the quantitative importance under physiological conditions and during hypoglycemia is unresolved. For T1D specifically, monocarboxylate-mediated lactate (and ketone) supply may partially substitute for glucose during prolonged or recurrent hypoglycemia.

### Quantitative characterization

| Parameter | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Adult brain mass | ~1.4 kg (≈2% of body weight) | [Mergenthaler 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23933069/) |
| Fraction of resting whole-body glucose disposal | ~20–25% | [Mergenthaler 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23933069/) |
| CMRglc, awake adult, whole brain (avg) | ~25–30 μmol/100 g/min (~5 mg/100 g/min) | [Mergenthaler 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23933069/) |
| Total brain glucose consumption | ~110–120 g/day | [Mergenthaler 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23933069/) |
| Gray matter : white matter CMRglc ratio | ~3–4 : 1 | [Mergenthaler 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23933069/) |
| BBB unidirectional transport Kt (in vivo) | 1.5–3.5 mmol/L | [Barros 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19890463/) |
| Neuronal GLUT3 Km (cell-level) | ~1–2 mmol/L | [Simpson et al., 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17579656/) |
| Brain glycogen pool | ~1–2 g/kg brain wet weight | [Mergenthaler 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23933069/) |
| Brain : plasma glucose ratio at euglycemia | ~0.2–0.3 | [Barros 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19890463/) |
| Glycemic threshold: glucagon and epinephrine release (non-diabetic) | ~3.6–3.9 mmol/L (65–70 mg/dL) | [Sprague & Arbeláez, 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24015370/) |
| Glycemic threshold: autonomic neurogenic symptoms (non-diabetic) | ~3.2–3.6 mmol/L (58–65 mg/dL) | [Sprague & Arbeláez, 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24015370/) |
| Glycemic threshold: cognitive dysfunction onset | ~2.7–3.0 mmol/L (49–54 mg/dL) | [Sprague & Arbeláez, 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24015370/) |
| Glycemic threshold: severe neuroglycopenia (confusion, behavioural change) | ~2.0–2.7 mmol/L (36–49 mg/dL) | [Sprague & Arbeláez, 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24015370/) |
| Glycemic threshold: seizure / coma risk | < ~2.0 mmol/L (< 36 mg/dL) | [McCrimmon & Sherwin, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20723825/) |
| Brain ketone contribution after prolonged fasting (5–6 wk) | up to ~50–60% of cerebral energy (sources vary: Owen et al. 1967 jugular-catheter data ~2/3; Cahill 2006 review ~50%) | [Cahill, 2006](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16848698/) |
| Time course of meaningful brain ketone adaptation | ~3–7 days of fasting | [Cahill, 2006](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16848698/) |

Note on thresholds: the values above derive from clamp studies in non-diabetic adults. Individual thresholds vary, and the *ordered hierarchy* (counterregulation > autonomic symptoms > cognitive impairment > coma) is more robust than the absolute mmol/L values. In T1D the entire hierarchy can be displaced — see below.

### T1D-specific aspects

**Hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF).** Antecedent hypoglycemia (single events of ≥ 60 minutes' duration, recent exercise, or sleep) attenuates both the sympathoadrenal response to and the symptom perception of subsequent hypoglycemia, displacing thresholds to lower glucose concentrations ([Cryer, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24095133/); [Hirsch & Skyler, 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27288006/)). Because the glucagon response is already lost in established T1D (within ~5 years of diagnosis, due to loss of the intra-islet insulin signal that normally restrains α-cells during hypoglycemia), and the epinephrine response is blunted by HAAF, T1D patients with hypoglycemia unawareness face up to a 25-fold increased risk of severe hypoglycemia. Critically, HAAF is largely functional and reversible: 2–3 weeks of strict hypoglycemia avoidance can restore symptom thresholds in many patients.

**Brain fuel during hypoglycemia is preserved in well-controlled T1D.** Boyle et al. demonstrated using arteriovenous tracer methods that T1D patients with intensive insulin therapy and recent antecedent hypoglycemia maintain near-normal cerebral glucose uptake at plasma glucose levels of 2.5 mmol/L — levels at which non-diabetic controls show clear neuroglycopenia ([Boyle et al., 1995](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8544261/)). The mechanism is presumed upregulation of BBB GLUT1 in response to repeated hypoglycemia. This adaptation is double-edged: it preserves cerebral function during low BG (good) but blunts the symptomatic signal that should trigger treatment (HAAF mechanism, bad).

**Chronic hyperglycemia and relative hypoglycemia.** The converse adaptation occurs in chronically uncontrolled T1D: BBB GLUT1 may be downregulated, shifting the brain's effective threshold upward, so that rapid normalization of plasma glucose to objectively normal levels (e.g., 5–6 mmol/L) can produce neuroglycopenic symptoms — the clinical phenomenon of "relative hypoglycemia" sometimes reported during the first weeks after intensification of insulin therapy.

**Pediatric vulnerability and recurrent severe hypoglycemia.** The developing brain (particularly < 6 years of age) appears more vulnerable to both severe hypoglycemia and DKA than the adult brain. Imaging studies in children with T1D and recurrent severe hypoglycemia have shown reduced gray-matter volume in hippocampus and prefrontal cortex and altered white-matter integrity, with deficits in memory, attention, and processing speed and IQ reductions of ~4–6 points relative to T1D controls without severe hypoglycemia ([Languren et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24018961/)). The DCCT/EDIC long-term cohort, however, found no statistically significant decline in adult cognitive function attributable to severe hypoglycemia over ~18 years of follow-up; effects in adults appear smaller than in early childhood.

**Cerebral edema during DKA treatment.** Subclinical brain swelling is detectable in most children at DKA presentation; clinically apparent cerebral edema occurs in 0.2–1% of episodes and accounts for the majority of DKA-related mortality in pediatric T1D ([Glaser et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11172153/); [Azova et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33289956/)). The pathophysiology is now understood as primarily ischemia–reperfusion injury with vasogenic and cytotoxic components rather than the older "osmotic disequilibrium during overzealous fluid replacement" model: hypocapnia-induced cerebral vasoconstriction and dehydration reduce perfusion at presentation, and treatment-related reperfusion drives inflammatory injury and BBB disruption. Risk factors at presentation include severe acidosis, low arterial PCO₂, elevated serum urea nitrogen, and treatment with bicarbonate. Cerebral edema is rare in adults.

**"Dead-in-bed" syndrome.** Sudden unexplained nocturnal death accounts for ~6% of mortality in young T1D patients (figure derived from registry analyses with ascertainment limitations). The most plausible mechanism combines severe nocturnal hypoglycemia (often unrecognized due to HAAF and sleep), prolonged QTc, and fatal arrhythmia; impaired arousal due to repeated nocturnal hypoglycemia (potentially via orexin-A neuron adaptation) may explain why these patients fail to wake to symptoms ([Tu et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19604134/); [Tanenberg et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20223987/)). Continuous glucose monitoring with predictive low-glucose suspend has reduced — but not eliminated — nocturnal hypoglycemia exposure.

**Insulin in the brain (independent of cerebral glucose uptake).** Although bulk cerebral glucose uptake is insulin-independent, insulin does cross the BBB via a saturable transporter and acts on hypothalamic and hippocampal neurons to influence appetite, autonomic tone, and aspects of cognition. CNS insulin signalling is mechanistically distinct from peripheral insulin-driven GLUT4 translocation and does not measurably alter whole-brain CMRglc on the timescales relevant to glycemic control. CNS insulin biology is therefore a separate topic from acute glucose-supply physiology and is not detailed further here.

NB: the simulator models brain glucose consumption as a fixed insulin-independent rate (F01) per kg body weight, with linearly reduced consumption when BG drops below ~4.5 mmol/L (Hovorka 2004 formulation). It does not model: GLUT1/GLUT3 saturation kinetics explicitly, regional gray/white matter variation, the astrocyte–neuron lactate shuttle, ketone utilization during prolonged hypoglycemia or fasting, BBB GLUT1 adaptation in chronic hyper- or hypoglycemia, HAAF-related symptom-threshold shifts (HAAF is implemented elsewhere as autonomic counterregulation, not as cerebral symptom thresholds), or DKA-associated cerebral edema risk.*

# Part 2: Macronutrient handling

<a name="carbohydrates"></a>
## 5. Carbohydrates -- from mouth to blood glucose

Ingested carbohydrate cannot raise plasma glucose until it has been mechanically dispersed in the stomach, hydrolysed to monosaccharides at the brush border, transported across the enterocyte, drained through the portal vein, and survived first-pass hepatic extraction. Each step has a characteristic time scale, and the slowest step under normal physiological conditions is gastric emptying ([Horowitz et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8405758/); [Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). The shape of the postprandial blood glucose curve — the rate of rise, the peak time, the peak height, and the duration — is therefore set principally by the kinetics of gastric processing and intestinal absorption rather than by the chemistry of the carbohydrate per se. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), this physiology is unchanged in early disease but is overlaid in long-standing T1D by a substantial prevalence of disordered gastric motility and by acute glycaemic feedback on emptying rate, both of which alter the kinetic alignment between meal absorption and exogenous insulin action.

### Mechanism

#### Cephalic phase and gastric processing

Sight, smell and oral exposure to food trigger a vagally mediated cephalic-phase response that increases gastric tone, salivary α-amylase secretion, and gastrin release before the bolus arrives. Once swallowed, the meal is mixed in the gastric body and fundus and propelled distally by phasic antral contractions ("antral grinding"). The pylorus functions as a graded sieve: liquid and finely dispersed semi-solid particles pass freely, whereas solid particles are retained until they have been reduced to ~1–2 mm by mechanical and acid–enzymatic disintegration ([Kong & Singh, 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18577009/)). Liquids therefore empty in a roughly first-order pattern with a half-time of 15–25 min for water and 20–60 min for nutrient liquids, while solids show a characteristic biphasic profile — an initial lag phase (10–60 min) during which trituration occurs, followed by an approximately linear emptying phase ([Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/)).

A central physiological feature is **caloric clamping** (duodenal feedback limiting caloric delivery rate to ~1–4 kcal/min regardless of meal size): the duodenum signals back to the proximal stomach (via cholecystokinin (CCK; I-cell hormone triggering pyloric contraction and emptying delay), GLP-1 (glucagon-like peptide-1, L-cell incretin slowing gastric emptying), PYY (peptide YY, L-cell hormone amplifying satiety and emptying delay) and vagal afferents) such that the rate at which calories are delivered to the small intestine is held at a relatively constant 1–4 kcal/min across a wide range of meal sizes and compositions ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). Larger meals therefore take proportionally longer to empty, but the *rate* of nutrient delivery — and hence the maximal flux of glucose entering the absorptive surface — is bounded.

#### Luminal digestion

Salivary α-amylase initiates starch hydrolysis in the mouth and gastric body, but is rapidly inactivated below pH ~3.5; pancreatic α-amylase, secreted into the duodenum, is the principal endoluminal starch-hydrolysing enzyme. α-Amylase cleaves α-1,4-glycosidic bonds randomly within amylose and amylopectin chains, generating maltose, maltotriose, and α-limit dextrins. Final hydrolysis to free monosaccharide occurs at the apical brush-border by the membrane-anchored disaccharidases **maltase-glucoamylase** (MGAM, encoded by *MGAM*), **sucrase-isomaltase** (SI), and **lactase-phlorizin hydrolase** (LCT). These enzymes liberate glucose, fructose, and galactose at or just within the unstirred water layer overlying the enterocyte apical membrane, positioning the monosaccharide products for immediate transporter capture.

#### Apical and basolateral transport in the enterocyte

Three transporters carry the bulk of dietary monosaccharide flux across the enterocyte ([Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/); [Roder et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24587098/)):

- **SGLT1** (SLC5A1; apical) is a Na⁺-coupled secondary-active symporter with 2 Na⁺ : 1 glucose stoichiometry, transporting both glucose and galactose. Reported apparent K_m values for glucose are ~0.5 mmol/L at fully active membrane potential and Na⁺ gradient ([Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/)) but rise toward ~1.8 mmol/L under enterocyte-realistic intracellular glucose concentrations in computational reconstructions ([Afshar et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34899386/)). Functional saturation occurs at luminal glucose concentrations around 30 mmol/L. Genetic loss of SGLT1 (glucose–galactose malabsorption) produces near-complete failure of intestinal glucose absorption — a ~73% reduction in plasma tracer appearance after an enteral load in murine knockouts and severe osmotic diarrhoea in humans ([Gorboulev et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22124465/)) — which establishes SGLT1 as the obligate quantitative pathway for dietary glucose under physiological conditions in humans.

- **GLUT5** (SLC2A5; apical) is a Na⁺-independent facilitative carrier specific for fructose, with K_m ≈ 6 mmol/L for human GLUT5 ([Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/)). It does not transport glucose or galactose.

- **GLUT2** (SLC2A2; basolateral) is a low-affinity, high-capacity facilitative carrier (K_m ≈ 17 mmol/L for glucose, 76 mmol/L for fructose, 92 mmol/L for galactose; [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/)) that mediates monosaccharide efflux from the enterocyte to the portal blood. Whether GLUT2 is also recruited to the apical membrane during high-carbohydrate meals — and provides up to ~3-fold additional absorptive capacity under those conditions — has been proposed in rodent models ([Kellett & Helliwell, 2000](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10677310/); [Kellett & Brot-Laroche, 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16186394/)) and disputed in others ([Gorboulev et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22124465/); apical GLUT2 was not detected by immunofluorescence in murine knockout-controlled studies). The clinical relevance in humans is unresolved — the persistence of severe diarrhoea in SGLT1-deficient patients argues against a major apical-GLUT2 bypass route in vivo (controversy summarised by [Roder et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24587098/)).

#### Splanchnic disposition

Monosaccharide absorbed from the small intestine drains directly via mesenteric and portal veins to the liver. In healthy adults, **first-pass splanchnic glucose extraction** (portal-bed and hepatic removal of absorbed glucose before it reaches systemic circulation) removes 25–40% of the ingested load before it reaches the systemic circulation, the bulk of which is hepatic uptake driven by the high portal-vein glucose and insulin concentrations ([Ludvik et al., 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9410915/) — measured 37.8 ± 5.9% splanchnic extraction with hepatic vein catheterisation after a 75 g OGTT in normoglycaemic controls; [Ferrannini et al., 1985](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3893478/)). Glucose taken up by the liver is incorporated into glycogen or oxidised, suppressing endogenous glucose production via direct hepatic insulin signalling and the portal–peripheral insulin gradient (see §2). Fructose is almost entirely cleared in the first hepatic pass — hepatocyte fructokinase (KHK) phosphorylates fructose to fructose-1-phosphate without insulin involvement, yielding ~20% conversion to glucose, ~25% to glycogen and the remainder to lactate, with little fructose reaching peripheral plasma after typical doses. Galactose, similarly, is converted to UDP-galactose and UDP-glucose by the hepatic Leloir pathway and contributes minimally to systemic glycaemia.

#### Gastric emptying as the principal kinetic determinant

Because intestinal absorptive capacity (SGLT1 + GLUT2 + GLUT5 in the proximal small intestine) substantially exceeds the rate at which the stomach normally delivers nutrient to the duodenum, **gastric emptying is the rate-limiting step** for postprandial glucose appearance under most physiological conditions ([Horowitz et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8405758/); [Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). Five meal properties account for most of the inter-meal variability in glycaemic response:

1. **Liquid versus solid form.** Liquids bypass the antral grinding and pyloric sieve mechanisms and empty in a roughly first-order pattern. A 75 g oral glucose drink (the standard OGTT; oral glucose tolerance test, 75 g oral glucose challenge) produces peak plasma glucose at 30–45 min in healthy adults; the same carbohydrate content in solid form (e.g., a starchy meal of equivalent mass) typically peaks at 60–90 min ([Horowitz et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8405758/)).

2. **Caloric and volumetric load.** Within the caloric-clamp regime, half-emptying time increases by approximately 18 ± 6 min per additional 100 kcal across physiological meal sizes ([Marciani et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19779010/)). Meal volume also slows emptying through gastric distension and accommodation reflexes that are partially independent of caloric content.

3. **Macronutrient composition — fat in particular.** Fat is the most potent single-meal modulator of gastric emptying. Long-chain fatty acids in the duodenum trigger CCK release from I-cells, GLP-1 from L-cells, and PYY from L-cells; these hormones slow antral motility and stimulate tonic pyloric contraction, prolonging gastric retention ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). The mechanism is developed in §6.

4. **Fibre as a kinetic modulator.** Dietary fibre is not absorbed as glucose but it is far from inert kinetically. Two distinct mechanisms operate concurrently and on different time scales:

    - *Soluble viscous fibre* (β-glucan in oats and barley, pectin in fruit, psyllium): the dissolved polysaccharide forms a viscous gel that increases bulk gastric content viscosity, impairs antral grinding and pyloric flow, and — once in the duodenum and jejunum — thickens the unstirred water layer overlying the brush border. The unstirred-layer effect retards diffusion of liberated monosaccharide to SGLT1, and is now considered to account for the larger fraction of viscous fibre's glycaemic benefit ([Würsch & Pi-Sunyer, 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9353622/)). The dose-response is well characterised for oat β-glucan: 2.5–3.5 g β-glucan per 30 g available carbohydrate reduces incremental postprandial glucose AUC (iAUC) by ~17% versus a matched low-fibre control; 4.5–5.5 g per 30 g — the threshold for the EFSA postprandial glucose health claim — reduces iAUC by ~31%; effect saturates above ~6–8 g per meal ([Würsch & Pi-Sunyer, 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9353622/); values from a single review-and-meta-analysis tradition; individual trial scatter is wide).

    - *Insoluble structural fibre* (cellulose, hemicelluloses, lignin in wheat bran, vegetable matrices): does not form a gel but adds bulk, prolongs the antral grinding phase, and traps starch granules within an intact cell-wall matrix that resists amylase access ("food-matrix effect"; [Liljeberg & Björck, 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8942411/)). The food-matrix effect explains why intact whole-grain rye bread produces a flatter postprandial glucose curve than white bread of the same starch content, despite similar chemical starch composition.

    Removing the natural fibre matrix from a starchy food (e.g., grinding whole grain into refined flour) accelerates both gastric emptying and intestinal digestion, raising both peak glucose and iAUC ([Frost et al., 2003](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12571662/)). Fibre is therefore quantitatively important to the glycaemic response and, on the EU/Nordic nutrition-label convention, is listed as a separate quantity from "carbohydrate" (which on those labels denotes digestible sugar + starch); the US/FDA convention bundles fibre into "total carbohydrate". The reporting difference matters for BG-modelling exercises and clinical carbohydrate counting but does not alter the underlying physiology.

5. **Glycaemic index (GI) is mechanistically a composite of these factors.** GI ranks foods by the iAUC of plasma glucose over 2 h after ingestion of 50 g available carbohydrate, normalised to glucose or white bread ([Jenkins et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6259925/); [Atkinson et al., 2008](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2584181/)). Mechanistically, GI integrates gastric-emptying rate, food-matrix integrity, viscous-fibre content, fat content, and starch crystallinity into a single number, which limits its predictive precision: between-laboratory and between-subject variability in GI for the same food can exceed ~20% ([Wolever, 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18175767/)).

Typical postprandial peak times for healthy adults are summarised below; intersubject variation is approximately 2–3-fold and within-subject day-to-day variation is also substantial.

| Food example | Carb form | Typical peak BG | Mechanistic basis |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cola, juice, sports drink | Liquid simple sugar | ~25–35 min | Bypass antral grinding + pyloric sieve |
| Glucose tablets, honey | Solid simple sugar | ~30–45 min | Solid-form delay; rapid disaccharidase hydrolysis |
| White bread, pasta | Complex carb, low fibre | ~45–75 min | Starch hydrolysis adds time; minimal matrix |
| Whole-grain rye, oats | Complex carb, high fibre | ~75–120 min | Matrix retards grinding + brush-border access |
| Pizza, lasagne | Complex carb + high fat | ~90–180 min, often biphasic | CCK/GLP-1/PYY-mediated emptying delay (§6) |

| Pyloric sieve solid-particle threshold | 1–2 mm | Established by gastric-aspirate sieving and disintegration studies | [Kong & Singh, 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18577009/) |

| Gastric emptying t½ — nutrient liquid | ~20–60 min | Caloric-clamp dependent | [Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/); [Marciani et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19779010/) |
| Gastric emptying lag phase — solid mixed meal | 10–60 min | Followed by approximately linear emptying | [Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/) |
| Caloric-load slowing | ~+18 ± 6 min per +100 kcal | MRI half-emptying time, modular liquid meal | [Marciani et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19779010/) |
