| SGLT1 saturation luminal glucose | ~30 mmol/L | Beyond which additional uptake mechanisms have been proposed but remain disputed in humans | [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/) |
| GLUT5 K_m (fructose) | ~6 mmol/L | Human isoform | [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/) |
| GLUT2 K_m (glucose; basolateral) | ~17 mmol/L | Low-affinity, high-capacity efflux | [Koepsell, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32829466/) |
| First-pass splanchnic glucose extraction (75 g OGTT, healthy) | ~25–40% (one HVC study: 37.8 ± 5.9%) | Hepatic vein catheterisation; small samples; method-dependent estimates | [Ludvik et al., 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9410915/); [Ferrannini et al., 1985](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3893478/) |
| Plasma glucose peak time, 75 g OGTT, healthy | ~30–45 min | Substantial inter-individual variation | [Horowitz et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8405758/) |
| Plasma glucose peak time, mixed solid meal, healthy | ~60–90 min | Composition-dependent | [Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/) |
| Bioavailability of digestible CHO (sugar + starch) | ~95–100% to portal blood | Excludes resistant starch (typically 1–10% of total starch depending on preparation) | [Englyst & Cummings, 1986](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3789750/) |
| Oat β-glucan dose for ~30% iAUC reduction | 4.5–5.5 g per 30 g available carbohydrate | EFSA postprandial-glucose health-claim threshold; effect saturates above ~6–8 g | [Würsch & Pi-Sunyer, 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9353622/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Loss of incretin-amplified meal insulin without loss of incretin secretion.** β-cell loss in T1D abolishes the GIP- and GLP-1-amplified postprandial insulin response that contributes ~50–70% of the total insulin response to oral glucose in healthy individuals (the "incretin effect"). However, GIP and GLP-1 secretion from K- and L-cells in response to luminal nutrients is preserved, and these gut hormones continue to slow gastric emptying via vagal and direct smooth-muscle pathways. The kinetic consequence is that meal absorption in T1D follows similar gastric-emptying kinetics to healthy individuals — but without the matched insulin pulse. The pharmacokinetic mismatch between subcutaneous rapid-acting analogues (peak action 60–90 min, duration 3–5 h) and gastric emptying of typical mixed meals (peak glucose appearance 60–180 min, with biphasic high-fat meals extending well beyond) is the kinetic origin of postprandial hyperglycaemia followed by late hypoglycaemia in T1D ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)).

2. **Diabetic gastroparesis — a late complication producing chronic, irreversible gastric motility disorder.**

    **Prevalence and epidemiology.** Gastroparesis is a well-established late complication of T1D. Symptomatic gastroparesis confirmed by scintigraphy occurs in ~5–8% of T1D patients ([Choung et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22343744/) — Olmsted County 10-year cumulative incidence 5.2% in T1D; [Ye et al., 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37640738/) — pooled prevalence 8.3%, 95% CI 6.4–10.2%, in T1D meta-analysis). However, subclinical delayed emptying — detectable on objective testing but without classic symptoms (nausea, early satiety, bloating) — is present in 27–65% of long-standing T1D patients when rigorously tested ([Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/); [Krishnasamy & Abell, 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6028327/)). The wide prevalence range reflects differences in ascertainment criteria (symptom-based vs. objective-test-based), referral bias (tertiary centres report 30–50%, population studies report 5–8%), and cohort selection (longer diabetes duration and poorer glycaemic control elevate prevalence). Incidence is approximately 4× higher in T1D than T2D, and ~4× higher in women than men ([Choung et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22343744/); population-based incidence: 9.8/100,000 person-years in women vs. 2.4/100,000 in men).

    **Pathophysiology.** The pathogenesis is multifactorial and involves progressive damage to multiple components of the gastric neuromuscular apparatus:
    - **Vagal autonomic neuropathy** — chronic hyperglycaemia damages the vagus nerve (via sorbitol accumulation, oxidative stress, and advanced glycation end-products), impairing parasympathetic-mediated gastric motility. Gastroparesis in T1D correlates with cardiac autonomic neuropathy and other manifestations of diabetic autonomic neuropathy (DAN).
    - **Depletion of interstitial cells of Cajal (ICC)** — ICC are the pacemaker cells that generate the gastric slow wave (3 cycles/min). Chronic hyperglycaemia reduces ICC density in the gastric body and antrum, disrupting the coordinated peristaltic pattern required for antral grinding and pyloric transit.
    - **Loss of neuronal nitric oxide synthase (nNOS)** — inhibitory nitrergic neurons in the myenteric plexus mediate pyloric relaxation and adaptive gastric accommodation. nNOS loss produces pylorospasm and impaired fundic relaxation, both of which slow emptying.
    - **Oxidative and inflammatory damage to enteric neurons** — hyperglycaemia-induced reactive oxygen species (ROS) and macrophage infiltration contribute to enteric neuronal apoptosis and smooth-muscle fibrosis ([Bharucha et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22070278/); [Camilleri et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30385743/)).

    **Risk factors.** The DCCT-EDIC cohort and registry studies identify the following quantitative risk factors:
    - **Diabetes duration ≥10 years** — gastroparesis is rare before 10 years of T1D; median duration at diagnosis is ~23–32 years in registry data.
    - **HbA1c** — higher HbA1c is an independent predictor of delayed emptying in logistic regression models, even after controlling for neuropathy and duration ([Krishnasamy & Abell, 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6028327/)).
    - **Coexisting microvascular complications** — gastroparesis correlates particularly with severe retinopathy and, to a lesser extent, with nephropathy and cardiovascular autonomic dysfunction.
    - **Female sex** — 4:1 female-to-male ratio in population studies.

    **Severity classification.** The American Neurogastroenterology and Motility Society/Society of Nuclear Medicine consensus uses gastric retention at 4 hours after a standardised egg-white meal ([Abell et al., 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18287197/)):
    - *Normal:* <10% retention at 4 h (t½ typically <120 min)
    - *Mild gastroparesis:* 10–15% retention at 4 h (t½ ~120–180 min)
    - *Moderate gastroparesis:* 16–35% retention at 4 h (t½ ~180–300 min)
    - *Severe gastroparesis:* >35% retention at 4 h (t½ >300 min; "grossly abnormal")

    For context, normal solid-meal t½ in healthy adults is 109–128 min (population mean; see variability section below). Mild gastroparesis roughly doubles this, while severe gastroparesis can extend t½ to >5 hours.

    **The insulin–meal mismatch problem.** Gastroparesis fundamentally disrupts the temporal alignment between subcutaneous insulin action and glucose appearance. Exogenous rapid-acting insulin analogue pharmacokinetics are unaffected by gastric motility (insulin is injected subcutaneously, not ingested orally), so peak insulin action still occurs 60–90 min post-injection. However, gastroparesis delays peak glucose appearance to 3–5+ hours. The consequence is a characteristic biphasic glycaemic pattern: early postprandial hypoglycaemia (insulin peaks before glucose arrives) followed by late postprandial hyperglycaemia (glucose arrives after insulin has waned). This mismatch is the kinetic basis for the clinical observation that gastroparesis paradoxically increases severe hypoglycaemia risk despite higher HbA1c levels ([Krishnasamy & Abell, 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6028327/)). Management strategies include post-meal rather than pre-meal bolusing, dose-splitting (partial bolus at meal, remainder 1–2 h later), and in some centres, use of sensor-augmented pumps with predictive low-glucose suspend.

    **Rapid gastric emptying — the opposite end of the spectrum.** In early T1D (or in T2D with vagal dysfunction), impaired nitrergic gastric accommodation can produce paradoxically *rapid* liquid-phase emptying: the proximal stomach fails to relax and accommodate the meal, generating higher intragastric pressure that drives rapid pyloric transit of liquids ([Krishnasamy & Abell, 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6028327/)). This produces unexpectedly early and sharp postprandial glucose spikes, contributing to glycaemic variability from the opposite direction.

    **Reversibility.** The prognosis for diabetic gastroparesis is guarded. Symptom severity is typically stable over 12–25 years even with improved glycaemic control ([Dudekula et al., 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7428659/)). Partial improvement in gastric function has been observed after combined pancreas–kidney transplantation (which normalises glycaemia and removes uraemic neuropathy), suggesting that some neuronal recovery is possible if the hyperglycaemic insult is completely removed ([Krishnasamy & Abell, 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6028327/)). However, ICC depletion and smooth-muscle fibrosis are largely irreversible, and prokinetic medications (metoclopramide, domperidone, erythromycin) provide symptomatic relief but have limited long-term efficacy and significant side-effect profiles. The practical implication is that gastroparesis, once established, must be treated as a permanent alteration in gastric-emptying kinetics rather than a reversible modulator.

    **Modelling considerations.** In a compartment model, gastroparesis can be represented as a sustained increase in τ_G to the 180–400 min range (vs. normal ~40–120 min), combined with a substantial increase in meal-to-meal stochastic variability (intra-individual COV rising from ~12% to 20–28%; see variability section below). The erratic nature of gastroparetic emptying — where the same meal can empty in 2 hours one day and 6 hours the next — may also justify adding a stochastic component (e.g., occasional "gastric stalling" events where τ_G transiently exceeds 400 min) rather than simply using a higher fixed τ_G. The lag-then-linear emptying pattern is more pronounced in gastroparesis than in normal emptying, which the Hovorka two-compartment model does not capture; the Dalla Man three-compartment nonlinear model is better suited for severe gastroparesis modelling.

3. **Acute hyperglycaemia modulates gastric emptying — a feedback loop relevant to hyperglycaemic episodes.** The relationship between acute glycaemia and gastric emptying in T1D is well-documented in direction but variable in magnitude and direction depending on disease stage. Phillips et al. 2015 estimate that gastric emptying accounts for ~35% of the variance in peak postprandial blood glucose, both in healthy individuals and in those with diabetes ([Phillips et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25421372/) — based on abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist).

    **Slowing during acute hyperglycaemia — well-established at the group level.** The canonical demonstration is Schvarcz et al. 1997, who used an insulin-glucose clamp to compare gastric emptying at 4 mmol/L versus 8 mmol/L in 8 healthy subjects and 9 IDDM patients without GI symptoms or neuropathy. Liquid-phase t½ rose from 32.2 → 57.0 min in healthy (+77%, P = 0.002) and from 29.1 → 41.3 min in IDDM (+42%, P = 0.002), with parallel — but smaller — effects on solid-meal retention at 100 min (healthy: 36.7% → 55.2%; IDDM: 35.7% → 44.2%; both P = 0.004) ([Schvarcz et al., 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9207262/) — numbers from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). The IDDM effect is approximately half the magnitude of the healthy effect at the same clamped glucose, suggesting that pre-existing autonomic alterations in T1D blunt — rather than amplify — the acute feedback. For severe hyperglycaemia (16–20 mmol/L), the systematic review by Halland & Bharucha 2016 pooled two T1D studies (n = 18 total) and reported t½ prolongation of 17–31 min versus euglycaemia ([Halland & Bharucha, 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4912904/)), broadly consistent with Schvarcz at the lower glucose range but not establishing a clear dose-response across studies. Plateau or ceiling effects above ~15 mmol/L have not been systematically characterised in humans.

    **Acceleration in early disease and adolescent T1D — paradoxical but reproducible.** A separate, often overlooked literature documents *faster*-than-control gastric emptying in early-stage and adolescent T1D, independent of current glycaemia. Perano et al. 2015 found gastric half-emptying of a ¹³C-labelled solid meal of 77.6 min (IQR 61.4–99.3) in 30 adolescents with T1D versus 109.1 min (IQR 70.8–124.2) in 15 matched controls (P = 0.02), with acceleration independent of HbA1c, diabetes duration, BMI, and study-day glycaemia ([Perano et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4428870/)). Horowitz et al. 1991 had previously observed in a 87-patient diabetic cohort (67 T1D, 20 T2D) versus 25 healthy controls that asymptomatic diabetics without orthostatic hypotension had solid-meal t½ of 70.1 ± 41.6 min — faster than the typical 90–120 min healthy adult range ([Horowitz et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2070801/)). A mechanistic candidate is offered by Kishi et al. 2019, who showed that streptozotocin-diabetic mice in the early disease phase (2–4 weeks) develop expansion of antral interstitial cells of Cajal (ICC density 60.9% vs 46.2%), with parallel acceleration of gastric Tmax (~48 vs ~110 min) ([Kishi et al., 2019](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6785066/) — mouse model; mechanism not directly verified in human T1D). The clinically consistent picture is biphasic over the natural history of T1D: early ICC expansion (possible acceleration) → late ICC depletion + autonomic neuropathy → frank gastroparesis (developed in point 2).

    **HbA1c is a poor cross-sectional predictor.** Four of five studies reviewed by Halland & Bharucha 2016 found no significant correlation between current HbA1c and gastric emptying rate, although the EDIC sub-study (n = 78 T1D followed 27 years) reported that *cumulative* glycaemic exposure was independently associated with delayed emptying — implicating long-term rather than current hyperglycaemia. The authors conclude that "there is no evidence that improved control of glycemia improves gastric emptying or vice versa."

    **Mechanism.** Acute slowing involves vagal and nitrergic-pathway feedback from duodenal nutrient and glucose sensors, with sympathetic involvement at higher glucose levels ([Phillips et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25421372/)); the signalling cascade is incompletely mapped. Chronic delay in long-standing T1D involves ICC loss and immune-cell infiltration of the gastric wall (see point 2). Acceleration in early disease has been proposed to involve ICC hyperplasia (Kishi et al. 2019) but is not yet mechanistically established in humans.

    **Clinical consequence and evidence-quality caveats.** During an acute hyperglycaemic excursion, the slowing component creates a self-prolonging loop: an under-bolused meal raises plasma glucose, the elevated glucose then slows gastric emptying of the remaining meal, and the appearance curve broadens, frustrating correction-bolus titration. However, the underlying evidence base is thinner than the textbook narrative implies: the Schvarcz study, while methodologically strong, is a single small (n = 9) IDDM cohort with neuropathy explicitly excluded; the severe-hyperglycaemia evidence rests on two pooled studies of 18 patients total; and the paradoxical acceleration literature suggests that the textbook "T1D = slow emptying" generalisation does not hold uniformly across disease stages. Inter-individual variability is large in every study reviewed.

4. **Counterregulatory acceleration.** Conversely, sympathoadrenal activation during exercise or psychological stress accelerates gastric emptying via β-adrenergic and direct neural pathways. In T1D this can shorten the gastric-emptying lag of a pre-exercise snack and shift the balance between glucose appearance and exercise-induced glucose disposal — a kinetic factor that interacts with the exercise mechanisms developed in §8.

5. **Carbohydrate counting and fibre.** Clinical insulin dosing typically uses a digestible-carbohydrate count (sugar + starch). On EU/Nordic food labels this is the displayed "carbohydrate" value; on US labels the displayed "total carbohydrate" includes fibre and must be reduced by the fibre content for an equivalent count. Because fibre slows but does not abolish glucose appearance, typical clinical practice ignores fibre below thresholds of ~5 g/meal and adjusts insulin timing (longer pre-bolus interval) for high-fibre, low-GI meals rather than reducing the dose.

### Physiological modulators of gastric emptying and intestinal absorption

The rate at which ingested carbohydrates appear in the bloodstream depends on two sequential processes, each independently modulated by physiological state:

1. **Gastric emptying** — the rate of chyme delivery from the stomach to the duodenum (τG in compartment models). This is the rate-limiting step for most solid meals.
2. **Intestinal absorption** — the rate of glucose transport across the intestinal epithelium into the portal circulation. This depends on epithelial transporter capacity (SGLT1/GLUT2) and critically on splanchnic blood flow, which removes absorbed glucose from the basolateral side of the enterocyte and maintains the concentration gradient that drives further uptake.

These two mechanisms are physiologically distinct: gastric emptying is governed primarily by vagal/sympathetic tone, pyloric feedback, and gastric distension, while intestinal absorption rate is dominated by mesenteric perfusion and enterocyte transporter density. A modulator can affect one without the other (e.g., hyperglycaemia slows gastric emptying via vagal feedback but does not directly reduce splanchnic flow), or both simultaneously (e.g., intense exercise increases sympathetic tone *and* redistributes blood flow away from the splanchnic bed).

#### Gastric emptying modulators

The gastric-emptying rate is not an intrinsic constant of a given meal but is dynamically modulated by the body's physiological state. This modulation is clinically significant because it changes the timing and shape of the postprandial glucose appearance curve, independent of what or how much was eaten. The principal modulators are:

**1. Autonomic stress (sympathoadrenal activation).** Acute psychological or physical stress activates the sympathetic nervous system, which has opposing effects on different GI segments. Sympathetic activation inhibits gastric motility (via splanchnic noradrenergic neurons and circulating catecholamines acting on β-adrenoceptors on smooth muscle), slowing antral contractions and increasing pyloric tone ([Mönnikes et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11283195/)). Acute laboratory stress (cold pressor, dichotomous listening) delays solid-meal gastric emptying by 20–40% in healthy subjects ([Thompson et al., 1983](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6411484/)). However, strenuous exercise-associated sympathoadrenal activation may accelerate liquid-phase emptying in some protocols, likely via β-adrenergic effects on proximal gastric relaxation (§5 point 4). The net effect depends on stress type, intensity, and whether the dominant phase is solid or liquid.

**2. Exercise intensity.** Moderate exercise (<60% VO2max) has little effect on gastric emptying. Higher intensities (>70% VO2max) progressively delay solid-meal emptying, with reductions of 20–50% at near-maximal effort ([Leiper et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11310927/)). The mechanism involves redistribution of splanchnic blood flow to exercising muscle (splanchnic flow decreases ~60–70% at heavy exercise) and increased sympathetic tone. Post-exercise, there may be a transient acceleration as splanchnic flow normalises. The practical consequence for T1D is that a pre-exercise snack may empty more slowly than expected, altering the timing of glucose appearance relative to exercise-induced glucose disposal.

**3. Sleep and circadian rhythm.** Gastric emptying follows a circadian pattern, with significantly slower emptying during nocturnal sleep and the early morning hours compared with daytime. Goo et al. (1987) demonstrated that gastric emptying of a solid meal was ~35–50% slower when eaten at 20:00 vs 08:00 in healthy subjects ([Goo et al., 1987](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3692672/)). This has direct relevance for evening snacks and nocturnal BG management in T1D: carbohydrates eaten before sleep produce a delayed, prolonged glucose appearance curve that may extend into the dawn-phenomenon window.

**4. Fasting state (migrating motor complex).** During prolonged fasting (>4–6 h without food), the stomach enters the interdigestive migrating motor complex (MMC) cycle — periodic high-amplitude contractions that sweep residual contents distally. When a meal is eaten after prolonged fasting, the initial emptying phase may be faster than after a meal eaten on top of a partially full stomach, because the MMC has "primed" the antro-pyloro-duodenal segment. The effect is modest and transient (first 15–30 min) and primarily relevant for liquid-phase emptying.

**5. Acute hyperglycaemia (developed in §5 T1D-specific point 3).** At the group level, plasma glucose clamped at 8 mmol/L prolongs liquid-meal t½ by ~77% in healthy subjects and ~42% in T1D versus 4 mmol/L (Schvarcz et al. 1997). Severe hyperglycaemia (16–20 mmol/L) adds a further 17–31 min t½ delay in T1D (Halland & Bharucha 2016 review). The effect direction is reversed in early-stage and adolescent T1D, where multiple studies report *accelerated* emptying independent of current glycaemia (Perano et al. 2015; Horowitz et al. 1991 asymptomatic subgroup) — see point 3 for the biphasic-over-disease-course interpretation. Plateau effects above ~15 mmol/L have not been systematically tested.

**6. Chronic stress (CRH-mediated).** Distinct from acute sympathoadrenal activation (modulator 1), chronic psychological stress operates through the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis. The hypothalamus releases corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH; master regulator of the stress axis, also acts directly on enteric neurons), which signals via two receptor subtypes with opposing gastrointestinal effects: CRH-R1 (CRH receptor type 1, primarily expressed in colonic myenteric neurons) stimulates colonic motility, while CRH-R2 (CRH receptor type 2, expressed in gastric smooth muscle) inhibits gastric emptying ([Taché et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11208537/)). Chronically elevated CRH — from sustained psychological stress, sleep deprivation, or anxiety — produces tonic delay of gastric emptying via CRH-R2 activation, contributing to the functional dyspepsia phenotype commonly observed in chronically stressed individuals ([Mönnikes et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11283195/)). The magnitude is difficult to quantify in isolation because chronic stress typically co-occurs with sleep disruption and altered eating patterns; available data suggest a 10–30% increase in solid-meal t½ in subjects with high trait anxiety versus low-anxiety controls, though this has not been isolated from confounders in controlled studies. The time scale is hours to days (cortisol half-life ~60–90 min, but HPA-axis upregulation persists with ongoing stressor).

**7. Protein content.** Dietary protein slows gastric emptying via two mechanisms: first, protein hydrolysates in the duodenum stimulate CCK release from I-cells, which increases pyloric tone and inhibits antral contractions; second, the caloric clamp (modulator of total caloric delivery rate to duodenum) applies to protein calories equally with carbohydrate and fat calories, meaning a high-protein meal extends total emptying time proportionally. Whey protein is particularly potent: a 55 g whey preload increased solid-meal t½ from 39 ± 6 min to 87 ± 5 min (+123%) in healthy subjects, with parallel increases in CCK and GLP-1 ([Stanstrup et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24467269/)). The effect is protein-type-dependent: whey (fast-digesting, strong CCK stimulant) delays more than casein (slow-digesting, weaker CCK response), which delays more than plant protein. A practical consequence for T1D carbohydrate counting is that a high-protein meal (e.g., a large steak with a side of rice) produces a slower, later glucose peak than the same carbohydrate eaten alone — requiring adjusted bolus timing rather than dose change.

**8. Alcohol (ethanol).** Ethanol inhibits gastric emptying at concentrations ≥4% v/v, with the effect being present but not strictly dose-dependent across the 4–40% range ([Franke et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15699055/)). At 6 units (~48 g ethanol), solid-meal t½ increased significantly compared with control, while 3 units produced minimal delay ([Jian et al., 1986](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3709325/)). The mechanism involves direct ethanol toxicity on antral smooth muscle and suppression of phase III MMC activity. The effect is compounded by the caloric load of ethanol (7 kcal/g) competing for the duodenal caloric clamp. Notably, the beverage matrix modifies the response: beer and white wine accelerate liquid-phase emptying compared with equivalent ethanol concentrations in water, likely via carbonation and low-molecular-weight congeners stimulating gastrin ([Franke et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15699055/)). For T1D, the alcohol-induced emptying delay compounds the already complex interaction of ethanol with hepatic gluconeogenesis (see §19): delayed carbohydrate appearance overlapping with ethanol-suppressed hepatic glucose output creates a high-risk window for hypoglycaemia 4–12 hours after a meal with alcohol.

**9. Meal osmolality.** Hypertonic luminal contents in the duodenum slow gastric emptying via osmoreceptor-mediated vagal feedback. Solutions at ~600 mOsm/kg empty at roughly half the rate of isotonic solutions (~280 mOsm/kg) under matched pressure conditions ([Hunt & Pathak, 1960](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13835866/)). The effect is clinically relevant for high-sugar beverages (a 500 mL cola at ~550 mOsm/kg empties slower per unit volume than water, despite being liquid) and for oral rehydration solutions. For T1D, the implication is that glucose-rich drinks, despite being liquid, do not necessarily produce the rapid glucose spike expected from their liquid form — the osmolality-dependent feedback partially compensates, flattening the absorption curve relative to an equivalent glucose dose in isotonic solution.

**10. GLP-1 receptor agonist medications.** GLP-1 receptor agonists (GLP-1 RAs; semaglutide, liraglutide, exenatide, and others) are increasingly used adjunctively in T1D for weight and glycaemic management, though they are not formally indicated for T1D in most jurisdictions. These agents produce clinically significant gastric-emptying delay: a meta-analysis of five scintigraphic studies (n=247) found a pooled mean increase in solid-meal t½ of ~36 min (95% CI 17–55; GLP-1 RA group t½ 138 ± 64 min vs placebo 95 ± 40 min; [Amin et al., 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11150091/)). The delay is pharmacologically mediated: GLP-1 binding to GLP-1R on vagal afferents and gastric smooth muscle inhibits antral contractions and increases pyloric tone, mimicking but exceeding the physiological incretin response. Short-acting agents (exenatide twice daily, lixisenatide) produce more pronounced acute delay but with intermittent dosing; long-acting agents (semaglutide, dulaglutide) produce sustained, moderate delay. A degree of tachyphylaxis (attenuation of effect with chronic exposure) develops with long-acting agents: liraglutide produced persistent gastroparesis-range delay in ~30% of subjects at 16 weeks, with normalisation in the remainder ([Halawi et al., 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10841973/)). The clinical consequence for T1D patients on GLP-1 RAs is a fundamental shift in meal–insulin timing: the standard pre-meal bolus timing becomes systematically too early, and dose-splitting strategies may be required.

#### Quantitative summary of modulators

| Modulator | Direction | Magnitude (change in t½) | Time scale | Key reference |
|-----------|-----------|--------------------------|------------|---------------|
| Acute stress (sympathetic) | Slower | +20–40% | Minutes–hours | [Thompson et al., 1983](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6411484/) |
| Chronic stress (CRH/HPA) | Slower | +10–30% (estimated) | Hours–days | [Taché et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11208537/) |
| Intense exercise (>70% VO2max) | Slower | +20–50% | During exercise | [Leiper et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11310927/) |
| Sleep / nocturnal | Slower | +35–50% | Hours (circadian) | [Goo et al., 1987](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3692672/) |
| Prolonged fasting (MMC active) | Faster | −10–20% (initial phase) | First 15–30 min | [Deloose et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22407798/) |
| Hyperglycaemia (>8 mmol/L) | Slower | +30–75% | Hours | [Schvarcz et al., 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9207262/) |
| High protein (>40 g co-ingested) | Slower | +30–120% (protein-type-dependent) | Per meal | [Stanstrup et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24467269/) |
| Alcohol (≥4% v/v, ≥6 units) | Slower | +15–40% | Hours | [Franke et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15699055/) |
| Hypertonic meal (>500 mOsm/kg) | Slower | +30–50% | Per meal | [Hunt & Pathak, 1960](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13835866/) |
| GLP-1 RA medication | Slower | +36 min pooled mean (≈+38%) | Persistent (medication) | [Amin et al., 2024](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11150091/) |

> **Research notes — areas requiring further investigation for modelling:**
> - Interaction between multiple simultaneous modulators (e.g., exercise + stress + hyperglycaemia) — are effects additive, multiplicative, or ceiling-limited? Preliminary evidence suggests that modulators acting via different mechanisms (sympathetic vs. hormonal vs. osmolarity-mediated) are approximately multiplicative when each is moderate, but a ceiling exists because the pylorus cannot close more than fully closed and antral contractions cannot slow below zero.
> - Whether the circadian pattern is driven by sleep per se or by the circadian clock (suprachiasmatic nucleus) — relevant for shift workers and irregular sleep. Available data cannot separate the two: Goo et al. (1987) confounded time-of-day with sleep proximity.
> - Quantitative dose-response for exercise intensity vs. gastric emptying delay (currently only bracket data at <60% and >70% VO2max).
> - Recovery kinetics after exercise ends — how quickly does gastric emptying normalise?
> - GLP-1 RA tachyphylaxis kinetics: only partial data exist for liraglutide at 16 weeks; semaglutide tachyphylaxis time course is not well characterised.
> - Protein-type-specific dose-response curves (whey vs. casein vs. plant protein) with matched caloric loads are sparse; most studies compare only two protein types at a single dose.

#### Intestinal absorption rate modulators

Even after chyme has passed the pylorus, the rate at which glucose crosses the intestinal epithelium and enters the portal circulation is not fixed. The dominant modulator is splanchnic blood flow, which determines how quickly absorbed glucose is cleared from the basolateral side of enterocytes, maintaining the transmembrane concentration gradient that drives SGLT1 and GLUT2 transport.

**1. Exercise-induced splanchnic hypoperfusion.** During exercise, cardiac output is redistributed from the splanchnic vascular bed to working skeletal muscle. At rest, splanchnic organs receive ~25–30% of cardiac output; at 70% VO2max this falls to ~10%, representing a ~60–70% reduction in absolute mesenteric flow ([Qamar & Read, 1987](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3678950/)). This reduction directly impairs intestinal glucose absorption: glucose accumulates in enterocytes when basolateral clearance is insufficient, slowing apical uptake via SGLT1/GLUT2 product inhibition. The effect is intensity-dependent and largely independent of the gastric-emptying delay (which is driven by sympathetic pyloric constriction rather than perfusion). At moderate exercise (<50% VO2max), splanchnic flow reduction is modest (~20–30%) and absorption is minimally affected. At near-maximal effort, both gastric emptying and intestinal absorption are substantially impaired, compounding the delay in glucose appearance.

**2. Post-exercise splanchnic hyperaemia.** After exercise cessation, splanchnic blood flow normalises over 15–60 min, with a transient overshoot in some subjects ([Otte et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11310927/)). Any glucose bolus present in the intestine during this window may be absorbed faster than baseline, potentially producing a sharper-than-expected postprandial spike — the opposite of the exercise-delayed pattern.

**3. Postprandial hyperaemia.** Meal ingestion itself triggers a 30–130% increase in superior mesenteric artery flow, peaking 20–40 min post-meal ([Someya et al., 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18303946/)). This is a normal physiological response that accelerates absorption of the meal that triggered it. If exercise begins during this postprandial hyperaemic window, the two effects (exercise vasoconstriction vs. meal-induced vasodilation) partially cancel, reducing the net absorption delay.

**4. Autonomic neuropathy (chronic, T1D-specific).** Long-standing T1D with autonomic neuropathy may impair both the postprandial hyperaemic response and the exercise-induced redistribution, leading to unpredictable absorption kinetics. This is conceptually related to gastroparesis (already covered in §5) but affects the intestinal rather than gastric compartment.

| Modulator | Direction | Magnitude (change in absorption rate) | Time scale | Key reference |
|-----------|-----------|---------------------------------------|------------|---------------|
| Exercise >70% VO2max (splanchnic steal) | Slower | −40–60% | During exercise | [Qamar & Read, 1987](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3678950/) |
| Exercise <50% VO2max | Minimal | −10–20% | During exercise | [Qamar & Read, 1987](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3678950/) |
| Post-exercise recovery | Faster (transient) | +10–30% overshoot | 15–60 min post | [Otte et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11310927/) |
| Postprandial hyperaemia | Faster | +30–130% SMA flow | 20–40 min post-meal | [Someya et al., 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18303946/) |

> **Research notes — intestinal absorption modulation:**
> - Quantitative data on glucose absorption rate vs. splanchnic flow is sparse; most studies measure total appearance (emptying + absorption combined) rather than isolating the intestinal step.
> - Whether SGLT1 transporter density is acutely regulated (minutes–hours) by luminal glucose sensing, or only chronically adapted (days–weeks), affects whether repeated meals change absorption kinetics within a single day.
> - The interaction between exercise-induced splanchnic hypoperfusion and simultaneous postprandial hyperaemia needs quantitative dose-response data at graded exercise intensities.
> - Portal blood flow (which drains to the liver) vs. systemic arterial supply to the gut wall — both contribute to enterocyte clearance; their relative importance during exercise is unclear.

#### Intra- and inter-individual variability

A central challenge for insulin dosing and simulation alike is that the postprandial glucose appearance curve varies substantially between individuals and — less intuitively — within the same individual from day to day, even when meal composition and timing are controlled. This variability arises primarily from gastric emptying, which accounts for more of the meal-to-meal glucose variability than any other single physiological step between plate and plasma.

**Inter-individual variability.** Gastric-emptying half-time (t½) for a standardised solid meal shows an inter-subject coefficient of variation (COV) of ~24–40% in healthy adults, depending on the cohort and measurement protocol. Tougas et al. (2000) reported inter-subject COV of ~24.5% for t½ in a large cohort (n=319, standardised egg meal, 4-hour scintigraphy; [Tougas et al., 2000](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3465511/)). Desai et al. (2018) found inter-subject COV of ~40% for t½ in a mixed clinical cohort (n=60; [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/)). Mean t½ for the standardised egg meal was 121.7 ± 29.8 min (median 120, 5th–95th percentile 78–174 min), with females emptying ~15% slower than males (127.7 ± 28.7 vs. 109.9 ± 28.6 min, P<0.0001; [Tougas et al., 2000](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3465511/)). These values describe the population spread of basal gastric emptying — the range across which individual "default" emptying speeds differ, before any modulator (meal composition, stress, exercise) is applied.

**Intra-individual variability.** Within the same individual eating the same standardised meal on different days, t½ varies with an intra-subject COV of ~12–24%, depending on the population studied. In healthy subjects with normal emptying, Desai et al. (2018) found intra-subject COV of ~12% for t½ — substantially less than the inter-subject spread ([Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/)). In patients with delayed emptying the intra-subject COV rose to ~18%, and in those with rapid emptying it reached ~28%. Collier et al. (1990) similarly found that intrasubject variability was not statistically significant in healthy volunteers but was significant in diabetic patients, who showed higher day-to-day fluctuation ([Collier et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2378789/)). Siegel et al. (1993) confirmed the pattern: diagnostic classification (normal vs. delayed vs. rapid) was reproduced in 70% of patients across two tests, but changed in 30%, indicating that the intra-individual noise is large enough to straddle clinical cut-offs in a substantial minority ([Siegel et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8281844/); [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/)).

**T1D-specific variability.** Diabetic patients show greater intra-individual variability than healthy subjects, likely reflecting day-to-day fluctuation in autonomic function, antecedent glycaemia (the hyperglycaemia-mediated emptying delay varies with prevailing glucose), and subclinical neuropathic changes. Bao et al. (2021) demonstrated that in children with T1D on insulin pumps and CGM, postprandial time-to-peak had a within-person standard deviation of 39–65 min across controlled and free-living meals — larger than the between-person standard deviation (6–19 min), yielding intraclass correlation coefficients (ICC) below 0.2 ([Bao et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8620341/)). Controlling meal macronutrients (matched carbohydrate, fat, and protein) did not substantially reduce within-person variability, suggesting that non-dietary physiological modulators (circadian phase, antecedent glycaemia, autonomic tone, physical activity level) contribute the dominant share of postprandial glucose-appearance variability in T1D.

| Variability measure | Healthy subjects | Diabetic patients | Source |
|---------------------|------------------|-------------------|--------|
| Inter-subject COV, t½ | 24.5% (n=319, scintigraphy) | 47% (n=60, mixed clinical) | [Tougas et al., 2000](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3465511/); [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Intra-subject COV, t½ (normal GE) | 12% | 20–21% | [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Intra-subject COV, t½ (delayed GE) | — | 18% | [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Intra-subject COV, t½ (rapid GE) | — | 28% | [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Diagnostic reproducibility (normal/delayed/rapid) | 92% (t½ classification) | 70% (30% changed classification) | [Siegel et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8281844/); [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Within-person SD, time-to-peak BG (T1D children, controlled meal) | — | 39 min (ICC 0.18) | [Bao et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8620341/) |
| Within-person SD, time-to-peak BG (T1D children, free-living dinner) | — | 65 min (ICC 0.01) | [Bao et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8620341/) |

> **Research notes — variability:**
> - The distinction between gastric-emptying variability (measured by scintigraphy or ¹³C breath test) and postprandial glucose-appearance variability (measured by CGM time-to-peak) is important: the latter integrates emptying, absorption, splanchnic extraction, and insulin-on-board effects. CGM-based variability data (e.g., Bao et al.) therefore overestimate gastric-emptying variability per se.
> - Menstrual-cycle effects on gastric emptying have been documented (slower in the luteal phase; [Collier et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2378789/)) but are infrequently controlled for in variability studies, which may inflate reported intra-individual COV in female subjects.
> - The contribution of gut microbiome composition to inter-individual gastric-emptying variability is an emerging research area with no quantitative data suitable for modelling as of 2026.

#### Modelling-relevant parameter summary

For compartmental glucose–insulin models, the key parameters governing postprandial glucose appearance are the time constant(s) of the gut absorption sub-model and the bioavailability fraction. The two most widely used frameworks differ in their compartment structure:

**Hovorka 2004 model (two-compartment gut).** Glucose passes through a two-compartment chain (D1 → D2 → plasma) with a single shared time constant τ_G. The rate of glucose appearance into plasma is U_G = D2 / τ_G. Peak glucose appearance occurs at approximately t_peak ≈ τ_G (for a step input) or t_peak ≈ 2 × τ_G (for a ramp input spanning the eating duration). The model uses a fixed bioavailability A_G (originally 0.8 under UK labelling convention; 1.0 under EU/DK convention where label "carbohydrate" already excludes fibre). τ_G is the single tunable parameter for meal speed; in practice, a "slow meal" (high-fat, high-fibre) is represented by increasing τ_G while a "fast meal" (liquid glucose) uses a lower τ_G. The original Hovorka 2004 value is τ_G = 40 min, corresponding to t_peak ≈ 55–80 min for a typical 15-min eating duration — appropriate for a mixed solid meal ([Hovorka et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15382830/)).

**Dalla Man 2006/2007 model (three-compartment gut, UVA/Padova).** This model separates the stomach into a solid-phase compartment (Q_sto1) and a liquid-phase compartment (Q_sto2), with intestinal absorption from a third compartment (Q_gut). Gastric emptying follows a nonlinear rate k_empt(Q_sto) that varies with residual gastric content, capturing the caloric-clamp behaviour: k_empt is high at the start (k_max), decreases as feedback hormones rise, and eventually stabilises at k_min. Typical population values: k_max ≈ 0.0558 min⁻¹, k_min ≈ 0.0080 min⁻¹, k_abs ≈ 0.057 min⁻¹, bioavailability f ≈ 0.90 ([Dalla Man et al., 2006](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17226757/)). The nonlinear k_empt profile naturally produces the lag-then-linear emptying pattern observed for solid meals, which the Hovorka two-compartment model does not capture.

**How modulators translate to model parameters.** In a two-compartment model (Hovorka-type), each modulator can be implemented as a multiplicative scaling of τ_G:

τ_G_effective = τ_G_base × product(modifier_i)

where each modifier_i represents a single physiological modulator's effect on emptying rate. This is physiologically justified when modulators act on independent pathways (e.g., sympathetic pyloric tone vs. hormonal CCK feedback vs. osmolarity sensing), and approximately correct when modulators are individually moderate (each <2×). For large combined effects (e.g., exercise + hyperglycaemia + high fat simultaneously), a ceiling should be applied — empirically, solid-meal t½ rarely exceeds ~300–350 min even under maximal physiological delay.

The table below summarises suggested modelling parameters derived from the quantitative data in the preceding sections.

| Parameter | Baseline | Modulated range | Modelling approach | Source for baseline |
|-----------|----------|----------------|--------------------|--------------------|
| τ_G (time to peak absorption) | 40 min (mixed solid meal) | 25–150 min (across meal types and modulators) | Two-compartment chain, first-order | [Hovorka et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15382830/) |
| Stress modifier (acute) | 1.0 | 1.2–1.4 (moderate–severe stress) | Multiplicative on τ_G | [Thompson et al., 1983](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6411484/) |
| Stress modifier (chronic, CRH) | 1.0 | 1.1–1.3 (estimated) | Multiplicative on τ_G; time constant hours–days | [Taché et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11208537/) |
| Exercise modifier (>70% VO2max) | 1.0 | 1.2–1.5 | Multiplicative on τ_G; active during exercise | [Leiper et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11310927/) |
| Sleep/circadian modifier | 1.0 | 1.35–1.50 (nocturnal) | Multiplicative on τ_G; circadian phase-dependent | [Goo et al., 1987](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3692672/) |
| Hyperglycaemia modifier | 1.0 | 1.3–1.75 (BG >8 mmol/L) | Multiplicative on τ_G; proportional to BG above threshold | [Schvarcz et al., 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9207262/) |
| Fasting/MMC modifier | 1.0 | 0.80–0.90 (first 15–30 min after prolonged fast) | Multiplicative on τ_G; transient | [Deloose et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22407798/) |
| Inter-individual COV on τ_G | — | ~25% (healthy), ~40–47% (diabetic) | Gaussian multiplicative factor on τ_G_base | [Tougas et al., 2000](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3465511/); [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Intra-individual COV on τ_G | — | ~12% (healthy, normal GE), ~20% (diabetic) | Per-meal Gaussian noise on τ_G | [Desai et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6160344/) |
| Splanchnic absorption modifier (exercise) | 1.0 | 0.4–0.6 at >70% VO2max | Multiplicative reduction on absorption rate (U_G) | [Qamar & Read, 1987](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3678950/) |
| Gastroparesis modifier (late complication) | 1.0 | 1.5–4.0 (mild–severe); permanent | Sustained multiplicative on τ_G + increased stochastic COV | [Abell et al., 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18287197/); [Krishnasamy & Abell, 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6028327/) |

> **Open questions for modelling implementations:**
> - **Multiplicative vs. additive combination:** The multiplicative approach assumes modulator independence. At extreme combined delays (τ_G_effective > 200 min), empirical capping is needed because the model can predict implausibly slow absorption that does not match clinical observation.
> - **Hysteresis in exercise effects:** Gastric emptying delay during exercise does not reverse instantaneously at cessation. Recovery kinetics (how quickly τ_G returns to baseline) are poorly characterised; a first-order return with τ_recovery ≈ 15–30 min is a reasonable first approximation based on splanchnic blood flow normalisation data.
> - **Absorption rate vs. emptying rate during exercise:** Exercise simultaneously delays gastric emptying (via pyloric/sympathetic mechanism) AND reduces intestinal absorption (via splanchnic steal). In a two-compartment model with a single τ_G, these two effects can be approximated by a larger τ_G modifier, but this conflates the two mechanisms. A more faithful implementation would separately modulate the D1→D2 transfer rate (emptying) and the D2→plasma rate (absorption), allowing the model to capture the post-exercise rebound (absorption normalises faster than emptying because splanchnic flow recovery is faster than pyloric tone normalisation).
> - **GLP-1 RA effect on τ_G:** The meta-analytic ~36 min absolute increase in t½ translates to approximately +30–40% multiplicative modifier on τ_G for patients on these medications. Tachyphylaxis suggests this modifier may attenuate to +15–25% over weeks of therapy in ~60–70% of patients, remaining at +30–40% in ~30%.

*Implementation: see [§5 Food — From Plate to Bloodstream in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#food) for the simulator implementation. NB: the simulator uses the two-compartment Hovorka 2004 gut absorption model with a single composite first-order time constant τ_G per food and bioavailability A_G; it does not separate SGLT1, GLUT2, and GLUT5 fluxes; it does not model the lag-then-linear solid emptying profile; the volume-dependent caloric clamp is captured only indirectly through composition-dependent τ_G; soluble and insoluble fibre are aggregated into a single fibre modulator; first-pass splanchnic extraction is implicit in A_G rather than represented as a separate hepatic compartment; diabetic gastroparesis is not modelled. The dynamic τ_G system implements fat-mediated, fibre-mediated, carb-type-mediated, and hyperglycaemia-mediated (Michaelis-Menten saturating modifier above 8 mmol/L, calibrated against Schvarcz et al. 1997) modifiers as multiplicative scalings; the variability and modelling-parameters sections above provide quantitative targets for extending this system with exercise, stress, sleep, and stochastic variability modifiers.*

---

<a name="fat-and-protein"></a>
## 6. Fat and protein -- the forgotten macronutrients

Postprandial glycemia after a mixed meal is determined not only by carbohydrate-derived glucose appearance but also by two distinct families of mechanism set in motion by dietary fat and protein. The first family is **kinetic**: lipid in the duodenum slows gastric emptying through nutrient-sensing enteroendocrine feedback, and a sustained postprandial elevation of plasma free fatty acids (FFA) reduces insulin-stimulated glucose disposal in skeletal muscle through a diacylglycerol (DAG; lipid second messenger activating novel PKC isoforms)–PKCθ (protein kinase C theta, serine-phosphorylates IRS-1 impairing insulin signaling) pathway. The second family is **substrate- and hormone-driven**: amino acids contribute carbon skeletons for hepatic gluconeogenesis (a quantitatively modest contribution under fed conditions in healthy adults), but more importantly stimulate pancreatic α-cells to release glucagon, which drives hepatic glucose production. In healthy subjects the parallel β-cell insulin response opposes the glucagon signal and net postprandial glycemia is little affected by the protein component of a meal; in type 1 diabetes (T1D) the absent endogenous insulin response unmasks the glucagonotropic (glucagon-secretion-stimulating) effect of protein and accounts for the late, prolonged postprandial glucose rise that characterizes high-fat / high-protein meals (the "pizza pattern"). The same loss of paracrine insulin restraint also weakens the kinetic match between subcutaneous bolus insulin (peak action ~60–90 min) and the 2–5 h tail of fat- and protein-driven hyperglycemia.

### Mechanism

#### Fat: gastric emptying delay and postprandial peripheral insulin resistance

Lipid entering the duodenum is sensed by enteroendocrine I cells (CCK), L cells (GLP-1, PYY) and vagal afferents. The resulting feedback inhibits antral motility, stimulates tonic pyloric pressure, and slows the delivery of luminal contents to the intestine; CCK and GLP-1 also independently slow gastric emptying through vagally mediated and direct gastric smooth-muscle effects ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). The response is **load-dependent**. In healthy adults, intraduodenal lipid infused at 0.25 kcal/min already stimulates CCK release and isolated pyloric pressure waves, but loads ≥1.5 kcal/min are required to elevate basal pyloric pressure and PYY, and ≥4 kcal/min to reduce ad-libitum energy intake ([Pilichiewicz et al., 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17942490/)). The threshold for a measurable gastric emptying delay is therefore well below the lipid load delivered by a typical mixed meal containing ≥10–15 g fat, but the magnitude of the delay scales continuously with caloric load up to the saturation of antropyloroduodenal feedback at ~3–4 kcal/min. A fat preload predictably and substantially slows emptying of a co-ingested carbohydrate meal but, in T2D, produces only a modest reduction in peak postprandial glucose because the additional caloric load and the absence of a frank insulin secretagogue effect of fat itself partially offset the kinetic benefit ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)).

In addition to delaying glucose appearance, sustained elevations of plasma FFA reduce the rate of insulin-stimulated glucose disposal. The original explanation proposed by Randle and colleagues (the Randle cycle, or glucose–fatty acid cycle: competing substrate utilization mechanism; [Randle et al., 1963](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13990765/)) — that fatty-acid-derived acetyl-CoA and citrate inhibit pyruvate dehydrogenase and phosphofructokinase, redirecting muscle fuel selection — has been superseded for the human in vivo phenotype. Combined ¹³C/³¹P MRS and tracer studies in healthy adults during euglycemic-hyperinsulinemic clamps with lipid/heparin infusion show that elevation of plasma FFA does not alter insulin-stimulated glucose uptake during the first 3 h, then progressively reduces whole-body glucose disposal to ~46% of control by 6 h, with parallel reductions of intramyocellular glucose-6-phosphate (onset ~1.5 h) and muscle glycogen synthesis (~50% reduction over 3–6 h) ([Roden et al., 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8675698/)). The kinetics indicate impaired insulin-stimulated glucose **transport/phosphorylation**, not impaired oxidation. Mechanistically, intracellular accumulation of diacylglycerol (DAG) activates novel PKC isoforms (PKCθ in skeletal muscle, PKCε in liver), which serine-phosphorylate IRS-1 and the insulin receptor; serine phosphorylation impairs tyrosine phosphorylation, attenuating PI3K/Akt signalling and the downstream GLUT4 translocation ([Boden et al., 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12086926/); [Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). The relevance for postprandial glycemia is that the FFA tail of a high-fat meal — postprandial plasma FFA suppression by insulin is itself impaired in T1D when bolus dosing is mistimed — overlaps in time with the delayed glucose appearance from gastric emptying, so the same meal both delivers glucose late and reduces peripheral disposal capacity at the time it arrives.

#### Protein: gluconeogenesis, glucagonotropic stimulation of α-cells, and incretin release

Three mechanisms link dietary protein to postprandial glycemia, with very different quantitative importance.

**(1) Direct gluconeogenesis from amino acid carbon.** Approximately 50–60% of the amino acids in mixed dietary protein are theoretically glucogenic, raising the textbook expectation that 100 g of protein could yield 50–80 g of glucose. Doubly-labelled (¹⁵N, ¹³C) tracer studies measure the actual conversion as substantially smaller. Fromentin and colleagues fed healthy adults 23 g of intrinsically labelled egg protein under conditions designed to maximize gluconeogenesis (extended fast, high glucagon/insulin ratio) and quantified that only 3.9 ± 0.7 g of glucose over 8 h originated from dietary amino acids — approximately 8% of the 50 g of total endogenous glucose produced over the period, peaking at a 12% contribution at 4.5 h ([Fromentin et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23274906/)). The bulk of the deaminated amino acid carbon was oxidized directly. Comparable tracer measurements in T2D and healthy adults after a 50 g protein load (cottage cheese, beef) yield 4–10 g of glucose over 8 h ([Gannon & Nuttall, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613553/)). Patient-education claims that ~25–36% of protein converts to glucose ("Bernstein 25% rule") therefore overstate the substrate-driven contribution by a factor of 2–6. The rate of new glucose production attributable to dietary amino acids is approximately 0.5–1.5 g/h, sustained over 5–8 h.

**(2) α-cell stimulation and unopposed glucagon in T1D.** Amino acids — particularly arginine, alanine, glutamine and leucine — are direct glucagon secretagogues. Intravenous arginine produces 5- to 10-fold increases in plasma glucagon; oral protein produces a more modest 1.5- to 3-fold increase, with magnitude scaling with the amino-acid absorption rate ([Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/)). In healthy subjects the simultaneous β-cell amino-acid response (insulin secretion) (a) restrains α-cell glucagon release through paracrine signalling and (b) suppresses hepatic glucose production directly; the net postprandial glucose excursion attributable to protein is therefore small. In T1D the β-cell response is absent, and the α-cell glucagon response to oral protein is largely **preserved** — unlike the glucagon response to hypoglycemia, which is lost within ~5 years of diagnosis ([Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/)). The result is unopposed hepatic glucose output (glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis) over 2–5 h. This hormonal mechanism, not amino-acid carbon flux into glucose, is the dominant driver of the postprandial glucose rise after a high-protein meal in T1D.

**(3) Incretin effect and modest gastric-emptying delay.** Amino acids stimulate L-cell GLP-1 release and, together with the lipid component of mixed meals, contribute to the incretin-mediated slowing of gastric emptying ([Marathe et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613599/)). In healthy subjects with intact β-cells GLP-1 also potentiates insulin secretion. The combined effect can produce a transient *fall* in plasma glucose after a low- or moderate-dose protein-only load (no carbohydrate), observed in T1D as well: in the Paterson 2016 dose-ranging study (whey isolate, no insulin), the 25 g dose lowered plasma glucose by 1.06 mmol/L (95% CI −1.57 to −0.54; *P* < 0.001) over 60–120 min relative to water ([Paterson et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26499756/)). The mechanism in T1D is presumably attenuated incretin/insulin amplification together with delayed glucose appearance from any small endogenous β-cell residual or from glycogenolytic suppression by the small amount of paracrine insulin signal still present.

#### Time course

Protein and fat shift the postprandial glucose curve later and broaden it. Carbohydrate-derived glucose appearance starts at ~15 min and peaks at ~60 min in the absence of fat. In contrast, the Paterson 2016 cohort (T1D, whey isolate without insulin) showed a delayed onset of the protein-driven excursion at ~90–100 min, peak at ~180 min, and continued elevation throughout the 300-min observation ([Paterson et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26499756/)). For mixed high-fat/high-protein meals the time-to-peak shifts from ~80 min (low-fat/low-protein control) to ~140–145 min, with continued elevation at 5 h ([Smart et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24170749/); [Bell et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25998293/)).

#### Additivity of fat and protein effects

Smart and colleagues administered four breakfasts to 33 children with T1D (mean age 12.2 ± 2.5 y), each with identical 30 g carbohydrate but varying fat (4 vs 35 g) and protein (5 vs 40 g) content ([Smart et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24170749/)). Glucose excursions vs the low-fat/low-protein baseline at 180 min were +1.8 mmol/L (high-fat alone), +2.4 mmol/L (high-protein alone) and +4.2 mmol/L (high-fat plus high-protein); the sum of single-component excursions (4.2 mmol/L) matched the combined excursion to within rounding error, with no statistically significant interaction term. Time-to-peak shifted from ~79 min (LF/LP) to ~143 min (HF/HP). At 300 min the HF/HP arm remained 5.4 mmol/L above baseline.

The mechanistic interpretation is that fat acts on early gastric emptying and on late peripheral insulin resistance, whereas protein acts on glucagon-driven hepatic glucose output and contributes a small amount of gluconeogenic substrate. The two pathways operate on largely non-overlapping kinetic windows and on different organs, so their effects sum approximately linearly. Notably, the same study reported a substantially **lower** odds of postprandial hypoglycemia after high-protein meals (OR 0.16, 95% CI 0.06–0.41; *P* < 0.001) — consistent with the hypothesis that the early protein-driven glucagon signal counters early bolus over-coverage.

#### Protein source: absorption kinetics shape the glucagon response

Postprandial glucose excursions vary markedly across protein sources at matched protein dose, and the variation tracks the rate of plasma amino-acid appearance rather than total amino acid load. In Dao and colleagues' randomized study of T1D adults, postprandial glucose iAUC ranged ~2-fold from chicken (203 ± 66 mmol·min/L) and egg (263 ± 100) through beef (309 ± 89) and salmon (338 ± 83) to whey (397 ± 115) ([Dao et al., 2025](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39951019/)). The proposed mechanism is that fast-absorbing whey protein produces a larger and earlier plasma amino-acid spike, a larger glucagon spike, and therefore a larger hepatic glucose output, whereas slowly absorbed proteins (whole meats, casein) blunt the α-cell stimulus.

| Glucose contribution from a 50 g protein load (cottage cheese / beef) over 8 h | ~4–10 g | Healthy and T2D, n = 7 + 8; [Gannon & Nuttall, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23613553/) |

| Threshold dose for protein BG rise in mixed meals (with carb) | ~12.5 g | Systematic review; [Paterson et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31454430/) |
| Plasma glucagon response to oral protein (healthy and T1D) | ~1.5–3 fold above baseline | Review summary; [Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/) |

| Postprandial glucose iAUC range across protein sources (T1D) | 203 ± 66 (chicken) to 397 ± 115 mmol·min/L (whey); ~2-fold | [Dao et al., 2025](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39951019/) |
| OR for early postprandial hypoglycemia, high-protein vs low-protein (children with T1D) | 0.16 (95% CI 0.06–0.41; *P* < 0.001) | [Smart et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24170749/) |
| Threshold intraduodenal lipid load for CCK release and pyloric pressure-wave stimulation | ~0.25 kcal/min | Healthy males, n = 14; [Pilichiewicz et al., 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17942490/) |
| Threshold intraduodenal lipid load for basal pyloric pressure / PYY release | ≥ 1.5 kcal/min | [Pilichiewicz et al., 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17942490/) |
| Onset of FFA-induced peripheral insulin resistance after lipid + heparin infusion | First reductions in muscle G6P at ~1.5 h; whole-body glucose disposal not affected until ~3.5 h, ~46% of control by 6 h | Healthy adults, n = 9, ¹³C/³¹P MRS + clamp; [Roden et al., 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8675698/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Loss of paracrine β-cell insulin allows unopposed α-cell glucagon response to amino acids.** Unlike the glucagon response to hypoglycemia (which is lost within ~5 years of T1D onset because the intra-islet insulin signal that normally restrains α-cells is gone), the α-cell glucagon response to oral protein, exercise and insulin withdrawal is largely preserved in T1D ([Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/)). The postprandial glucose effect of protein in T1D is therefore primarily a hormonal phenomenon (glucagon/insulin imbalance), not a substrate phenomenon (amino acids → glucose).

2. **Loss of β-cell amplification of incretin signalling reduces or abolishes the early protein-induced glucose-lowering effect.** The transient fall in plasma glucose after low- to moderate-dose protein observed in some T1D dose-ranging studies ([Paterson et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26499756/)) is smaller than the analogous effect in healthy subjects and is absent above ~50 g dose, where the glucagon-driven hepatic glucose output dominates the net balance.

3. **Pharmacokinetic mismatch between subcutaneous bolus insulin and the fat/protein BG tail.** Rapid-acting analogue boluses (aspart, lispro) have peak action at ~60–90 min and effective duration ~3–4 h. The protein- and fat-driven glycemic effect peaks at ~140–180 min and persists ≥ 5 h. A single upfront bolus matched to carbohydrate content is therefore kinetically misaligned with the late tail of an HF/HP meal, producing the characteristic biphasic curve (early adequacy, late hyperglycemia) clinically described as the "pizza pattern."

4. **Reduced early postprandial hypoglycemia risk with high-protein meals.** Smart and colleagues observed an 84% reduction in postprandial hypoglycemia odds (OR 0.16) with high-protein vs low-protein meals in children with T1D using upfront carbohydrate-only boluses ([Smart et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24170749/)). The most plausible mechanism is the protein-induced glucagon response counterbalancing early bolus-driven glucose disposal.

5. **No consensus dosing algorithm for fat and protein in T1D.** Several practical algorithms (Pankowska fat–protein units, percentage uplift of carbohydrate-calculated dose, dual-wave/extended bolus) are in clinical use and are reviewed in pediatric and adult guidelines ([ISPAD 2022 nutritional management](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36468223/); [Evert, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32425452/)). Their selection and titration are clinical-practice questions outside the scope of this physiology document.

*Implementation: see [§Food — From Plate to Bloodstream in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#food) and [FFA-Induced Insulin Resistance in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#ffa-resistance) for the simulator implementation. NB: the simulator (a) approximates fat-induced gastric-emptying delay through a single fat-dose-dependent additive term in τG without resolving CCK/GLP-1/PYY kinetics; (b) represents the protein-driven late hyperglycemia as a delayed glucose-appearance flux rather than as an explicit α-cell glucagon response acting on hepatic glucose output (i.e., glucagon kinetics are not represented as a state variable); (c) implements FFA-induced peripheral insulin resistance as a static postprandial adjustment rather than the time-developing DAG–PKCθ-driven phenomenon documented above; (d) does not differentiate protein-source absorption kinetics. The dose-thresholds (≥ 75 g protein alone, ≥ 12.5 g in mixed meals) and the additivity coefficient from Smart 2013 are useful reference values for calibrating these terms but are not currently parameterized as such.*

# Part 3: Insulin pharmacology

<a name="insulin-pharmacology"></a>
## 7. Insulin -- from injection to effect

In healthy physiology, the β-cell delivers insulin in 5–8 min secretory bursts directly into the hepatic portal vein, generating a portal-to-peripheral concentration gradient of approximately 2–3:1 and an oscillatory signal that the liver discriminates from sustained background ([Pørksen, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11845219/); [Matveyenko et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22688333/)). Subcutaneous (SC) insulin therapy in T1D inverts this geometry: insulin enters peripheral venous return, bypasses hepatic first-pass extraction, and arrives at the hepatocyte and at peripheral tissues with a pharmacokinetic profile shaped almost entirely by the absorption from the SC depot. The clinical time-to-effect curve of an SC insulin dose is therefore the convolution of three serial processes — molecular dissociation in the depot, capillary uptake and systemic distribution, and intracellular signal transduction at the target cell — each of which has been characterised quantitatively and each of which contributes to the inter- and intra-individual variability that constrains glycemic control in T1D.

### Mechanism

#### Insulin receptor signaling

The insulin receptor (INSR) is a constitutive α₂β₂ tetrameric receptor tyrosine kinase. The *INSR* gene is alternatively spliced to yield two isoforms differing only by the inclusion (IR-B) or exclusion (IR-A) of the 12-residue exon-11 segment in the C-terminal portion of the α-subunit. IR-B predominates in liver, skeletal muscle, and adipose — the principal metabolic targets — whereas IR-A is enriched in fetal tissue, brain, and many tumours. Insulin binds both isoforms with comparable affinity (Kd ≈ 0.9–1.0 nmol/L for insulin), but IR-A binds IGF-2 (insulin-like growth factor 2, a mitogenic ligand with partial IR-A agonism) ~11-fold more tightly than IR-B (Kd ≈ 3.3 vs 36 nmol/L) and binds proinsulin ~7-fold more tightly, conferring its characteristic mitogenic-leaning signaling pattern ([Belfiore et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28973479/)).

Ligand engagement induces trans-autophosphorylation of the activation loop tyrosines (Tyr1158, Tyr1162, Tyr1163). The activated kinase recruits and tyrosine-phosphorylates IRS-1 and IRS-2 (insulin receptor substrate adaptor proteins, obligate metabolic signaling scaffolds) scaffolds; combined skeletal-muscle or hepatic *Irs1*/*Irs2* knockout phenocopies *Insr* knockout, establishing IRS proteins as the obligate metabolic signaling node ([Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). Phosphorylated IRS recruits class IA PI3K (phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase, generates PIP3 (phosphatidylinositol-3,4,5-trisphosphate, membrane lipid second messenger) for Akt activation) (p85 regulatory + p110 catalytic), generating PIP3 at the inner leaflet, which docks PDK1 and Akt (protein kinase B, the central insulin signaling kinase); PDK1 phosphorylates Akt at Thr308, mTORC2 at Ser473.

In skeletal muscle and adipose, activated Akt phosphorylates AS160/TBC1D4, releasing its Rab-GAP activity and permitting GLUT4 storage vesicle exocytosis to the sarcolemma. Combined *Tbc1d1*/*Tbc1d4* germline deletion in mice abolishes insulin-stimulated muscle glucose uptake ([Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). In hepatocytes, Akt phosphorylates FoxO1 at Thr24/Ser256/Ser319, causing nuclear exclusion and de-repression of gluconeogenic transcription (G6PC, PCK1) — sufficient on its own, in *Foxo1*-ablated mice, to normalise fasting hepatic glucose production. The parallel mitogenic arm (Shc → Grb2/SOS → Ras → Raf → MEK → ERK) is activated at higher insulin concentrations than the metabolic arm and is of secondary relevance in T1D therapy ([Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). Tissue-level dose–response is graded: insulin EC50 for suppression of adipocyte lipolysis is ~8–11 μU/mL (~44–68 pmol/L), well below the ~55 μU/mL EC50 for stimulation of whole-body glucose disposal ([Rizza et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7011057/); [Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)). See §25 for the full Rizza dataset and tissue-specific Hill kinetics.

#### Subcutaneous depot kinetics and analogue chemistry

Soluble insulin in pharmaceutical formulation exists as a concentration-dependent mixture of zinc-coordinated hexamers (~36 kDa), dimers (~12 kDa), and monomers (~6 kDa). The capillary endothelium of subcutaneous tissue is permeable to monomers and dimers but excludes hexamers; absorption is therefore rate-limited by the hexamer → dimer → monomer dissociation that follows dilution of the depot in interstitial fluid. In typical pharmaceutical concentrations (100 U/mL), human insulin and zinc-stabilised analogues are predominantly hexameric; the dimer dominates at 2–15 U/mL, the monomer below ~2 U/mL, with tissue absorption rates of ~5–10 min for monomers, ~20–30 min for dimers, and 1–2 h for hexamers ([Søeborg et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19150402/)).

Rapid-acting analogues are engineered to destabilise the hexamer:
- **Lispro (Humalog):** B28 Pro→Lys and B29 Lys→Pro reverse the canonical dimer-interface charge pattern.
- **Aspart (NovoRapid/NovoLog):** B28 Pro→Asp introduces a negative charge that destabilises the hexamer.
- **Glulisine (Apidra):** B3 Asn→Lys plus B29 Lys→Glu, formulated zinc-free and stable as a dimer; absolute SC bioavailability ~70%, apparent SC half-life ~42 min vs ~86 min for regular human insulin ([Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/)).
- **Faster aspart (Fiasp):** insulin aspart formulated with niacinamide (an absorption-accelerating excipient acting via local vasodilation) and L-arginine; in pooled clamp data (n=218 T1D adults, euglycemic clamp, 0.2 U/kg fasting), onset of appearance ~5 min earlier and tmax ~7 min shorter vs aspart, with early exposure (AUC₀–₃₀ min) approximately doubled ([Heise et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27878566/); [Heise et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31667789/)).

Long-acting basal analogues use the inverse strategy of stabilising or extending the depot:
- **Glargine (Lantus):** A21 Asn→Gly plus two C-terminal B-chain arginines shift the isoelectric point toward neutral pH; the formulation is acidic (pH 4) and precipitates as microcrystals on injection into neutral interstitial pH, then re-dissolves slowly. The active species in plasma is the proteolytically processed M1 metabolite (21A-Gly-human insulin). Steady-state plasma concentration is reached after ~2 days for U100 glargine and ~4 days for U300 ([Evans et al., 2011](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21410860/)).
- **Detemir (Levemir):** B30 Thr-deletion plus a C14 myristic-acid acylation at Lys B29; reversibly binds plasma albumin (>98% bound), buffering free-insulin fluctuations.
- **Degludec (Tresiba):** B30 Thr-deletion plus C16 hexadecandioic-acid acylation at Lys B29 via a γ-glutamic acid linker. After SC injection the molecule self-assembles into soluble multi-hexamer chains in the depot, from which monomers separate slowly and continuously; plasma half-life ~25 h (vs ~12 h for glargine U100), steady state within 3 days, and within-subject AUC variability ~4-fold lower than glargine U100 (CV ~20% vs ~82%) ([Haahr & Heise, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24729196/)).

Absolute SC bioavailability of soluble rapid-acting analogues is ~70% — values close to 100% have been reported on within-formulation crossover comparisons (e.g., faster aspart vs aspart, where the bioavailability ratio is ~1.0 because both routes share the same loss processes), but referenced against IV the extraction is closer to 70% ([Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/); [Heise et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27878566/)). Bioavailability falls modestly with increasing dose volume because larger depots pool, slow capillary uptake, and increase local proteolysis. Capillary uptake is regionally heterogeneous: in human studies of regular insulin and lispro, abdominal injection produced Tmax less than half that of deltoid or thigh, with Cmax ~32% lower at thigh and ~42% lower at deltoid relative to abdomen ([Heinemann, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30058925/)). Heating the injection site to 40 °C reduced Tmax by ~42% for insulin aspart in T1D, and exercise of the underlying muscle (legs after thigh injection) accelerates absorption further; intramuscular injection halves Tmax relative to SC ([Heinemann, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30058925/)).

#### Insulin clearance

Endogenous insulin secreted into the portal circulation is removed predominantly by the liver: per-pass hepatic extraction of insulin in healthy humans is ~50%, with reported values up to ~80% in some receptor-mediated kinetic analyses ([Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/)). Hepatic clearance is receptor-mediated: insulin binds hepatocyte INSR, the complex is internalised via clathrin-coated pits coordinated by phosphorylated CEACAM1, and the internalised insulin is degraded in early endosomes by insulin-degrading enzyme (IDE), a Zn²⁺-dependent metalloprotease — though liver-specific *Ide* deletion in mice does not cause hyperinsulinemia, suggesting IDE has additional regulatory roles in receptor trafficking beyond proteolysis ([Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/)). The kidney contributes ~30–40% of total clearance via glomerular filtration with ~99% tubular reabsorption and degradation; renal clearance falls in chronic kidney disease and prolongs apparent insulin half-life. Plasma half-life of intravenous insulin is ~4–6 min in healthy adults; for SC injection the apparent half-life is dominated by the absorption rate constant (typically 30–90 min for rapid-acting analogues, several hours for basal analogues), not by elimination ([Becker, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18076215/)).

SC injection routes insulin into peripheral venous return, bypassing portal first-pass extraction. The resulting peripheral hyperinsulinemia (relative to portal-vein hyperinsulinemia at matched whole-body insulin action) is the structural pharmacokinetic divergence between exogenous SC insulin therapy and endogenous β-cell secretion, and underlies several characteristic features of T1D management discussed below.

#### Loss of pulsatility

Healthy β-cells co-ordinate insulin secretion in 5–8 min pulses superimposed on a slower 80–120 min ultradian rhythm. Studies of pulsatile vs constant intra-portal insulin infusion in canine and rodent models showed that the pulsatile pattern produced 50–80% higher hepatic IRS-1/IRS-2 phosphorylation, stronger Akt and FoxO1 activation, and approximately doubled hepatic glucokinase mRNA induction relative to constant infusion at matched mean concentration ([Matveyenko et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22688333/)). The clinical magnitude of this effect in human T1D — where pulsatility is irretrievably lost regardless of analogue or pump strategy — has not been precisely quantified, but it is one mechanistic strand contributing to the residual hepatic insulin resistance observed even in T1D patients on optimised SC therapy.

| Hepatic first-pass insulin extraction (healthy) | ~50% (range up to ~80%) | First-pass extraction; receptor-mediated uptake + IDE-dependent degradation | [Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/) |
| Renal clearance contribution | ~30–40% of total clearance | Glomerular filtration + ~99% tubular reabsorption/degradation; falls in CKD | [Najjar & Perdomo, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30968756/) |
