| GLUT4 surface translocation kinetics on insulin stimulation | Half-maximal in ~5–10 min, full effect in ~20–30 min | Kinetics drawn from secondary reviews; primary canonical source ([Richter & Hargreaves, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23899560/)) is paywalled — see wishlist | [Chadt & Al-Hasani, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32591907/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Inverted insulin gradient.** Endogenous β-cell insulin establishes a portal:peripheral concentration ratio of ~2–3:1 with ~50% per-pass hepatic extraction. SC-injected insulin enters peripheral venous return, bypasses hepatic first-pass extraction, and reaches the liver only after systemic dilution. To suppress hepatic glucose production by an equivalent amount, peripheral insulin concentrations in T1D must therefore exceed physiological values, producing relative peripheral hyperinsulinemia. Adipose lipolysis is suppressed at lower insulin concentrations than hepatic glucose production (EC50 ~8–11 vs ~29 μU/mL; Rizza et al., 1981), so peripheral hyperinsulinemia preferentially over-suppresses lipolysis at peak action and under-suppresses gluconeogenesis at trough — a pharmacokinetic asymmetry that contributes to fasting hyperglycemia and the difficulty of replicating physiological 24-hour glucose homeostasis with SC therapy ([Edgerton et al., 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34328272/); [Petersen & Shulman, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30067154/)).

2. **Loss of pulsatile signaling.** All current SC regimens (multiple daily injections, CSII (continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion, i.e., pump therapy) basal-rate pumps) deliver continuous or near-continuous insulin appearance rather than the 5–8 min pulses of healthy β-cell secretion. In animal models, replacing pulsatile portal infusion with constant infusion at matched mean concentration reduces hepatic IRS-1/IRS-2 phosphorylation by 50–80% and roughly halves hepatic glucokinase induction ([Matveyenko et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22688333/)). The quantitative contribution to T1D hepatic insulin resistance in humans has not been isolated experimentally.

3. **Inter-injection variability.** Even under controlled glucose-clamp conditions in healthy subjects, intra-individual CV (coefficient of variation, measure of intra-individual variability as %) of regular SC insulin AUC is 15–25%, and inter-individual CV is ~10% higher again ([Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/)). Long-acting NPH and intermediate-acting insulins have CV >50% — depot mixing complexity drives variability. Modern rapid-acting analogues do not substantially reduce this variability under controlled conditions, although degludec achieves ~4-fold lower within-subject variability than glargine U100 (CV ~20% vs ~82%) by virtue of its multi-hexamer depot mechanism ([Haahr & Heise, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24729196/)).

4. **Lipohypertrophy.** Repeated injection into the same SC site produces fibrofatty hypertrophic nodules in 30–50% of long-treated T1D patients (prevalence figures vary across observational studies and ascertainment methods). Insulin injected into lipohypertrophic tissue shows ~25% lower Cmax and ~22% lower AUC₀–₄ₕ than into normal SC tissue, with within-subject CV rising to 52–55% (vs 11–15% in normal tissue) — sufficient to abolish the predictability needed for meal bolusing ([Heinemann, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30058925/)). Site rotation and short (4–6 mm) needles are recommended in current consensus guidance to mitigate this complication ([Frid et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27594187/)).

5. **Modifiers of absorption rate.** Regional injection site (abdomen > arm > thigh > buttock by Tmax), local temperature (40 °C heating reduces aspart Tmax by ~42%), exercise of the underlying muscle, depth of injection (intramuscular halves Tmax vs SC), depot dose volume, and lipohypertrophic distortion all modulate absorption ([Heinemann, 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30058925/)). The exercise-modified absorption interaction has further pharmacodynamic consequences described in §8.

6. **Insulin antibodies.** Anti-insulin antibodies (AIAs) are detectable in 5–30% of T1D patients on modern human insulin and analogues — substantially less than the 60–90% prevalence seen with bovine and porcine insulins of the pre-recombinant era. AIAs can buffer free insulin and produce anomalous PK profiles (delayed peak, prolonged tail), but their clinical impact in modern therapy is generally modest; specific quantitative descriptions are beyond the scope of this section and the prevalence ranges are aggregated from heterogeneous assay platforms.

*Implementation: see [§4 Insulin — The Key to the Cells in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#insulin) for the simulator implementation. NB: the simulator (a) uses a two-compartment Hovorka 2004 SC absorption cascade (S1 → S2 → plasma I) with a single absorption rate constant per insulin source rather than resolving hexamer → dimer → monomer dissociation kinetics; (b) does not differentiate insulin analogues by molecular chemistry — calibration is via lumped Tmax and bioavailability for each insulin "type" rather than via the analogue-specific dissociation constants; (c) does not model dose-volume-dependent absorption slowing for large boluses; (d) does not model hepatic first-pass extraction (since SC-injected insulin bypasses portal delivery, the simplification is appropriate for the SC-therapy context but excludes the modelling of physiological/intra-portal infusion modes); (e) does not represent insulin antibodies, lipohypertrophy, or regional injection-site variability as state variables; (f) represents insulin signaling at the kinetic-rate level (x1 transport, x2 disposal, x3 EGP suppression remote effects) rather than as explicit IR–IRS–PI3K–Akt–AS160 cascades. The intra-injection variability noted above is partially captured via a CV ≈ 25% on the absorption time constant (see §13 Variability in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md).*

# Part 4: Physical Activity

<a name="aerobic-exercise"></a>
## 8. Aerobic exercise -- running, cycling, swimming

Aerobic exercise creates a high glucose-flux state in skeletal muscle that is largely insulin-independent during the bout and that produces a sustained, insulin-sensitizing aftereffect lasting up to 48 h. Three kinetic phases govern the glycemic response in T1D: (1) acute contraction-mediated glucose uptake, in which working muscle increases its glucose uptake up to ~50-fold above resting values via translocation of GLUT4 to the sarcolemma without requiring insulin signaling ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/nrendo.2016.162)); (2) an early post-exercise window (~30 min to several hours) dominated by glycogen-resynthesis-driven glucose disposal and persistent surface GLUT4; and (3) a late post-exercise insulin-sensitivity (PEIS (post-exercise insulin sensitivity, lasting 24–48 h after the bout)) phase lasting 24-48 h, mediated by enhanced AS160 phosphorylation downstream of an unchanged proximal insulin signaling cascade ([Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/); [Wojtaszewski et al., 2000](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10868952/)). In T1D, all three phases interact with circulating exogenous insulin in ways that determine acute hypoglycemia risk during the bout and delayed (often nocturnal) hypoglycemia risk after it ([Riddell et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28126459/)).

### Mechanism

#### Acute contraction-stimulated glucose uptake

Contraction activates glucose transport via signaling pathways distinct from -- and partially additive with -- those engaged by insulin ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/nrendo.2016.162)).

- **AMPK**: AMP/ATP elevation during contraction allosterically activates AMP-activated protein kinase, particularly the α2 isoform in working muscle. AMPK phosphorylates TBC1D1 (and AS160/TBC1D4), inactivating their Rab-GAP activity and permitting GLUT4 vesicle exocytosis. AMPK α2 activity reverses to baseline within ~30-60 min after exercise stops ([Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/)).
- **Ca²⁺/CaMKII**: SR Ca²⁺ release during excitation-contraction coupling activates Ca²⁺/calmodulin-dependent protein kinase II independently of AMPK; CaMKII regulates contraction- but not insulin-induced glucose uptake (rodent KO and pharmacological evidence; mechanism inferred to operate similarly in humans).
- **Rac1**: A small Rho-family GTPase activated by mechanical stretch and contraction, Rac1 drives actin cytoskeletal remodeling required for GLUT4 vesicle docking; muscle-specific Rac1 deletion in mice reduces contraction-stimulated glucose uptake by ~30-40% (rodent data; human evidence supportive but indirect).
- **GLUT4 trafficking**: At rest only ~5% of skeletal-muscle GLUT4 resides at the cell surface. Insulin alone or contraction alone each increases surface GLUT4 to ~25-50%, and combined insulin + contraction is more than additive, consistent with at least partially distinct intracellular GLUT4 storage pools ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/nrendo.2016.162)). The molecular identity of the contraction-recruited pool remains under investigation.
- **Substrate flux**: Whole-body glucose disposal can rise from ~2 mg/kg/min (basal post-absorptive) to >10 mg/kg/min during moderate aerobic exercise; muscle-specific extraction can rise up to 50-fold over rest (key-points statement, not a single quantified mean; [Sylow et al., 2017](https://www.nature.com/articles/nrendo.2016.162)).
- **Skeletal-muscle blood flow**: Increases from ~3-5 mL/min/100 g at rest to 50-80 mL/min/100 g during moderate cycling and ≥100-150 mL/min/100 g during near-maximal exercise (a 15-30-fold rise), supplying both substrate delivery and mass-transport capacity for glucose extraction.

#### Insulin pharmacokinetic modulation during exercise

Subcutaneous insulin absorption accelerates during and immediately after exercise. The mechanism combines (i) increased subcutaneous adipose-tissue blood flow, (ii) raised local tissue temperature reducing hexamer dissociation time, and (iii) -- when the depot lies near working muscle -- mechanical mobilization of insulin from the depot ([Pitt et al., 2020](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7609903/)). Pooled data summarized in the Pitt review report 1.5- to 3-fold acceleration of plasma-insulin appearance during moderate exercise; the magnitude depends on injection-to-working-muscle distance, exercise intensity, ambient temperature, and individual SC perfusion. Quantitative effects on T_max and AUC vary substantially across studies and insulin analogs (heterogeneous protocols; values cited from review tables, not a single primary dataset).

#### Post-exercise insulin sensitivity -- two kinetic phases

**Early phase (~1-4 h post-exercise) -- enhanced insulin-stimulated glucose uptake without proximal signaling amplification.** In a one-legged exercise paradigm with euglycemic-hyperinsulinemic clamp 4 h after exercise, the previously exercised leg activated insulin-stimulated glucose uptake faster (half-time 11 vs 34 min) and 2- to 4-fold more strongly than the rested leg, despite no enhancement of insulin-receptor tyrosine kinase, IRS-1-associated PI3K activity, or proximal Akt activity ([Wojtaszewski et al., 2000](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10868952/)). The effect localizes to phosphorylation of AS160 (TBC1D4) on multiple Akt and AMPK target sites (Thr⁶⁴², Ser⁵⁸⁸, Ser³¹⁸, Ser³⁴¹, Ser⁷⁵¹), increasing GLUT4 exocytosis at submaximal insulin concentrations ([Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/)). Glycogen depletion in the working fibers is a permissive condition: refeeding with carbohydrate to restore glycogen rapidly reverses the increment in insulin-stimulated glucose uptake (causal relationship to glycogen depletion is supportive but not conclusively established; [Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/)).

**Late phase (~24-48 h post-exercise) -- the classical Mikines/PEIS phenomenon.** A single 60-min bout at ~150 W on a cycle ergometer in untrained men reduced apparent half-maximal insulin concentration (Km) for whole-body glucose uptake from 52 ± 3 μU/mL at rest to 40 ± 3 μU/mL at 48 h post-exercise, while raising V_max from 9.5 ± 0.8 to 10.7 ± 0.8 mg·min⁻¹·kg⁻¹ ([Mikines et al., 1988](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3126668/) -- abstract data; small sample, n = 7, untrained men, single intensity). The effect was no longer detectable at 5 days. Maximal glucose-to-glycogen conversion rose from 5.7 ± 0.6 to 7.2 ± 0.2 mg·min⁻¹·kg⁻¹ at 48 h. Subsequent clamp studies have replicated 25-50% increases in whole-body insulin-stimulated glucose disposal at 16-48 h post-exercise across both healthy and insulin-resistant populations ([Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/)).

#### Substrate utilization and the crossover concept

Across exercise intensities the relative contributions of carbohydrate (CHO) and fatty-acid oxidation shift predictably ([Brooks & Mercier, 1994](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7928844/) -- abstract truncated at 250 words; full text on paywalled wishlist). At low intensity (≤45% V̇O₂max (maximal oxygen uptake, gold-standard measure of aerobic capacity)) lipid oxidation predominates; at high intensity (~75% V̇O₂max) CHO oxidation predominates. The "crossover point" -- the power output at which CHO-derived fuels overtake lipids as the dominant energy source -- typically lies in the moderate-intensity range. Endurance training shifts the crossover rightward (higher absolute intensity) by enhancing mitochondrial fat-oxidation capacity and reducing sympathoadrenal responses to a given submaximal load. The crossover quantitatively depends on training status, fiber-type composition, glycogen availability, and prior nutrition; a single universal threshold percentage should be interpreted cautiously.

In T1D, the substrate-mix shift carries kinetic implications: low-intensity, long-duration aerobic work draws disproportionately on lipid oxidation and glycerol/lactate gluconeogenic precursors and exerts comparatively modest pressure on circulating glucose, whereas high-intensity continuous aerobic exercise rapidly draws muscle glycogen and plasma glucose. Counterregulatory catecholamine rise at higher intensities partially offsets this glucose flux via hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis ([Hatting et al., 2017](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5723278/)).

| Whole-body glucose disposal, moderate aerobic (~60-65% V̇O₂max) | ~6-10 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ | Healthy adults and T1D; clamp + tracer (Romeres protocol) | [Romeres et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8525018/); [Boiroux & Lichtenstein, 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8321821/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Acute hypoglycemia risk during the bout.** In T1D, exogenous insulin cannot be down-regulated in real time. When a bolus or basal insulin remains pharmacologically active during aerobic exercise, contraction-driven GLUT4 translocation is added on top of the insulin-stimulated GLUT4 pool, producing a near-additive (sometimes supra-additive) increase in muscle glucose extraction. This kinetic combination -- working muscle drawing on both pools simultaneously -- is the proximate cause of the rapid BG fall observed during moderate aerobic exercise on active insulin. Empirically, 45 min of treadmill running at 60% V̇O₂max produced a 3.4 mmol/L plasma BG drop in physically active T1D adults, a fall of ~4.5 mmol/L per hour ([Yardley et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3579339/)). In a separate hyperinsulinaemic-euglycaemic clamp, the extra glucose infusion required to prevent BG from falling increased from 1.8 ± 0.4 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ at 35% V̇O₂peak to 4.2 ± 0.7 at 65%, then remained statistically similar at 3.5 ± 0.7 at 80% ([Shetty et al., 2021](https://academic.oup.com/jcem/article/106/1/e83/5937229)). This small study (n=9, with only two participants completing 40 min at 80%) supports an intensity plateau under high circulating insulin rather than protection from hypoglycaemia at the highest continuous aerobic workload.

2. **Inability to suppress endogenous insulin secretion.** In healthy subjects the islet downregulates insulin output sharply at exercise onset, allowing hepatic glucose production to rise unopposed. T1D patients lack this counterregulatory adjustment; circulating exogenous insulin continues to suppress hepatic glucose production while peripheral glucose disposal climbs. Modeling work has quantified that the insulin-dependent component of exercise-stimulated glucose disposal is several-fold larger in T1D (+81 to +155%) than in matched non-diabetic controls (+10 to +40%), and the difference is statistically significant ([Boiroux & Lichtenstein, 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8321821/) -- n=6 per group, single intensity). This kinetic asymmetry is the population-level explanation for why aerobic exercise carries higher hypoglycemia risk in T1D than the underlying muscle physiology alone would predict.

3. **Catecholamine-driven counterregulation is variable and conditional.** Adrenaline and noradrenaline rise at higher exercise intensities and stimulate hepatic glycogenolysis, partially offsetting muscle glucose uptake. However, the response is blunted in (i) patients with hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure, (ii) those with depleted hepatic glycogen reserves from prolonged poor glycemic control or recent fasting, and (iii) sustained moderate-intensity work where catecholamine rise is modest. Reliance on catecholamine-driven hepatic glucose output as a safety mechanism is therefore unreliable for moderate aerobic exercise.

4. **Delayed (often nocturnal) hypoglycemia.** Late-phase PEIS, with a half-life of ~12-24 h ([Cartee, 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4816200/); [Mikines et al., 1988](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3126668/)), overlaps with sleep when an evening exercise session is followed by overnight basal insulin. The combination of reduced hepatic glucose output (basal insulin acting on a relatively glycogen-depleted liver) and elevated insulin sensitivity in skeletal muscle (PEIS) produces a kinetic window of high hypoglycemia susceptibility several hours after the bout. CGM studies indicate post-aerobic-exercise nocturnal hypoglycemia is more common than after resistance exercise ([Yardley et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3579339/)).

5. **Kinetic asymmetry of onset and recovery.** BG falls quickly when aerobic exercise begins on top of active insulin (minutes); BG recovery after the bout is slow, because insulin pharmacokinetics continue to act on a now insulin-sensitized muscle bed and PEIS persists for hours-to-days. Practical implication: pre-exercise insulin reduction or carbohydrate intake must be planned in advance of the bout (the pharmacokinetic delay of even rapid analogs prevents a same-minute correction), and post-exercise carbohydrate / basal-insulin titration must extend through the PEIS window, not just the immediate recovery period ([Riddell et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28126459/)).

NB: the simulator uses the Rashid/Resalat extended Hovorka model with a discrete fast (contraction-mediated) and slow (PEIS) component; substrate switching between CHO and fat oxidation is not represented; AMPK / Ca²⁺ / Rac1 activation is collapsed into a single contraction-effect surrogate; and the SC insulin-absorption acceleration during exercise is approximated by a heart-rate-scaled rate constant rather than mechanistic blood-flow / temperature compartments.*

---

<a name="anaerobic-exercise"></a>
## 9. Anaerobic training -- resistance training and sprinting

Resistance training, sprinting and high-intensity interval training (HIIT) are often grouped as “anaerobic” exercise, but their acute glycemic responses are not interchangeable. Fasted HIIT commonly raises plasma glucose, whereas conventional resistance exercise has produced a smaller fall than aerobic exercise, no mean change under clamp conditions, or a modest rise depending on time of day and circulating insulin ([Yardley et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3579339/); [Toghi-Eshghi & Yardley, 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31211392/); [Young et al., 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10511169/); [Riddell et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30641168/)). The shared mechanism is simultaneous elevation of EGP (endogenous glucose production) and contraction-mediated glucose disposal; net BG direction depends on their balance with circulating insulin, prandial state, exercise structure and time of day.

When EGP exceeds glucose disposal, sympathoadrenally driven glycogenolysis and lactate-supported gluconeogenesis (the Cori cycle; lactate-to-hepatic-glucose recycling pathway) produce acute hyperglycemia. Resistance exercise can instead leave BG stable when the two fluxes rise by similar amounts. The post-exercise phase adds muscle glycogen resynthesis and prolonged insulin sensitization, which can shift the balance toward delayed hypoglycemia. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), the catecholamine response, lactate flux and contraction-mediated GLUT4 translocation are largely intact, but the post-exercise endogenous insulin pulse is absent and glucagon defense against later hypoglycemia is impaired ([Marliss & Vranic, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11815492/); see [§11](#counterregulatory-hormones)). This physiology produces substantial inter- and intra-individual variation rather than a universal “strength raises BG” rule.

### Mechanism

#### Sympathoadrenal activation and the catecholamine surge

Supramaximal exercise (>80% V̇O₂max), heavy resistance training, and the high-intensity intervals of HIIT activate the sympathoadrenal axis far more rapidly and intensely than continuous aerobic work matched for total energy expenditure. In the islet-cell-clamp study of [Sigal et al. (1996)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8549858/), 14 min of cycle ergometry at 87 ± 2% V̇O₂max raised plasma norepinephrine and epinephrine by more than 12-fold above baseline (numbers cited from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). [Bally et al. (2016)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26739816/) confirmed that intermittent high-intensity exercise produces significantly higher catecholamine, growth-hormone and lactate excursions than energy-matched continuous moderate-intensity exercise in adults with T1D, while [Moser et al. (2015)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26317981/) reported peak plasma lactate of 4.93 ± 1.80 mmol/L during HIIT at the second lactate turn point in trained T1D men. Cortisol rises with a delay (~30 min onset) and contributes to the slower-developing component of post-exercise insulin resistance, while pulsatile growth-hormone secretion during and immediately after intense exercise is a third counterregulatory signal acting on hepatic glucose output and peripheral lipolysis ([Marliss & Vranic, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11815492/); [Bally et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26739816/)).

#### Catecholamine-driven hepatic glucose production

The acute hyperglycemic response in intense exercise is driven primarily by a hepatic glucose production (Ra) that outruns peripheral glucose utilization (Rd). [Purdon et al. (1993)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8445012/) measured a 7-fold rise in Ra and 4-fold rise in Rd during exhaustive cycle ergometry (~12 min at 100% V̇O₂max) in both T1D and control subjects (numbers from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). The same Ra/Rd ratio is reported in [Marliss & Vranic (2002)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11815492/). Mechanistically, hepatic glycogenolysis is activated by β2-adrenergic Gαs–cAMP–PKA signaling, with parallel α1-adrenergic IP3/DAG/Ca²⁺ contributions to phosphorylase kinase activation (see [§2](#hepatic-glucose-production) for the molecular cascade). The causal role of catecholamines was directly demonstrated by [Kreisman et al. (2003)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12765943/), who showed that infusion of norepinephrine to 33.1 ± 2.9 nmol/L plus epinephrine to 7.06 ± 0.44 nmol/L during moderate exercise reproduced the high-intensity exercise Ra (12.9 ± 0.8 vs 3.8 ± 0.4 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ in control) and BG rise (numbers from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist).

A countervailing view comes from the [Wasserman (2009)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2636990/) review of canine and human catheterization studies, which concluded that pancreatic-clamp and hepatic-denervation experiments dissociate catecholamine concentrations from hepatic glucose output during low-to-moderate-intensity work, and attribute the dominant exercise Ra signal to a small drop in insulin combined with glucagon. Wasserman explicitly notes that "attempts to directly assess a role for catecholamines in stimulating endogenous glucose production during high-intensity exercise (>80% V̇O₂max) in humans have also been negative." The reconciliation that has emerged is that at intensities ≥80% V̇O₂max, where post-exercise hyperinsulinemia is observed in healthy subjects, a non-pancreatic catecholamine-driven Ra drive becomes detectable; at lower intensities the hepatic insulin–glucagon axis dominates. The disagreement is a live issue in the field rather than a settled question, and the quantitative weight assigned to catecholamines in exercise glucose production should be regarded as intensity-dependent.

#### Lactate, the Cori cycle, and gluconeogenesis

Type II glycolytic muscle fibers, recruited preferentially during heavy resistance work and supramaximal sprints, produce lactate at rates that rapidly exceed local mitochondrial oxidation, raising plasma lactate from a resting ~1 mmol/L to peaks of 3–8 mmol/L during HIIT and resistance protocols ([Bally et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26739816/); [Moser et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26317981/)). Lactate is exported from glycolytic fibers via monocarboxylate transporter MCT4 (lactate efflux transporter in glycolytic fibers) and enters oxidative fibers, the heart and the liver via MCT1 (lactate influx transporter in oxidative tissues) ([Brooks, 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2805372/)). Hepatic uptake of lactate fuels gluconeogenesis along the lactate dehydrogenase → pyruvate → pyruvate carboxylase → PEPCK → fructose-1,6-bisphosphatase → glucose-6-phosphatase pathway (see [§2](#hepatic-glucose-production)). In direct stable-isotope tracer measurements at the lactate threshold ([Emhoff et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8846961/)), gluconeogenesis accounts for ~25% of total hepatic glucose output in trained subjects and ~40% in untrained subjects during exercise, and rises to nearly 60% of total glucose production when arterial lactate is clamped at high concentrations — the mechanistic basis for the lactate-correlated component of the BG rise reported in T1D HIIT studies ([Riddell et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30641168/)). Most of the lactate produced is oxidized rather than converted to glucose: at sustained exercise of 50–75% V̇O₂max, ~75–80% of lactate disposal is oxidative and only ~20% supplies gluconeogenesis ([Brooks, 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2805372/)). The Cori cycle therefore contributes a consistent but minority share to acute exercise hyperglycemia; the catecholamine-driven glycogenolytic flux dominates.

#### Skeletal-muscle glucose uptake during anaerobic work

Muscle contraction translocates GLUT4 to the sarcolemma via an AMPK/Rac1-dependent pathway that is independent of insulin signaling ([Sylow et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27739515/)). Young et al. (2023) separated glucose utilization into insulin-mediated and non-insulin-mediated components during moderate- and high-intensity resistance exercise in 25 adults with T1D. Non-insulin-mediated utilization increased during exercise and returned toward baseline approximately 30 min after exercise, whereas insulin-mediated utilization remained unchanged; EGP AUC increased by 1.04 mmol/L (95% CI 0.65–1.43) and Rd AUC increased by 1.26 mmol/L (95% CI 0.41–2.10), leaving mean BG unchanged ([Young et al., 2023](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10511169/)). This is distinct from exhaustive or HIIT protocols, in which hepatic production can outpace the concurrent rise in disposal. Bally et al. (2016) similarly found lower exogenous glucose requirements during the final 30 min of HIIT than during continuous moderate exercise of equal mean intensity, consistent with a larger endogenous glucose contribution.

#### Post-exercise glycogen resynthesis and the delayed disposal phase

Resistance exercise depletes muscle glycogen by approximately 24–40% of resting content per session, with type II fibers showing markedly greater depletion than type I fibers (~48% near-depletion of intramyofibrillar pools in type II vs ~33% loss of intermyofibrillar pool in type I) ([Hamidvand et al., 2025](https://physoc.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.14814/phy2.70683)). HIIT and prolonged sprint protocols produce comparable or greater depletion. Replenishment proceeds biphasically: while glycogen is severely depleted (<25% of resting), local control by glycogen-synthase activation and glucose transport drives resynthesis at high rates (~33 mM·h⁻¹) that are largely insulin-independent; once glycogen recovers above ~35 mM, resynthesis becomes obligatorily insulin-dependent and falls to ~3.5 mM·h⁻¹ ([Price et al., 1994](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7909795/)) (numbers from open abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). The transition from insulin-independent to insulin-dependent resynthesis coincides with the post-exercise insulin sensitization (PEIS) discussed in [§8](#aerobic-exercise): the slow component of PEIS persists 24–48 h, with magnitudes scaling with intensity (~+45% at HIIT-equivalent intensities). The kinetic consequence is that for 4–12 h after a heavy resistance or HIIT bout, muscle constitutes a large insulin-sensitive sink that competes successfully with hepatic output for circulating glucose, biasing the system toward hypoglycemia.

[Yardley et al. (2013)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23172972/) provided comparative T1D data: in 12 active adults randomized to 45 min of resistance training, aerobic running or rest, plasma glucose fell from 8.4 ± 2.7 to 6.8 ± 2.3 mmol/L during resistance training (P = 0.008), compared with 9.2 ± 3.4 to 5.8 ± 2.0 mmol/L during running. Young et al. (2023) observed no mean BG change during tracer-clamp resistance exercise, while Toghi-Eshghi and Yardley (2019) observed a rise from 9.5 ± 3.0 to 10.4 ± 3.0 mmol/L during fasted morning resistance exercise but a fall from 8.2 ± 2.5 to 7.4 ± 2.6 mmol/L in the afternoon. Together, these studies define resistance exercise as context-dependent: typically less glucose-lowering than continuous aerobic exercise, but not intrinsically hyperglycemic.

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Catecholamine-driven hyperglycemia uncovered by absent post-exercise insulin pulse.** In healthy individuals, intense exercise terminates with a transient hyperinsulinemia (~40–60 min) that suppresses Ra and accelerates glycogen replenishment ([Marliss & Vranic, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11815492/)). In T1D this pancreatic response is absent, so Rd remains relatively elevated through recovery while Ra falls only as catecholamine levels decay; the resulting prolonged hyperglycemia is a kinetic consequence of the route-of-insulin-delivery problem (see [§2](#hepatic-glucose-production), [§7](#insulin-pharmacology)). Acute correction with subcutaneous insulin during the bout therefore over-corrects, because the bolus reaches peak action (60–90 min) on a curve that is already falling and that is then potentiated by the developing PEIS — the recipe for late hypoglycemia.

2. **Preserved glucagon response to exercise but lost glucagon defense against hypoglycemia.** The α-cell glucagon response to exercise is largely retained in T1D, in contrast to the rapid loss of the glucagon response to hypoglycemia within the first ~5 years of disease ([Bengtsen & Møller, 2021](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34405569/); see [§11](#counterregulatory-hormones)). The exercise-glucagon signal contributes to the acute hepatic glucose output during the bout, but provides little defense against the post-exercise hypoglycemic phase — the most clinically relevant defect.

3. **Delayed and frequently nocturnal hypoglycemia.** The timing of post-exercise hypoglycemia after a heavy resistance or HIIT session typically falls in the 4–12 h window, with [Yardley et al. (2013)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23172972/) documenting significant interstitial glucose suppression at 4.5–6.0 h. Evening or late-afternoon training therefore creates substantial nocturnal hypoglycemia risk, compounded by the impaired glucagon response and by sleep-blunted catecholamine and symptomatic awareness.

4. **Prandial-state-dependent BG response.** Meta-analytic data ([McClure et al., 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36549943/)) show that the same HIIT protocol raises BG by +1.7 mmol/L in fasted T1D and lowers it by −2.1 mmol/L when performed postprandially. The dichotomy reflects the balance between catecholamine-driven Ra (intensity-dependent, prandial-state-independent) and bolus-insulin-amplified Rd (only present postprandially). Within-subject reproducibility is high (r ≈ 0.58 across four sessions; [Riddell et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30641168/)), justifying individual prediction algorithms.

5. **Elevated baseline gluconeogenesis amplifies the exercise Ra rise.** [Petersen et al. (2004)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15356077/) demonstrated that fasting hepatic glucose production is ~60% higher in moderately controlled T1D than in healthy controls and that the increase is entirely accounted for by elevated gluconeogenesis; the contribution of glycogenolysis to total Ra is reduced (rest 20% vs 32% in controls; high-intensity exercise 32% vs 57%). Because hepatic glycogen stores are smaller in T1D and gluconeogenic flux is already elevated, the exercise Ra response is shifted toward the lactate-driven gluconeogenic component and the Cori cycle assumes a quantitatively larger role than in healthy subjects.

6. **Acute resistance-exercise BG fall is smaller than aerobic, but post-exercise disposal is larger.** [Yardley et al. (2013)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23172972/) reported that the within-bout BG drop during 45 min of resistance training (−1.6 mmol/L) was about half the drop during 45 min of aerobic running (−3.4 mmol/L), but mean interstitial glucose between 4.5 and 6.0 h was significantly lower after resistance training. The kinetic distinction matters clinically: an algorithm that is calibrated to the within-bout BG response of aerobic exercise will under-predict the late-phase risk after resistance training.

7. **Resistance exercise and HIIT require separate calibration.** Fasted HIIT produced a mean rise of +3.7 ± 1.6 mmol/L across 64 sessions ([Riddell et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30641168/)), whereas resistance protocols produced −1.6 mmol/L, no mean change, or approximately +0.9 mmol/L across the controlled studies above. Applying the HIIT response magnitude to ordinary resistance exercise therefore overstates acute hyperglycemia and obscures the roles of active insulin and time of day.

*Implementation: see [§Activity — Four Activity Types with Different Physiology in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#activity) for the simulator implementation. The simulator separates contraction-mediated uptake, exercise-specific hepatic glucose production, delayed insulin-mediated sensitivity, perfusion-dependent insulin absorption and muscle-glycogen turnover. Strength training uses a smooth fourth-order Hill latency gate with a 150-minute half-onset time for its insulin-mediated sensitivity response. This keeps the response near baseline during a 45-minute bout and early recovery, consistent with Young et al. (2023), while avoiding an unsupported biological switch at one exact minute. The half-onset time is a conservative implementation parameter rather than an identified molecular constant. The exercise-specific hepatic response is shared by all fictional characters; only the existing weight-based body scale changes. The absolute contraction flux is scaled around the 70 kg reference because the game has no independent lean-mass state. Plasma lactate and MCT1/MCT4 transport are not explicit states; their net hepatic contribution is aggregated in the exercise-specific hepatic-drive term. Prandial state and time of day affect the shared insulin and circadian physiology but are not resistance-specific parameters.*

---

<a name="exercise-inflammation"></a>
## 10. Exercise-induced inflammation -- when exercise hurts

Habitual moderate exercise is durably anti-inflammatory and improves insulin sensitivity, but a single bout of unaccustomed, eccentric, or excessive exercise produces a transient pro-inflammatory state that *raises* insulin requirements for 24–72 h before sensitivity returns and improves. Three mechanistic dimensions overlap kinetically: (i) the contraction-induced release of myocyte-derived cytokines, principally interleukin-6 (IL-6) acting as a myokine; (ii) classical pro-inflammatory cytokines (TNF-α, IL-1β) released by neutrophils and infiltrating macrophages in response to mechanical sarcomere disruption; and (iii) downstream attenuation of insulin signalling in skeletal muscle and liver through serine phosphorylation of insulin receptor substrate-1 (IRS-1) and induction of suppressor of cytokine signalling 3 (SOCS3). The phenomenon is well characterized in healthy adults and athletes, with limited direct measurement in type 1 diabetes (T1D); in clinical T1D practice the post-marathon and post-unaccustomed-resistance-training phase is recognized as a period of erratic glucose control and frequent late-onset hyperglycaemia despite predicted post-exercise insulin sensitization (PEIS, see §8). The signal magnitude depends critically on the loading mode: lengthening (eccentric) contractions produce far more sarcomere damage and a larger post-exercise insulin-resistance signal than shortening (concentric) contractions of comparable mechanical work.

### Mechanism

#### IL-6 as a contraction-induced myokine — biphasic and metabolically context-dependent

IL-6 is the prototypical exercise myokine. Skeletal muscle contraction induces transcription of *IL6* via a TNF-independent calcium- and AMPK-dependent pathway, and contracting muscle releases IL-6 into the circulation in proportion to exercise intensity, duration, and muscle mass recruited ([Pedersen & Febbraio, 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18923185/) — full text paywalled, quantitative figures cited below derive from open abstract and the meta-analysis of [Bekos et al., 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8893166/); [Pedersen, 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19696361/)). Plasma IL-6 increases approximately exponentially during exercise, peaks at or shortly after exercise cessation, and falls rapidly during recovery. Reported fold-rises depend on the workload: 60 min of moderate cycling produces ≈10–20-fold increases above the resting concentration of ≈1–2 pg/mL; a 21-km half-marathon produces a similar order-of-magnitude rise; a 42-km marathon produces 30- to 100-fold rises; and ultra-endurance events (50–250 km, multi-day) produce the largest documented elevations, 80- to >100-fold ([Pedersen & Febbraio, 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18923185/); [Bekos et al., 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8893166/) — pooled SMD −1.36 half-marathon, −6.81 marathon, −8.00 ultra-marathon). Plasma IL-6 has a short circulating half-life (estimates range from ≈5 to 11 min during the rapidly clearing post-exercise phase, lengthening to 1–6 h at low resting concentrations because of soluble-receptor binding; population values are method-dependent). Half-marathon IL-6 typically returns to baseline within ≈30 min to a few hours, marathon IL-6 remains elevated for ≥24 h, and ultra-marathon IL-6 returns to baseline by 24 h ([Bekos et al., 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8893166/)).

The acute exercise-induced rise in IL-6 is **anti-inflammatory in net effect**: it suppresses TNF-α production, induces IL-10 and the IL-1 receptor antagonist (IL-1ra), increases lipolysis and fat oxidation, and acutely *enhances* insulin-stimulated glucose disposal in healthy subjects ([Petersen & Pedersen, 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15772055/); [Pedersen & Febbraio, 2008](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18923185/)). This profile is distinct from the sustained, low-grade IL-6 elevation observed in obesity, metabolic syndrome, and type 2 diabetes, where IL-6 (in concert with TNF-α) is associated with insulin resistance. The conventional reconciliation — that the cellular source, kinetics, and accompanying cytokine milieu determine the metabolic outcome of an IL-6 signal — is consistent with the in vivo data but mechanistically incomplete; the precise determinants of "good" myokine IL-6 versus "bad" inflammatory IL-6 remain an open question in exercise immunology.

#### Mechanical muscle damage: eccentric loading, neutrophil and macrophage recruitment, secondary inflammation

The transition from a primarily anti-inflammatory exercise response to a pro-inflammatory one is driven by **mechanical sarcomere disruption**, not by metabolic load per se. Lengthening (eccentric) contractions — as occur during downhill running, the lowering phase of resistance exercise, novel resistance loads, or unaccustomed activity — generate high tensile force on individual sarcomeres and produce Z-disk streaming, focal sarcomere disruption, plasma membrane disruption, and local oedema visible on histology and MRI within hours ([Asp et al., 1996](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1160686/)). Concentric (shortening) contractions of comparable mechanical work produce relatively little sarcomere damage: the Philippe et al. comparison of matched-work uphill (concentric) vs downhill (eccentric) walking found a significant IL-6 rise after concentric but not eccentric loading and divergent 24-h glucose-tolerance responses ([Philippe et al., 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4885626/)).

Disrupted myocytes release damage-associated molecular patterns (DAMPs) including HMGB1, mitochondrial DNA, creatine kinase, and myoglobin into the interstitium and circulation, recruiting neutrophils within hours and mononuclear phagocytes (M1 macrophages) over the next 24–48 h. Plasma creatine kinase (CK), an indirect index of sarcolemmal damage, peaks 24–96 h after eccentric exercise and is several-fold higher than after matched-work concentric exercise ([Kirwan et al., 1992](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1629073/) — CK 273 ± 73 IU/L at 48 h after eccentric vs 87 ± 25 IU/L control; [Asp et al., 1996](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1160686/)). Macrophage activation amplifies local TNF-α and IL-1β release; neutrophils generate reactive oxygen species that produce additional ("secondary") muscle damage and oxidatively modify insulin-signalling proteins. Notably, **systemic plasma TNF-α and IL-1β responses to eccentric exercise are inconsistent across studies** — with several investigations reporting little or no plasma rise despite clear evidence of muscle damage and insulin resistance ([Hirose et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15633588/) — 6 sets of 5 maximal eccentric elbow-flexor contractions in 10 untrained males produced large CK and myoglobin rises but no significant systemic TNF-α elevation). The most parsimonious reading is that the relevant cytokine signal is paracrine and intramuscular rather than systemic, and that systemic plasma cytokine sampling underestimates the local insulin-signalling impairment.

After a marathon, the systemic cytokine pattern is characteristic: IL-6 and IL-10 rise sharply (≈4-fold and ≈11-fold respectively immediately post-race), TNF-α rises modestly (≈1.5-fold) and returns to baseline by 24 h, IL-1ra rises substantially (pooled SMD −5.65), while CK remains elevated for ≥72 h, indicating ongoing muscle repair ([de Sousa et al., 2021](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8554198/); [Bekos et al., 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8893166/)).

#### Cytokine-induced insulin resistance: serine kinases, IRS-1 Ser307, and SOCS3

The convergence of eccentric mechanical damage, paracrine TNF-α/IL-1β release, and ROS generation activates two stress-responsive serine/threonine kinase cascades in skeletal muscle and liver:

- **JNK (c-Jun N-terminal kinase)** is activated by TNF-α, oxidative stress, and elevated free fatty acids. Activated JNK phosphorylates IRS-1 on Ser307 (mouse numbering; the homologous human residue Ser312 is functionally equivalent in most studies). IRS-1 Ser307 phosphorylation impairs subsequent insulin-stimulated tyrosine phosphorylation of IRS-1 by the activated insulin receptor and dissociates IRS-1 from the receptor, attenuating PI3K–Akt signalling ([Hilder et al., 2003](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14668089/)). Because IRS-1 Ser307 phosphorylation is also driven by intramyocellular diacylglycerol via PKCθ in the FFA-induced insulin resistance pathway (see §6), the post-damaging-exercise and FFA pathways converge on a common downstream lesion.

- **IKKβ (IκB kinase β)** is activated by similar inflammatory inputs and phosphorylates IκBα, releasing NF-κB to translocate to the nucleus and induce transcription of inflammatory genes including *SOCS3*. SOCS3 then physically binds the insulin receptor and IRS-1, sterically blocking tyrosine phosphorylation and accelerating IRS-1 ubiquitin–proteasomal degradation ([Emanuelli et al., 2001](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC419873/) — IL-6 induces SOCS3 in adipocytes and abolishes insulin-stimulated IRS-1 tyrosine phosphorylation).

The combined effect of attenuated IRS-1 tyrosine phosphorylation and SOCS3 induction is reduced PI3K–Akt activation, reduced AS160 (TBC1D4) phosphorylation, reduced GLUT4 translocation to the sarcolemma, and reduced glucose uptake at any given insulin concentration. Asp and colleagues, after eccentric exercise of the elbow flexors, additionally found a ≈39% reduction in basal GLUT4 protein content in the eccentric muscle (P < 0.05) — a transcriptional/translational reduction in cellular GLUT4 capacity that compounds the signalling defect ([Asp et al., 1996](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1160686/)).

Hepatic insulin resistance follows analogous logic: cytokines (especially IL-6 in chronic states; TNF-α and IL-1β acutely) and elevated FFA induce SOCS3 and activate IKKβ in hepatocytes, attenuating insulin's normal suppression of hepatic glucose production (HGP). The kinetic consequence is that the same insulin dose produces less suppression of HGP for ≈24–48 h after damaging exercise.

#### Time course of post-damaging-exercise insulin resistance

Combining the available human clamp studies, the kinetic envelope of post-eccentric-exercise insulin resistance is:

- **0–6 h post-exercise:** Insulin sensitivity is acutely *increased* (PEIS dominates; see §8); contraction-induced GLUT4 translocation and depleted glycogen accelerate glucose uptake.
- **24–48 h post-eccentric exercise:** Insulin-stimulated whole-body glucose disposal is reduced by ≈12–37% relative to control, depending on muscle mass involved and severity of damage. [Kirwan et al. (1992)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1629073/) measured 3.47 ± 0.51 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ at 48 h post-eccentric vs 5.48 ± 1.0 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ control (≈37% reduction). [Asp et al. (1996)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1160686/) measured a 15.7% reduction in whole-body glucose infusion rate (P < 0.05) and an 11.9% reduction in maximal insulin-stimulated leg glucose uptake at 48 h, with the local muscle effect insufficient to fully account for the systemic deficit. [Tuominen et al. (1996)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8779957/) measured a 12% reduction in whole-body glucose disposal the morning after a 42-km marathon, with a 36% reduction in glucose oxidation under clamp and 2.2-fold elevated basal FFA — consistent with both cytokine- and FFA-driven serine-kinase pathways operating concurrently.
- **48–72 h:** Insulin sensitivity recovers to baseline in trained or moderately stressed individuals; in severely damaged muscle (heavy unaccustomed eccentric loading) the deficit can persist for 4–7 days.

#### Adaptation and the repeated-bout effect

Repeated exposure to the same exercise stimulus markedly attenuates the damage and inflammatory response — the **repeated-bout effect** ([Hirose et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15633588/)). After a second bout of identical eccentric exercise 4 weeks later, CK release, soreness, strength loss, and the (modest) systemic cytokine response are typically reduced by 30–60%; the effect appears to involve neural adaptation, sarcomere remodelling toward longer optimal length, extracellular matrix reinforcement, and conditioning of the inflammatory infiltrate. Trained individuals therefore mount substantially smaller IL-6, TNF-α, and CK responses to a given workload, and chronic training reduces basal high-sensitivity CRP by approximately 20–35% relative to sedentary controls ([Kasapis & Thompson, 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15893167/) — full text paywalled; figures cited from open abstract and structured summary). The clinical implication is a continuous gradient: an unaccustomed bout in an untrained individual is maximally pro-inflammatory and insulin-desensitising; the same workload performed habitually by a trained individual is anti-inflammatory and insulin-sensitising in net effect.

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Direct T1D data are scarce.** Most quantitative information on cytokine kinetics, eccentric-damage signalling, and the magnitude of post-damaging-exercise insulin resistance derives from healthy adults, T2D cohorts, and athletes; very few studies have directly measured insulin-clamp glucose disposal or insulin-dose requirements in the 24–72 h window after damaging or unaccustomed exercise in T1D specifically. Inferences for T1D therefore rely on (i) the assumption that the muscle-cell-autonomous and hepatic mechanisms (IRS-1 Ser307 phosphorylation, SOCS3 induction, GLUT4 down-regulation) are not altered by the absence of endogenous insulin, and (ii) clinical observation of glycaemic patterns in T1D athletes. Both lines of evidence converge on a qualitatively similar mechanism, but the precise magnitude of insulin-requirement increase in T1D cannot be cited with the same numerical confidence as the healthy-cohort values above.

2. **Insulin requirements in T1D may rise after unaccustomed intense or prolonged exercise.** Clinical practice guidelines and case series describe a glucose-rise pattern in T1D 12–48 h after marathons, intensive resistance training, and unaccustomed novel exercise that is qualitatively consistent with cytokine-mediated insulin resistance overlapping the late phase of PEIS ([Riddell et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28126459/)). The reported magnitude of any *increase* in basal/correction insulin requirement during this window varies widely; the often-quoted "10–30% increase in insulin requirements for 1–2 days after unaccustomed hard training" is a clinical heuristic rather than a rigorously quantified clamp-based number, and the lower bound is often dominated by the opposing PEIS effect.

3. **Inverse phenotype to typical PEIS.** The same activity has opposite glycaemic implications depending on training status and damage burden: in trained T1D individuals, habitual aerobic exercise reduces 24-h post-exercise insulin requirements through PEIS (§8); in untrained individuals or after a damaging novel bout, insulin requirements during the 24–72 h window may rise above pre-exercise baseline. The transition is continuous along the fitness × novelty × eccentric-damage gradient, not a binary switch, and predicting it on an individual session is the central practical challenge.

4. **Eccentric-dominant activities at higher inflammation risk.** Downhill running, novel resistance training (especially the lowering phase), unaccustomed plyometrics, and prolonged endurance events with significant downhill or impact components are most likely to produce the post-exercise pro-inflammatory state. Concentric-dominant activities of comparable metabolic load (uphill walking, cycling at moderate intensity, swimming) tend to remain net insulin-sensitising.

5. **Practical kinetic implication.** The post-marathon glucose-rise pattern in T1D — rising insulin requirements with simultaneously elevated FFA and depleted muscle glycogen — is consistent with concurrent FFA-induced PKCθ-mediated insulin signalling impairment (§6), cytokine-induced JNK/IKKβ activation, and reduced cellular GLUT4 capacity in the damaged musculature. The three pathways converge on attenuated PI3K–Akt signalling and operate over the same 24–72 h kinetic window. The clinical consequence is erratic glucose control during this window with a tendency toward hyperglycaemia rather than the hypoglycaemia expected from PEIS alone, and a need for cautious correction-dosing with extended monitoring.

*Implementation: see [§Activity — Four Activity Types in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#activity). NB: exercise-induced inflammation is not currently implemented in the simulator — neither the acute pro-inflammatory cytokine state, the eccentric-damage signal, nor the 24–72 h post-damaging-exercise insulin-resistance phase. The simulator's PEIS model (see §8) captures the *anti-inflammatory* insulin-sensitizing aftereffect of moderate non-damaging exercise but not its inverse for damaging or unaccustomed exercise. A future extension would require a fitness-level state, an exercise-novelty / eccentric-load metric, or per-session muscle-damage scoring with a 24–72 h decaying insulin-resistance multiplier acting on both peripheral glucose disposal and hepatic insulin sensitivity.*

---

<a name="relaxation-yoga"></a>
## 10b. Relaxation, yoga, and meditation -- stress reduction and blood glucose

Yoga, meditation, mindfulness-based interventions, and structured slow-paced breathing protocols engage the autonomic and neuroendocrine substrates of the stress response: they raise cardiac vagal tone, attenuate sympathoadrenal activation, and dampen hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis output. Each of these efferent arms also drives glucoregulatory effectors — hepatic glucose production, peripheral insulin action, lipolysis, and cytokine-mediated insulin signalling — so a measurable glycaemic footprint is mechanistically plausible. The empirical literature, however, is dominated by type 2 diabetes (T2D) and non-diabetic populations, with reductions in HbA1c that are statistically significant but small (point estimates ~0.25–0.35%) and a heterogeneous evidence base ([Hamasaki, 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37755243/); [Innes & Selfe, 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26788520/)).

In type 1 diabetes (T1D), direct outcome data are limited to a single recent randomised trial in adolescents ([Maurya et al., 2025](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40518537/)) and indirect inference from autonomic-physiology studies. The most defensible interpretation is that relaxation practices contribute to glycaemic control through indirect channels — mitigation of stress-induced hyperglycaemia, improved sleep, reduced diabetes distress, and better self-care adherence — rather than through a direct, large insulin-independent glucose-lowering action. Caveats around effect-size magnitude and population transferability are flagged inline below where they affect interpretation.

### Mechanism

#### HPA-axis attenuation and cortisol

Stress activation of the paraventricular nucleus of the hypothalamus drives corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH) → anterior pituitary adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) → adrenocortical cortisol secretion. Cortisol acts genomically via the glucocorticoid receptor on glucocorticoid-response elements in the *PCK1* and *G6PC* promoters, upregulating phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase (PEPCK) and glucose-6-phosphatase (G6Pase) and increasing hepatic gluconeogenic capacity over 30–120 min ([Hatting et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28868790/) — cited in §2). Cortisol additionally induces post-receptor peripheral insulin resistance and is permissive for glucagon and adrenaline action.

Mindfulness-based stress reduction (MBSR) and structured yoga interventions reduce cortisol output. The Pascoe et al. (2017) meta-analysis of 42 trials reported significant reductions in evening cortisol and waking cortisol with yoga and MBSR versus active controls, alongside lower resting heart rate and elevated high-frequency (HF) HRV (heart rate variability, non-invasive autonomic nervous system marker) — a parasympathetic marker ([Pascoe et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28963884/)). Earlier MBSR work in oncology populations (Carlson et al., 2004) found shifts in diurnal salivary cortisol patterns from inverted-V to V-shaped profiles after an 8-week programme, with afternoon cortisol decreases tracking quality-of-life improvement ([Carlson et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14749092/) — cancer cohort, n = 58 pre / 42 post; results characterised by the authors as pilot data). The magnitude of cortisol reduction in non-clinical adults is most commonly reported as a 15–25% reduction in salivary cortisol AUC after 8 weeks of MBSR; values in T1D populations have not been quantified separately.

#### Autonomic balance and the inflammatory reflex

Slow-paced breathing at the resonance frequency (~6 breaths per minute, with prolonged exhalation) entrains baroreflex oscillations and amplifies HF-HRV power. Single-session crossover studies show large within-subject increases in RMSSD (root mean square of successive RR-interval differences, vagal HRV index) during the breathing epoch (Cohen's d ≈ −1.2 across 5–20 min sessions, with no detectable dose-response between session durations), accompanied by a "turn-on/turn-off" return to baseline at cessation ([Laborde et al., 2022 — single-session study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8656666/)). A meta-analysis of 223 studies confirms vagally-mediated HRV increases during voluntary slow breathing, immediately after one session, and after multi-session interventions; quantitative pooled effect sizes are not provided in the open abstract — full-text on paywalled wishlist ([Laborde et al., 2022 — meta-analysis](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35623448/)).

Vagal activation has a downstream anti-inflammatory action through the cholinergic anti-inflammatory pathway: efferent vagal cholinergic signals act on the α7-nicotinic acetylcholine receptor (α7-nAChR) on splenic macrophages and other immune cells, suppressing release of TNF-α, IL-1β, IL-6, and IL-18 without affecting the anti-inflammatory cytokine IL-10 ([Borovikova et al., 2000](https://www.nature.com/articles/35013070); [Tracey, 2002 — concept and review](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1430829/) — the reflex framework was developed primarily in rodent endotoxaemia models, with subsequent human translation through bioelectronic stimulation studies). Because TNF-α and IL-6 contribute to hepatic and skeletal-muscle insulin resistance through Ser-phosphorylation of IRS-1 and SOCS-3 induction, the reflex provides a plausible route from sustained vagal training to improved insulin signalling. The clinical magnitude of this contribution in T1D is undefined.

#### Direct vasomotor and microvascular effects

Slow paced breathing increases arterial baroreflex sensitivity in healthy adults and in chronic heart failure, with concomitant reductions in resting blood pressure and modest peripheral vasodilation. Insulin-mediated vasodilation in skeletal muscle is functionally coupled to insulin-stimulated glucose uptake in healthy humans ([Cleland et al., 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9931164/)), so any sustained increase in resting microvascular flow could in principle augment insulin delivery to GLUT4-expressing fibres. The clinical relevance for fasting or postprandial T1D glycaemia has not been quantified and is likely small relative to the cortisol/catecholamine route.

### Quantitative characterization

| HbA1c reduction, mindfulness-based interventions in diabetes (mostly T2D) | Pooled MD ≈ −0.25 to −0.35% (95% CI typically −0.7 to −0.04); one outlier review reported −0.75% | Five overview-of-reviews entries; heterogeneity I² 0%–93% across reviews; T1D contribution minimal | [Hamasaki, 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37755243/) |
| HbA1c reduction, yoga in T2D (controlled trials) | ~0.5% (range 0.31–21% across studies; large heterogeneity, mostly small RCTs) | 25 controlled trials, n = 2 170; 12-week interventions most common | [Innes & Selfe, 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26788520/) |
| HbA1c reduction, yoga adjunct in adolescent T1D | Within-group −1.37 percentage points (9.33 → 7.96%); −14.7% relative; p < 0.001 vs control | Single RCT, completers n = 46/92 (high attrition during COVID-19); preliminary, not yet replicated | [Maurya et al., 2025](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40518537/) |
| Insulin total daily dose change, adolescent T1D yoga RCT | −6.5% within-group (52.4 → 49.0 U·kg⁻¹·d⁻¹); not significant between groups | Same trial; unblinded; secondary outcome | [Maurya et al., 2025](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40518537/) |
| Cortisol effect, yoga / MBSR meta-analysis (mixed populations) | Significant reductions in evening and waking cortisol vs active control; specific effect sizes from open abstract not given | 42 RCTs across populations; full quantitative table on paywalled wishlist | [Pascoe et al., 2017](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28963884/) |
| Diurnal salivary cortisol pattern, MBSR 8-week (oncology cohort) | Shift from inverted-V to V-shape; afternoon cortisol decreased; no overall change in DHEAS or melatonin | Pilot data, n = 58 pre / 42 post; non-T1D | [Carlson et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14749092/) |
| Acute increase in vagally-mediated HRV (RMSSD), single 5–20 min slow-paced breathing | Within-subject d ≈ −1.2 vs control; effect size flat across 5–20 min | Healthy adults; effect dissipates rapidly after cessation | [Laborde et al., 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8656666/) |
| Resting HF-HRV in T1D vs healthy (supine) | T1D 459 (633) ms² vs control 1 087 (1 741) ms²; LF/HF ratio not different | Reduced cardiac vagal modulation already at early-stage T1D; relevance: T1D often starts from a lower vagal baseline | [Bekkering et al., 2022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35846022/) |
| Cardiac autonomic neuropathy prevalence, long-standing T1D | Up to ~35% | Caps the upper bound of vagal-tone improvement attainable through breathwork in established disease | [Bekkering et al., 2022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35846022/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Most evidence is T2D-derived and not directly transferable.** Four of five systematic reviews of mindfulness in diabetes pooled T1D and T2D data without separate outcome reporting; only one cited primary trial (Ellis et al., emerging adults with poorly controlled T1D) is purely T1D-based ([Hamasaki, 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37755243/)). Innes & Selfe (2016) explicitly excluded T1D from their yoga review on grounds of distinct aetiology ([Innes & Selfe, 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26788520/)). Effect estimates on HbA1c (≈ −0.3%) should therefore be regarded as upper-bound extrapolations to T1D rather than established treatment effects.

2. **Pathway specificity in T1D differs from T2D.** Stress hyperglycaemia in T1D is driven by catecholamine- and cortisol-stimulated hepatic glucose production *unopposed* by an endogenous insulin response, with additional dysregulated alpha-cell glucagon secretion (see §11 and §20). Relaxation interventions plausibly attenuate all three counterregulatory drivers transiently. The unresolved empirical question is whether transient attenuation translates to clinically meaningful HbA1c improvement when the patient remains dependent on exogenous insulin for the dominant glucose-lowering signal.

3. **Indirect mechanisms are likely the larger contributor.** Diabetes distress and elevated perceived stress are independently associated with higher HbA1c, more frequent severe hypoglycaemia, missed boluses, and lower quality of life in T1D cohorts ([Ehrmann et al., 2023 — DIA-LINK1](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36625417/)). Improvements in sleep, mood, and self-management adherence may account for more of any observed HbA1c benefit than direct hormonal modulation. The simulator-relevant point: the glycaemic effect of relaxation training is best modelled as a downward modulation of stress-hormone tone rather than as an autonomous glucose-lowering input.

4. **The single-trial T1D yoga RCT is preliminary.** Maurya et al. (2025) reported a 1.37-percentage-point HbA1c reduction in adolescents after a 3-month adjuvant yoga programme ([Maurya et al., 2025](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40518537/)). The study has substantial methodological limits flagged by its authors: completer analysis (n = 46 of 92 randomised), high COVID-related attrition, unblinded design, and no biomarker corroboration (no cortisol, HRV, or continuous glucose data reported). The point estimate is implausibly large compared with T2D meta-analytic estimates and should be regarded as hypothesis-generating until replication.

5. **Cardiac autonomic baseline is reduced in T1D.** Even at early disease stages, T1D patients exhibit reduced HF-HRV and impaired vagal heart-rate modulation, with cardiac autonomic neuropathy prevalence reaching ~35% in long-standing disease ([Bekkering et al., 2022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35846022/)). The ceiling on vagally-mediated effects of slow breathing or yoga is therefore lower in T1D than in healthy controls, and likely lower still in patients with established autonomic neuropathy. This biological substrate is rarely controlled for in T1D intervention trials.

6. **Indication during high-stress periods.** Acute stressors — infection, surgery, examinations, sleep deprivation — drive transient elevations in cortisol, catecholamines, and growth hormone that produce well-documented stress hyperglycaemia. Relaxation protocols are mechanistically best positioned during these windows, where the counterregulatory burden is high and a transient reduction in the catecholamine/cortisol axis can yield clinically detectable glycaemic improvement.

Cortisol and catecholamine modulation is implemented through a generic stress-input parameter (see [§Stress hormones in MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#stress-hormones)); decreases in that parameter therefore approximate the indirect glycaemic effect of relaxation training but do not represent any practice-specific mechanism.*
