# Part 5: Hormones and Circadian Rhythm

<a name="counterregulatory-hormones"></a>
## 11. Counterregulatory hormones — the body's defense against hypoglycemia

Plasma glucose homeostasis is maintained within a narrow physiologic range (roughly 3.9–7.8 mmol/L in non-diabetic adults) by the opposing actions of insulin and a coordinated set of glucose-raising hormones. Because the brain is an obligate glucose consumer that cannot synthesize or store meaningful glucose and cannot use free fatty acids directly, even brief or modest hypoglycemia threatens central nervous system function and ultimately survival. Evolution has therefore endowed humans with a redundant, hierarchically activated counterregulatory system: as plasma glucose declines, insulin secretion is suppressed first, then glucagon and adrenaline are released as the rapid first-and-second lines of defense, while cortisol and growth hormone provide slower secondary support. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), each of these defenses is partially or completely compromised — a fact that explains why iatrogenic hypoglycemia, rather than hyperglycemia, is the rate-limiting factor in glycaemic management of T1D ([Cryer, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12107742/); [Cryer, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23883381/)).

### 11.1 The Cryer hierarchy: glycaemic thresholds and the three lines of defense

The canonical framework for counterregulation was established by Philip Cryer and collaborators across stepped hyperinsulinaemic glucose-clamp studies in the 1980s and refined by [Mitrakou et al. (1991)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1987794/) and the systematic review by [Verhulst et al. (2022)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/). The hierarchy is:

1. **Decreased insulin secretion** — first line of defense. As plasma glucose falls into the high-normal range (~4.4–4.7 mmol/L), pancreatic β-cells reduce insulin output. This is the only physiological defense that fails completely at T1D diagnosis: exogenous insulin cannot "switch off."
2. **Increased glucagon secretion** — second line of defense. In healthy adults, glucagon rises at a glucose threshold of approximately 3.6–3.9 mmol/L (mean 3.8 mmol/L; [Verhulst 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/)). Onset is rapid (within 2–5 minutes of a glucose dip) and the dominant mechanism is hepatic glycogenolysis with secondary stimulation of gluconeogenesis. Glucagon alone can transiently increase hepatic glucose output 3–5-fold.
3. **Increased adrenomedullary epinephrine secretion** — third line of defense, becoming critical when glucagon is absent. The threshold is similar to glucagon (~3.4 mmol/L in healthy adults; rises to ~3.8 mmol/L in long-standing T1D, indicating attenuation) ([Mitrakou 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1987794/); [Verhulst 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/)).

Below this hormonal hierarchy lie symptomatic and behavioural defenses: autonomic warning symptoms (tremor, palpitations, sweating, anxiety) appear at ~3.4 mmol/L, and neuroglycopenic symptoms and frank cognitive dysfunction appear at ~2.7–2.8 mmol/L ([Mitrakou 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1987794/), n=10 healthy volunteers, hyperinsulinaemic stepped clamp). Cortisol and growth hormone rise at slightly lower thresholds (~2.8–3.2 mmol/L) but their pharmacodynamic effects are too slow to contribute to acute recovery (see §11.5). The crucial physiological inference is that hormonal counterregulation is *prophylactic*: it activates before symptoms emerge so brain glucose supply is protected before consciousness is threatened.

### 11.2 Glucagon and the paracrine biology of the pancreatic islet

Glucagon, a 29-amino-acid peptide secreted by α-cells of the islet of Langerhans, is the single most important acute glucose-raising hormone in non-diabetic humans. The classic dual-blockade study by [Rizza, Cryer, Haymond and Gerich (1979)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36413/) showed in healthy adults (n=12) that combined α- and β-adrenergic blockade alone did not impair glucose recovery from insulin-induced hypoglycemia, but somatostatin-induced glucagon suppression did, and combined glucagon-plus-adrenergic blockade nearly abolished recovery — establishing glucagon as the primary driver and adrenaline as the principal back-up.

The α-cell is not a freestanding glucose sensor. It sits embedded in the islet, where it is exposed to a locally extreme paracrine and juxtacrine environment from neighbouring β- and δ-cells. β-cells comprise ~60–80% of islet endocrine cells and, in human islets, are *intermingled* with α- and δ-cells rather than confined to a strict core as in rodents ([Brissova et al., 2005](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2493924/) — autopsy study, n=14 non-diabetic donors of mixed European ancestry, laser-scanning confocal microscopy; results may not generalise to all populations). This intermingled architecture amplifies paracrine cross-talk:

- **Insulin** is released from β-cells at concentrations 50–100× higher in islet capillary blood than in the systemic circulation ([Unger & Orci, 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2941311/)). At these concentrations insulin binds α-cell insulin receptors and tonically suppresses glucagon secretion.
- **GABA** (γ-aminobutyric acid) is co-stored in β-cell secretory granules and activates GABA<sub>A</sub> receptors on α-cells, hyperpolarising them and inhibiting exocytosis ([Gromada et al., 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17213323/)).
- **Zinc** ions co-released with insulin from β-cell granules close α-cell K<sub>ATP</sub> (ATP-sensitive potassium channels governing α-cell membrane potential and exocytosis)-independent channels and contribute to glucagon suppression at high β-cell activity. (The relative contributions of insulin, GABA and zinc remain debated; Unger & Orci 2010 emphasise insulin, while Gromada 2007 weights GABA and zinc more heavily.)
- **Somatostatin** from δ-cells acts as a tonic brake on both α- and β-cells, providing fine local damping.

Unger and Orci (2010) framed this as a "Sherringtonian" (mutual inhibition between adjacent cell populations, by analogy with Sherrington's spinal motor inhibition) reciprocal arrangement: high β-cell activity actively *inhibits* α-cells, and falling β-cell activity removes that inhibition — the so-called **"switch-off hypothesis"**. In healthy humans, falling glucose causes β-cell insulin (and GABA, zinc) output to drop; the resulting *rate of decrease* in local insulin paracrine tone is itself a primary stimulus that releases α-cells to secrete glucagon. Direct α-cell glucose sensing (via K<sub>ATP</sub> channels and metabolism) contributes but is now thought to be secondary to this paracrine switch-off signal ([Gromada et al., 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17213323/)).

### 11.3 Why the glucagon defense fails in T1D

In T1D, β-cell mass is reduced by 70–95% at diagnosis and approaches zero in C-peptide-negative long-standing disease. α-cells, in contrast, *survive* the autoimmune attack ([Brissova 2005](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2493924/)) and even hypertrophy in some studies. They retain the molecular machinery to produce and release glucagon, and they continue to respond to non-glucose stimuli — most importantly to amino acids (arginine, alanine), as Gerich demonstrated in his landmark 1973 *Science* paper ([Gerich et al., 1973](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/4581053/)). What they lose is the ability to mount a glucose-specific glucagon response to falling plasma glucose.

The mechanism is most parsimoniously explained by loss of paracrine input. Without juxtaposed functioning β-cells, α-cells lack (i) the tonic insulin/GABA/zinc suppression that "primes" the switch-off response, and (ii) the falling local insulin gradient that physiologically signals hypoglycemia from within the islet.

The clinical correlate is striking and quantitatively well documented. In healthy adults the glucagon increment during hypoglycemia averages 100–200 pg/mL above baseline. In T1D the glucagon response to hypoglycemia is *progressively attenuated* with disease duration and is **almost universally lost within ~5 years of diagnosis** ([Verhulst et al., 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/)) — though the precise pace varies between individuals and is influenced by residual C-peptide. A second paradox arises from the same paracrine deficit: because tonic suppression is gone, T1D α-cells *over-secrete* glucagon postprandially in response to dietary amino acids, contributing to post-meal hyperglycemia even when prandial insulin is delivered ([Unger & Orci, 2010](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2941311/)). Thus T1D patients have *too much* glucagon when they need none and *too little* glucagon when they need it most.

### 11.4 Adrenaline, autonomic symptoms, and hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF)

When the glucagon response is absent, the adrenomedullary epinephrine response becomes the de facto primary hormonal defense. Epinephrine raises plasma glucose by stimulating hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, suppressing endogenous insulin (irrelevant in C-peptide-negative T1D), inhibiting muscle glucose uptake, and stimulating lipolysis (which provides glycerol as a gluconeogenic substrate and FFAs that feed back to suppress muscle glucose uptake). In healthy adults the epinephrine threshold is ~3.4 mmol/L, with a circulating half-life of only 1–3 minutes; effects on glucose flux are detectable within 5–10 minutes.

The same sympathoadrenal response that drives hepatic glucose production also generates the warning symptoms — palpitations, tremor, sweating, anxiety, hunger — that prompt patients to recognise and treat hypoglycemia. Symptom thresholds in healthy controls (~3.4 mmol/L for autonomic, ~2.8 mmol/L for neuroglycopenic; [Mitrakou 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1987794/)) lie *below* the hormonal thresholds, ensuring physiological defense is engaged before behavioural defense is needed.

In T1D, this final defensive layer is itself compromised by **hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF)**, a syndrome characterised by Cryer and colleagues across two decades of clamp studies ([Cryer, 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16306382/); [Cryer, 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23883381/)). HAAF has two components: (1) **defective glucose counterregulation** — the epinephrine response to a given hypoglycemic stimulus is attenuated in amplitude and shifted to lower glucose thresholds (pooled adrenaline threshold rises from ~3.4 mmol/L in healthy controls to ~3.8 mmol/L in T1D; [Verhulst 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/)); (2) **hypoglycemia unawareness** — attenuated sympathetic neural and partly adrenomedullary responses blunt warning symptoms, so patients do not perceive hypoglycemia until cognitive function is already impaired. Estimated prevalence is 20–25% of adults with T1D, rising sharply with disease duration and recurrent hypoglycemia.

The triggering event is **antecedent hypoglycemia** itself: a single episode of hypoglycemia in healthy volunteers blunts both hormonal and symptom responses to a subsequent episode 24 hours later ([Heller & Cryer, 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2010052/), n=15 non-diabetic adults). Repeated cycles produce a vicious loop: hypoglycemia begets hypoglycemia. Sleep and antecedent moderate exercise produce similar HAAF-like attenuation. The mechanism likely involves central (hypothalamic) adaptation, with hypothesised mediators including elevated brain glucose transport, opioid signalling, and altered hypothalamic glucose sensing — all unresolved (caveat: most mechanistic work is rodent or healthy-volunteer; T1D-specific human mechanistic data are sparse and largely correlational). Critically, HAAF is **largely reversible by 2–3 weeks of scrupulous hypoglycemia avoidance** ([Cryer 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23883381/); [Fanelli et al., 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8243826/), n=21 short-duration T1D patients), which is the clinical rationale for raising glucose targets temporarily in patients with hypoglycemia unawareness.

### 11.5 Cortisol and growth hormone — the slow secondary defenses

Cortisol and growth hormone (GH) rise during hypoglycemia at thresholds slightly below glucagon and adrenaline (cortisol ~2.8 mmol/L in healthy, ~3.5 mmol/L in T1D; GH ~3.0–3.2 mmol/L; [Verhulst 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/)). Both hormones are insulin-antagonistic, but their pharmacodynamics make them irrelevant for *acute* counterregulation. Cortisol acts via the glucocorticoid receptor (a nuclear transcription factor); genomic effects on hepatic gluconeogenic enzyme transcription (PEPCK, glucose-6-phosphatase) and on adipose lipolysis require 1–2 hours to emerge and persist for 8–12 hours ([De Feo et al., 1989](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2665516/)). Cortisol also induces insulin resistance in muscle and adipose. Its primary contribution is therefore to *prolonged* hypoglycemia (>2 h) and to the recovery phase, not to first-hour rescue. Growth hormone stimulates lipolysis (raising FFAs, which suppress muscle glucose uptake via the Randle cycle), reduces muscle glucose uptake directly, and modestly increases hepatic glucose production; like cortisol, GH acts with a 2–4 h delay through IGF-1 and direct receptor signalling.

In healthy adults, selective deficiency of either hormone alone has minimal effect on acute recovery from insulin-induced hypoglycemia, but combined deficiency (e.g. in panhypopituitarism) impairs late-phase glucose recovery and predisposes to fasting hypoglycemia ([Bolli et al., 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6376870/), case series of pituitary patients). In T1D, cortisol and GH responses are generally preserved, although their thresholds are shifted leftward by HAAF.

### 11.6 Quantitative summary of counterregulatory hormone parameters

The numerical values below synthesise [Mitrakou 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1987794/), [Schwartz et al. 1987](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC424197/), [Verhulst 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/), [Rizza 1979](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36413/), and [Gromada 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17213323/). Thresholds are arterialised-venous plasma glucose, hyperinsulinaemic stepped-clamp, healthy adult volunteers unless stated; ranges reflect inter-study variability. Values shift in T1D due to HAAF (see §11.4).

| Hormone | Glucose threshold (healthy) | Glucose threshold (T1D pooled) | Time to peak | Plasma half-life | Onset of glucose-raising effect | Primary mechanism |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Glucagon | 3.6–3.9 mmol/L | Response largely absent within ~5 yr of diagnosis | 5–10 min | 4–6 min | 2–5 min | Hepatic glycogenolysis + gluconeogenesis (cAMP–PKA) |
| Adrenaline | 3.3–3.6 mmol/L | ~3.8 mmol/L (attenuated amplitude) | 5–15 min | 1–3 min | 5–10 min | Hepatic glycogenolysis, lipolysis, ↓ muscle glucose uptake (β-adrenergic) |
| Cortisol | 2.8–3.2 mmol/L | ~3.5 mmol/L | 30–60 min | 60–90 min | 1–2 h (genomic) | ↑ Gluconeogenesis, insulin resistance, permissive for catecholamines |
| Growth hormone | 3.0–3.3 mmol/L | ~3.8 mmol/L | 30–60 min | 15–20 min | 2–4 h | ↑ Lipolysis (FFA → ↓ muscle glucose uptake), ↑ hepatic glucose production |
| Autonomic symptoms | ~3.4 mmol/L | ~3.4 mmol/L (blunted in unawareness) | — | — | — | Sympathoadrenal activation (tremor, sweating, palpitations, hunger) |
| Neuroglycopenic Sx / cognitive dysfunction | ~2.7–2.8 mmol/L | ~3.0 mmol/L | — | — | — | Direct cerebral glucose deprivation |

### 11.7 Integration and clinical implications

The composite picture in long-standing T1D is sobering. The first defense (insulin suppression) is structurally absent because exogenous insulin is administered and cannot be withdrawn in real time. The second defense (glucagon) is functionally lost within a few years of diagnosis through paracrine deafferentation of α-cells. The third defense (adrenaline) is progressively attenuated by HAAF in patients exposed to recurrent hypoglycemia. Cortisol and GH, even when intact, are pharmacodynamically incapable of preventing acute hypoglycemia because their effects emerge only after 1–4 hours. The patient is thus left dependent on behavioural defense — perceiving symptoms and ingesting carbohydrate — which is itself blunted by hypoglycemia unawareness.

Three clinical inferences follow directly. First, recurrent hypoglycemia is self-perpetuating; each episode lowers the threshold for the next, so any sustained reduction in episode frequency (typically targeted via glucose floors of 5–10 mmol/L for 2–3 weeks) can restore symptom awareness in most affected patients. Second, CGM-driven hypoglycemia avoidance is mechanistically *disease-modifying*, not merely symptomatic — by interrupting the antecedent-hypoglycemia → HAAF cycle. Third, glucagon emergency therapy is rational because the molecular target (the hepatic glucagon receptor) is intact in T1D; what is lost is endogenous secretion, not target sensitivity.

### 11.8 Controversies and uncertainties

Several tensions in the field remain unresolved and should be flagged when this physiology is invoked quantitatively. The **relative contribution of insulin vs GABA vs zinc to α-cell paracrine suppression** is contested: Unger & Orci (2010) emphasise insulin; Gromada et al. (2007) and Rorsman's group emphasise GABA and zinc; recent optogenetic work in mouse islets supports a multi-factor model. The "switch-off" mechanism is therefore better viewed as a *system property* of intact β-cell juxtaposition than as a single signal. The balance between **direct α-cell glucose sensing and paracrine sensing** is unsettled — α-cells express K<sub>ATP</sub> channels and metabolise glucose, suggesting some intrinsic capacity, but their behaviour in dispersed culture differs sharply from in-islet behaviour, and whether intact T1D α-cells retain any preserved direct glucose sensing in vivo is unknown. The **mechanism of HAAF** remains hypothetical: cortisol-mediated central adaptation, opioid signalling, increased brain glucose transport and altered hypothalamic glucose sensing have all been proposed but none is definitively established. **Threshold variability** is large: pooled thresholds in [Verhulst 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9477942/) carry inter-quartile ranges of ~0.6–1.0 mmol/L and describe population behaviour, not individual responses; individual thresholds shift with antecedent glycaemia, antecedent exercise, sleep, sex (women have somewhat blunted responses), age (children retain partially preserved glucagon longer), and pregnancy. Finally, **most threshold data come from White European or North American adult cohorts**; pediatric, pregnant, elderly, and non-European populations are under-studied, and quantitative thresholds should not be assumed identical.

<a name="dawn-phenomenon"></a>
## 12. The dawn phenomenon — pre-awakening glucose rise

The dawn phenomenon is a circadian rise in fasting plasma glucose and in the insulin requirement to maintain euglycaemia, occurring during the late nocturnal and pre-awakening hours (approximately 03:00–08:00) in the absence of intervening hypoglycaemia, food intake, or waning exogenous insulin. It was first described in T1D by Schmidt and colleagues, who reported a reproducible night-to-morning blood glucose elevation that was not preceded by a nocturnal nadir below 3.5 mmol/L and therefore could not be attributed to counter-regulatory rebound (Somogyi effect; see §13) ([Schmidt et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7318489/)). Bolli and Gerich subsequently demonstrated that an analogous, but quantitatively much smaller, dawn rise in endogenous glucose production (EGP) and insulin secretion occurs in healthy non-diabetic adults — establishing the phenomenon as a normal physiological adaptation to the awakening state, rather than a diabetes-specific abnormality ([Bolli et al., 1984 — *Diabetes* 33:1150–1153, n = 8 healthy volunteers, half-hourly sampling 01:00–09:00](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6389230/)). The clinical relevance for T1D arises because endogenous beta-cell compensation is absent: the pre-dawn rise in hepatic insulin requirement is unmet, and fasting morning hyperglycaemia, post-breakfast excursions, and the empirical need for a higher breakfast insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio follow directly. Recent CGM analyses suggest that approximately 50% of well-treated T1D pump users show a clear dawn phenomenon when defined by a rise in basal insulin requirement of more than 20% sustained for at least 90 minutes ([Carlson et al., 2019 — IQR 28.8–110.6%, n = 33 T1D pump users](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31630987/)), and the original cohort study by Perriello and colleagues found the phenomenon present in 89% of T1D subjects (101/114) when assessed by overnight Biostator-controlled euglycaemic insulin titration ([Perriello et al., 1991 — *Diabetologia*](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2055337/)).

### Hormonal drivers — cortisol, growth hormone, and the awakening pulse

Two hormone systems with distinct circadian profiles converge in the pre-awakening hours and together produce the dawn signal.

**Cortisol** follows a robust ACTH-driven circadian rhythm anchored to the suprachiasmatic nucleus. Plasma cortisol reaches its nocturnal nadir near sleep onset (around 23:00–01:00, ~50–80 nmol/L) and rises through the second half of the night to a peak between approximately 06:00 and 08:00 (~400–550 nmol/L in healthy adults), with the steepest rate of rise in the final two hours before awakening; the cortisol awakening response adds a further short-lived 38–75% increment in the first 30–45 minutes after waking ([Pruessner et al., 1997 — cited in §11; healthy cohorts](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9415946/)). Cortisol acts genomically through the glucocorticoid receptor on glucocorticoid-response elements in *PCK1* and *G6PC*, upregulating phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase and glucose-6-phosphatase and increasing hepatic gluconeogenic capacity over a 30–120 min window (see §2). It also induces post-receptor peripheral insulin resistance and is permissive for catecholamine and glucagon action. Bolli's original work in healthy controls showed that plasma cortisol, epinephrine, and norepinephrine all rose significantly from their nocturnal nadirs between 04:00 and 06:30, preceding by roughly 30–60 minutes the measurable rise in glucose production ([Bolli et al., 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6389230/)) — temporally consistent with cortisol-mediated transcriptional priming of hepatic gluconeogenesis.

**Growth hormone** is secreted in pulses tied to slow-wave sleep (SWS), with the largest pulses concentrated in the first half of the night (typically 23:00–03:00) but with smaller, late-night pulses persisting into the early-morning REM-dominated period in many individuals. GH peaks reach 5–30 ng/mL during pulses (versus inter-pulse troughs near 0.1–0.5 ng/mL); the integrated 24-hour GH output is markedly higher in adolescents than in adults, and is amplified by puberty. GH lowers insulin sensitivity in liver and skeletal muscle through post-receptor IRS-1 serine phosphorylation, increased FFA flux from lipolysis, and induction of hepatic IGF-binding protein responses; the insulin-antagonistic effect lags the GH pulse by 3–5 hours, which places its peak metabolic action squarely in the 04:00–08:00 window even when the inciting pulse occurred earlier in the night ([Møller et al., 1990 — somatostatin GH-suppression abolishes the dawn rise in T1D](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2406181/)). Two converging T1D experiments are mechanistically decisive. Perriello and colleagues showed that suppressing nocturnal GH pulses with somatostatin abolished the pre-dawn rise in insulin requirement in T1D adults, while GH replacement during somatostatin infusion restored it ([Perriello et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2406181/)). Edge and colleagues demonstrated in pubertal T1D adolescents that the magnitude of the dawn rise in pre-breakfast insulin demand correlated with mean overnight GH concentration, and that pharmacological GH suppression reduced the pre-breakfast insulin requirement ([Edge et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2096008/)). Cortisol and GH thus contribute through distinct, non-redundant pathways — cortisol via gluconeogenic transcription and permissive action, GH via delayed peripheral and hepatic insulin resistance — and explain why the dawn phenomenon is most pronounced during adolescence, when GH output is highest, and is reduced (but not abolished) in older T1D patients ([Perriello et al., 1991 — inverse correlation with diabetes duration, r = −0.72](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2055337/)).

### Hepatic insulin resistance and the rise in EGP

The mechanistic core of the dawn phenomenon is a transient increase in hepatic glucose output driven by reduced hepatic insulin sensitivity. In healthy controls, EGP rises by approximately 30–50% above its pre-dawn nadir between 05:00 and 08:00, and is matched by a parallel rise in endogenous insulin secretion (C-peptide rises significantly after 05:30) so that fasting glucose increases only modestly (typically ≤0.5–1.0 mmol/L) ([Bolli et al., 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6389230/)). In T1D, the hepatic insulin demand rises by a similar 30–60% — Perriello reported a mean ~33% rise in intravenous insulin infusion required to maintain euglycaemia, from ~0.102 mU·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ at 02:40 to ~0.135 mU·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ at 06:40 ([Perriello et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2055337/)) — but, in the absence of adaptive endogenous insulin secretion, the unmet demand translates directly into fasting hyperglycaemia. Both glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis contribute: cortisol-driven transcriptional upregulation of *PCK1* and *G6PC* primarily augments gluconeogenesis, while GH-driven lipolysis raises FFA flux and substrate-couples gluconeogenesis through increased acetyl-CoA. The net hepatic effect is consistent across studies regardless of insulin-treatment modality, but the *manifest* glucose rise depends critically on the overnight insulin-delivery profile (see below).

### Peripheral insulin resistance

GH-mediated reduction in skeletal-muscle insulin sensitivity continues into the post-breakfast period and is the dominant mechanism behind the higher breakfast insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio observed in many T1D patients. Muscle glucose uptake during euglycaemic-hyperinsulinaemic clamping is reduced by approximately 20–40% in the early-morning window compared with the late-evening window in T1D adults; the reduction is larger in adolescents, in whom GH output is greater ([Carroll & Schade, 2005 — review](https://www.endocrinepractice.org/article/S1530-891X(20)39957-2/fulltext)). Some, but not all, controlled studies report breakfast insulin requirements 20–40% above lunch and dinner requirements at the same carbohydrate load; the magnitude is heterogeneous between individuals and across study protocols, and the diurnal-ISF literature is summarised in §14. In the dawn-phenomenon literature specifically, the empirical recommendation to magnify the breakfast bolus or the ICR (insulin-to-carbohydrate ratio, units of insulin per gram of carbohydrate) is best understood as the combined contribution of (a) ongoing post-dawn hepatic insulin resistance, (b) GH-driven skeletal-muscle insulin resistance, and (c) prolonged FFA-mediated effects from the overnight lipolysis surge.

### Magnitude — healthy versus T1D, and the "extended dawn" / "dawn-of-the-dawn" phenomenon

The healthy dawn signal is small: fasting glucose rises typically by ≤1 mmol/L, and the rise is contained by the parallel insulin-secretion response. In T1D, the manifest pre-breakfast glucose rise typically falls in the 1–3 mmol/L range when residual basal insulin coverage is adequate, and rises substantially with insulin-delivery regimens that wane overnight: Porcellati and colleagues reported a fasting BG increase of ~3 mmol/L (55 mg/dL) on evening NPH-insulin regimens but only ~0.9–1.4 mmol/L (15–25 mg/dL) on continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII) or evening insulin glargine, in matched T1D cohorts ([Porcellati et al., 2013 — *Diabetes Care*; PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836156/)). In T2D, where some endogenous insulin secretion typically persists, the median fasting BG rise quantified by CGM is ~1.0 mmol/L (16 mg/dL) and the phenomenon affects approximately half of all patients ([Monnier et al., 2013 — *Diabetes Care*, n = 248 non-insulin-treated T2D, CGM](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24170753/); [Monnier et al., 2012](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3507613/)).

A separate phenomenon must be distinguished. The classical dawn phenomenon refers to the pre-breakfast rise in hepatic insulin demand driven by endogenous hormonal mechanisms and is anchored to the awakening transition. Carroll and Schade introduced the term **"extended dawn phenomenon"** (sometimes informally "dawn-of-the-dawn") to describe a *post-breakfast prolongation* of hyperglycaemia in which the morning meal's glycaemic excursion is exaggerated and prolonged by the residual hormonal milieu ([Carroll & Schade, 2005](https://www.endocrinepractice.org/article/S1530-891X(20)39957-2/fulltext)). The mechanism is the persistence of GH-driven peripheral insulin resistance, ongoing FFA flux, and slowed hepatic glucose disposal during the first 1–3 hours after breakfast, producing a higher and later post-breakfast peak than is seen after lunch or dinner with comparable carbohydrate loads. Quantitatively, post-breakfast peak glucose has been reported at 191–208 mg/dL (10.6–11.6 mmol/L) versus lower peaks after lunch and dinner in T2D cohorts on identical meals ([Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836156/)). The extended dawn phenomenon explains why a pre-breakfast bolus correction alone is often insufficient: even after fasting glucose is normalised, the breakfast bolus dose and timing must independently account for reduced post-breakfast insulin sensitivity. Caveat: some authors use "dawn phenomenon" inclusively to encompass both the pre- and post-breakfast components, and the literature is not uniform on terminology — readers should attend to whether a quoted magnitude is fasting-only or includes the post-breakfast tail.

### Clinical implications

The dawn phenomenon is a normal physiological process exposed by absent endogenous insulin compensation; it is not a behavioural failure or a sign of inadequate evening dosing in the absolute sense, but it does identify a window where the evening basal-insulin profile is mismatched to the patient's overnight insulin-demand curve. Three management consequences follow. First, basal-insulin pharmacokinetics matter: regimens with peaked overnight kinetics that wane before dawn (NPH given at supper, twice-daily premixed insulins) systematically expose the dawn rise, whereas CSII with a programmed pre-dawn basal increase, or long-acting analogues with flat 24-hour profiles (glargine U-100/U-300, degludec), substantially attenuate the manifest fasting rise ([Porcellati et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3836156/)). Second, dawn-tailored basal programming on CSII typically increases the basal rate from approximately 03:00 by 30–60% over the nocturnal nadir to peak between 05:00 and 07:00, returning to daytime baseline by mid-morning; the clinically observed dawn-window timing in pump users centres on a 03:00 onset with interquartile range 03:00–04:15 ([Carlson et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31630987/)). Third, because the extended dawn component is independent of fasting BG, breakfast bolus strategy (higher ICR, longer pre-bolus, or split bolus for high-fat/protein meals) must be evaluated separately from the overnight basal — and a patient whose fasting BG is well-controlled may still need a magnified breakfast bolus.

CGM has transformed the differential diagnosis of morning hyperglycaemia. A gradual, monotonic rise from a stable nocturnal plateau beginning at ~03:00 is characteristic of dawn phenomenon; a fall to <3.5 mmol/L overnight followed by a rebound to hyperglycaemia is the Somogyi pattern (see §13); and a flat or rising trace from bedtime onward without a clear pre-dawn inflection points to insufficient basal insulin rather than dawn-driven hyperglycaemia. CGM-based prevalence estimates in T1D range from approximately 50% (well-treated pump cohorts, strict thresholds) to approaching 90% (overnight Biostator titration, looser thresholds) — the discrepancy reflecting threshold and methodology rather than true biological variability ([Carlson et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31630987/); [Perriello et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2055337/)).

### Controversies and limitations

Three issues remain unsettled. (i) The relative contributions of cortisol and GH to the manifest T1D dawn signal are debated. Selective somatostatin GH-suppression studies make GH the dominant pulsatile driver in adolescent and adult T1D, but cortisol's contribution is harder to isolate experimentally because adrenocortical suppression with metyrapone introduces confounding compensatory ACTH responses; the consensus position is that GH provides the proximate insulin-resistance trigger while cortisol provides permissive transcriptional priming. (ii) Diurnal duration. Whether a true "dusk phenomenon" — a smaller pre-dinner rise in hepatic insulin demand — exists as a separate physiological entity is contested; the §14 evidence on diurnal ISF variation suggests it is more likely a continuum of circadian insulin-sensitivity variation than a discrete dawn-mirroring event. (iii) Reproducibility. Within-patient night-to-night variability in dawn-phenomenon magnitude is substantial (coefficient of variation reported at 40–60%), driven by sleep architecture, prior-day exercise, evening meal composition, and variability in nocturnal GH-pulse timing — consequently a single night of CGM data is insufficient to characterise an individual's dawn profile, and 5–7 nights are typically needed for a stable estimate.

<a name="somogyi-effect"></a>
## 13. The Somogyi effect — rebound hyperglycemia after nocturnal hypoglycemia

The Somogyi effect (also called Somogyi phenomenon, posthypoglycemic hyperglycemia, or chronic Somogyi rebound) is the proposed clinical pattern in which an undetected nocturnal hypoglycemic episode triggers a robust counterregulatory hormone response that drives blood glucose to hyperglycemic levels by morning, producing fasting hyperglycemia despite — or because of — adequate or excessive nighttime insulin. The hypothesis was articulated in its mature form by Michael Somogyi in *Diabetogenic effect of hyperinsulinism* ([Somogyi, 1959](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13617276/)) after almost two decades of clinical observation, and it became a foundational teaching in diabetology: if a patient with type 1 diabetes (T1D) wakes with elevated glucose, the clinician must consider that the cause is too much insulin, not too little. Modern continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) has substantially weakened that teaching. The phenomenon is physiologically possible and occasionally documented, but population-scale CGM data show that fasting hyperglycemia in T1D is far more often produced by the dawn phenomenon, by waning basal insulin during the second half of the night, or by simple insulin underdosing — not by an unrecognized hypoglycemic rebound.

This section describes the classical mechanism, the historical evidence base on which it rested, the modern CGM-era re-evaluation that has reframed it as uncommon in routine practice, and the clinical decision rules that follow. The framing is deliberately critical: the Somogyi hypothesis is one of the most cited yet weakest pillars of historical diabetes teaching, and its inappropriate application — reducing nocturnal insulin to "treat" suspected rebounds that are actually waning basal — has been documented to cause real harm.

### 13.1 Classical mechanism: counterregulation as a glucose-raising engine

The proposed sequence is biologically plausible and rests on well-characterized counterregulatory physiology (see §10 for the underlying hormonal cascade). When plasma glucose falls below approximately 3.6–3.9 mmol/L, suppression of insulin secretion fails in T1D (no endogenous β-cell capacity remains), and four counterregulatory pathways activate: glucagon secretion (typically deficient or absent in long-duration T1D), epinephrine release, growth hormone (GH) pulses, and a delayed cortisol rise. Adrenaline and GH together can raise hepatic glucose output (HGO) by 100–250% above basal within 20–60 minutes and produce frank insulin resistance in skeletal muscle and liver lasting 6–12 hours after the nadir ([Cryer, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24962915/); see §10.3 for quantitative effects).

The classical Somogyi narrative chains these elements as follows: (1) overnight insulin (basal NPH, glargine, degludec, detemir, or pump basal) overshoots between roughly 02:00 and 04:00, producing an asymptomatic hypoglycemic nadir; (2) counterregulatory hormones surge; (3) HGO rises sharply while peripheral insulin sensitivity falls; (4) plasma glucose rebounds to 12–18 mmol/L by 07:00–08:00; (5) the patient or clinician interprets the high fasting reading as insulin underdosing and increases the bedtime dose, deepening the next night's hypoglycemia and worsening the rebound on subsequent mornings. Somogyi labelled the resulting state "chronic insulin-induced hyperglycemia" and predicted that empirical insulin reduction would paradoxically improve fasting control.

Several quantitative features of this narrative deserve scrutiny. First, the timing requirement: a true Somogyi rebound requires a nadir early enough that counterregulatory hormones have time to act, the post-hypoglycemic insulin resistance has time to manifest, and a measurable hyperglycemic overshoot can develop before the morning capillary check. Second, the magnitude requirement: counterregulation must overshoot by enough to push glucose well above the patient's usual fasting set-point, despite the simultaneous presence of any residual exogenous insulin. Third, the persistence requirement: chronic Somogyi rebound — the form Somogyi himself emphasized — requires that this pattern repeats across consecutive nights and produces a stable elevated fasting trajectory. Each of these requirements is met less often than the qualitative narrative suggests, especially in long-duration T1D where glucagon counterregulation is partially or fully lost ([Cryer, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24962915/)) and the dominant defense against hypoglycemia becomes adrenaline alone — sufficient to terminate the nadir but typically not to drive a sustained overshoot.

### 13.2 Historical evidence: clinical observation, not controlled trials

Somogyi's 1938–1959 case series were uncontrolled, non-blinded, single-center clinical observations on a small number of patients receiving regular soluble insulin (animal-derived; human recombinant insulin did not arrive until the early 1980s). His glucose measurements were intermittent capillary samples; no overnight glucose tracing existed. The dose reductions that "improved" fasting glucose in his patients were applied without controls for regression to the mean, observer bias, or the confounding effect of better daytime glycemic awareness ([González-Vidal et al., 2024](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39718705/)). The hypothesis spread rapidly through clinical teaching despite this thin evidentiary base, in part because the underlying counterregulatory physiology (independently established by Cryer and colleagues from the late 1970s onward) made the mechanism sound mechanistically inevitable.

The first quantitative challenge came from [Gale, Kurtz, and Tattersall (1980)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6105438/), whose *Lancet* study sampled overnight blood glucose, free insulin, GH, cortisol, and glucagon in 17 episodes of asymptomatic nocturnal hypoglycemia in patients with T1D. They found that fasting glucose at 07:00 ranged from 0.7 to 17 mmol/L after these episodes, and — critically — that the patients with apparent rebound hyperglycemia did **not** have higher counterregulatory hormone concentrations than those whose glucose remained low. The dominant correlate of morning glucose was the late-night free insulin level: hyperglycemia appeared when overnight insulin was waning, not when counterregulation was peaking. Their interpretation reframed the morning rise as relative late-night insulin deficiency, with counterregulation playing at most a permissive role.

### 13.3 The Hirsch and Perriello experiments: controlled disconfirmation

Two controlled studies in 1990 effectively settled the experimental question for moderately controlled T1D patients on standard regimens. [Hirsch et al. (1990)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2190769/) studied 10 adults with IDDM in three crossover conditions on otherwise identical regimens: control nights, prevented nocturnal hypoglycemia (intravenous dextrose clamp keeping plasma glucose >5.6 mmol/L), and induced nocturnal hypoglycemia (stepped intravenous insulin lowering plasma glucose to <2.8 mmol/L for ~1 hour). Mean fasting and breakfast plasma glucose after induced hypoglycemia (1.9 ± 0.2 mmol/L nadir) were statistically indistinguishable from the prevention arm. Daytime glucose excursions did not correlate with nocturnal counterregulatory hormone peaks. The investigators concluded that "nocturnal hypoglycemia does not appear to cause clinically important daytime hyperglycemia" in typical IDDM. Perriello and colleagues, working contemporaneously in Perugia, reached compatible conclusions and additionally showed that the dawn phenomenon was driven by late-night GH pulses acting on a substrate of waning insulin — i.e., a mechanism that does **not** require a preceding hypoglycemic episode ([Perriello et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2178966/)).

### 13.4 The CGM era: population-scale disconfirmation

Continuous glucose monitoring transformed the question from "can a rebound occur after an experimentally induced nadir?" to "how often does this pattern actually arise in real life?" Three CGM-era studies are the load-bearing pieces of evidence:

- **[Høi-Hansen, Pedersen-Bjergaard, and Thorsteinsson (2005)](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00125-005-1946-5)** analyzed 594 nights of CGM in T1D adults during routine living, capturing 218 episodes of asymptomatic nocturnal hypoglycemia (CGM <3.5 mmol/L). Mean morning glucose was **lower**, not higher, after hypoglycemic nights compared with non-hypoglycemic nights. A fasting capillary glucose <7 mmol/L was a positive predictor of preceding nocturnal hypoglycemia, the opposite of what the Somogyi hypothesis would imply.
- **[Guillod et al. (2007)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17652003/)**, *Diabetes Metab*, used CGM in T1D outpatients on multiple daily injections and pump therapy and found a similar pattern: hypoglycemic nights were followed by lower, not higher, fasting glucose, with only a small minority of episodes producing measurable rebound above the patient's usual fasting range.
- **[Choudhary et al. (2013)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23672623/)** analyzed CGM data from 89 T1D patients in the UK Hypoglycaemia Study. Capillary fasting glucose was again **lower** following nights with hypoglycemia. Only 2 episodes across the dataset were followed by fasting glucose >10 mmol/L. The authors titled the paper bluntly — *The Somogyi effect — more fiction than fact?* — and argued that a low fasting glucose is a more reliable indicator of preceding nocturnal hypoglycemia than a high one. Pediatric CGM cohorts (e.g. [Bachran et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22487548/), and the broader pediatric prevalence literature) reach equivalent conclusions: nocturnal hypoglycemia is common (20–50% of nights in some series), but morning hyperglycemia following such episodes is the exception, not the rule.

The convergent finding across these CGM studies is that the **modal** post-hypoglycemic-night morning glucose is normal-to-low, the **median** is at or below the usual fasting level, and only a long thin tail of episodes (probably fewer than 5–10% of confirmed nocturnal hypoglycemias) produces the textbook rebound pattern. Inter-individual variability is large — a small subset of patients does appear to mount a vigorous counterregulatory response with overshoot — but the population-level signal is the opposite of what classical teaching predicts.

### 13.5 Distinguishing Somogyi, dawn phenomenon, and waning basal

Three distinct overnight glucose trajectories produce fasting hyperglycemia in T1D, and CGM allows them to be separated cleanly:

| Pattern | Overnight CGM trace | Mechanism | Clinical action |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Dawn phenomenon** (§12) | Stable through 03:00, gradual rise from ~04:00 to wake | GH/cortisol-driven hepatic insulin resistance; no preceding nadir | Increase pre-dawn basal coverage; consider pump basal profile or degludec/glargine timing |
| **Waning basal** | Steady rise from bedtime onward, often starting before midnight | Insulin pharmacokinetics insufficient through the full night | Switch to longer-duration basal (degludec U100/U200), split NPH dosing, or adjust pump basal |
| **Somogyi rebound** | Distinct nadir <3.5 mmol/L in first half of night, then rapid rise to >10 mmol/L by morning | Counterregulatory overshoot after hypoglycemic episode | Reduce overnight insulin; investigate cause of nadir (timing, dose, exercise, alcohol) |

The clinical implication is that empirical insulin reduction in response to fasting hyperglycemia — the action prescribed by Somogyi and embedded in older diabetes teaching — is the correct response in only one of these three scenarios, and is the wrong response in the other two. CGM (or, when CGM is unavailable, structured 03:00 capillary checks over several nights) is required to distinguish them.

### 13.6 Controversies and current consensus

The position now broadly accepted in the modern T1D literature ([StatPearls — Somogyi Phenomenon](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK551525/); [González-Vidal et al., 2024](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39718705/)) can be summarized as follows:

- The **counterregulatory mechanism is real**: hypoglycemia activates adrenaline, GH, and cortisol, and these hormones produce hepatic and peripheral insulin resistance lasting hours. The biology Somogyi inferred is correct.
- The **clinical pattern of rebound to morning hyperglycemia is uncommon** in modern T1D care: most nocturnal hypoglycemic episodes are followed by morning glucose at or below the patient's typical fasting level.
- The **inter-individual variability is large** (Gale, 1980; Choudhary, 2013): a minority of patients do show overshoot, possibly more often in those with intact glucagon counterregulation, in pediatric T1D, and in the early years after diagnosis.
- The **default differential diagnosis for fasting hyperglycemia in T1D should now be**, in order of frequency: insufficient overnight basal coverage, dawn phenomenon, evening insulin underdosing or missed bolus for late carbohydrates, and only then Somogyi rebound. Empirical reduction of overnight insulin without CGM evidence of a preceding nadir is no longer recommended.
- The **persistence of Somogyi teaching** despite weak evidence — documented in survey studies of clinicians and patient-facing materials well into the 2010s — is itself a case study in how mechanistically attractive but evidentially thin hypotheses propagate in clinical practice.

The phrase "the Somogyi effect" is therefore best understood today as describing a **physiologically possible but clinically uncommon** trajectory whose historical importance lies less in its frequency than in the cautionary tale it provides: a plausible mechanism, asserted from uncontrolled observation, embedded in textbooks for half a century, and substantially overturned only when continuous glucose data became routinely available.

A residual area of legitimate disagreement concerns the **subgroups** in which classical Somogyi rebound may still be clinically relevant. Three populations are plausibly enriched for the phenomenon. (i) Young children with T1D, who retain robust glucagon counterregulation and typically lower hypoglycemia thresholds for adrenaline release, and in whom basal pump rates are small enough that small absolute errors produce large relative overdoses ([Bachran et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22487548/)). (ii) Newly diagnosed T1D patients in the partial-remission ("honeymoon") phase, where residual β-cell function and intact counterregulation may both be preserved. (iii) Patients with HAAF (hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure; see §11) who have undergone a recent first hypoglycemic episode after a long period of avoidance — although in this group the typical pattern is impaired counterregulation and prolonged hypoglycemia, not overshoot. None of these subgroups have been characterized with the rigor of the adult cohorts in Hirsch (1990) or Choudhary (2013), and pediatric CGM studies have generally found the same pattern of low-not-high morning glucose after nocturnal nadirs. The honest summary is that the Somogyi effect, where it exists at all, exists at the tail of the distribution and not in the middle — and CGM is the only practical tool that can identify the individual patients in whom it does.

<a name="diurnal-variation-isf"></a>
## 14. Diurnal variation in insulin sensitivity

The dawn phenomenon (§12) is the *acute* pre-waking pulse of cortisol and growth hormone that raises hepatic glucose production from approximately 04:00–08:00 and is largely complete by mid-morning. Layered on top of, but conceptually separable from, that early-morning event is a slower, lower-amplitude **24-hour rhythm in whole-body insulin sensitivity** that persists across the entire wake/sleep cycle and modulates how the same insulin dose, the same carbohydrate load, and the same meal composition translate into glycaemia at different clock times. In healthy adults studied under controlled feeding the postprandial glucose excursion is consistently lowest at breakfast and largest after an isocaloric evening meal — a 17–25% peak-glucose increase from morning to evening at matched insulin secretion ([Van Cauter et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1885778/); [Saad et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22751690/); [Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/)). Forced-desynchrony protocols that uncouple the central circadian clock from behavioural cycles localise roughly 6–17% of that variation to an *endogenous* circadian component independent of meal timing, sleep, and prior caloric intake ([Scheer et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2657421/); [Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/); [Qian et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29862620/)). Most of the quantitative evidence is from healthy adults; data in T1D are sparser, more heterogeneous, and confounded by basal insulin pharmacokinetics, and the §14 rhythm should not be mistaken for the §12 dawn phenomenon — they are separable physiological processes that happen to overlap temporally in the early morning.

### Mechanism

#### Circadian organisation: SCN, peripheral clocks, and the BMAL1/CLOCK transcription–translation feedback loop

The mammalian circadian system is a hierarchy of self-sustained molecular oscillators. The master pacemaker resides in the suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) of the anterior hypothalamus, where ~20,000 neurons entrain to environmental light via the retinohypothalamic tract and impose a coherent ~24-h phase on the rest of the organism through autonomic outflow, body-temperature rhythms, and the cortisol and melatonin axes ([Dyar et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24567902/)). Virtually every peripheral tissue — liver, skeletal muscle, adipose, pancreatic islet, gut — contains its own cell-autonomous clock based on the canonical BMAL1/CLOCK (core circadian transcription factor heterodimer driving 24-h gene expression oscillations) transcription–translation feedback loop: BMAL1::CLOCK heterodimers drive transcription of *Per1/2/3*, *Cry1/2*, and *Rev-erbα/β*, whose protein products feed back to repress BMAL1::CLOCK activity, generating ~24-h oscillations in thousands of downstream "clock-controlled genes". In liver and skeletal muscle, 5–20% of the transcriptome is rhythmic, including transporters (GLUT4/SLC2A4), glycogen-handling enzymes (glycogen synthase, glycogen phosphorylase), gluconeogenic enzymes (PEPCK, G6Pase), mitochondrial biogenesis regulators, and components of insulin signalling itself ([Harfmann et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25512305/); [Harfmann et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27486508/)).

Peripheral clocks are entrained by the SCN through neural and hormonal cues but are also strongly responsive to feeding. In rodent restricted-feeding paradigms the liver clock can uncouple from the SCN within days, demonstrating that nutrient timing is itself a *zeitgeber* (environmental time-giver that entrains biological clocks) for peripheral oscillators independent of the central pacemaker ([Reutrakul & Van Cauter, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24628249/)). This dual entrainment — light to SCN, food to peripheral tissues — is the substrate for circadian misalignment when shift work, jet lag, or late-night eating drive the two systems out of phase.

#### Tissue-specific contributions to the 24-h ISF curve

The 24-h ISF rhythm is not a single phenomenon but a temporally coordinated sum of contributions from skeletal muscle, liver, adipose, and the β-cell:

- **Skeletal muscle (~80% of postprandial glucose disposal in healthy adults).** Muscle-specific *Bmal1* knockout in mice abolishes the diurnal rhythm in muscle glucose uptake and produces glucose intolerance with normal insulin secretion, identifying the intrinsic muscle clock — not systemic hormones alone — as a determinant of insulin-stimulated disposal ([Dyar et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24567902/); [Harfmann et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27486508/)). The mechanism is multi-layered: GLUT4 mRNA and protein abundance oscillate ~2-fold across 24 h; AS160/TBC1D4 phosphorylation, which gates GLUT4 vesicle exocytosis, is rhythmic; mitochondrial respiratory capacity peaks during the active phase and falls overnight; and glycogen synthase activity is highest in the early active phase, favouring storage of incoming glucose at breakfast.
- **Liver.** Hepatic insulin sensitivity (suppression of EGP per unit insulin) is highest in the late biological day and lowest in the late biological night/early morning, with the latter contributing — together with the cortisol/GH dawn pulse — to dawn fasting hyperglycaemia. Hepatic glycogen synthesis, gluconeogenesis (PEPCK, G6Pase), and bile acid handling are all under strong clock control.
- **Adipose tissue.** Lipolytic rhythmicity tracks the cortisol axis (peak FFA flux in late night/early morning), generating an early-morning rise in plasma free fatty acids that promotes hepatic gluconeogenesis and acutely impairs muscle insulin signalling — a mechanism that overlaps with the §22-style intracellular DAG/PKC-θ pathway and contributes to morning peripheral insulin resistance.
- **Pancreatic β-cell.** β-cell responsiveness to a glucose stimulus is also rhythmic. Saad and colleagues showed that disposition index (insulin secretion normalized to insulin sensitivity, measure of β-cell compensation) is highest at breakfast and falls progressively across the day — meaning that the body's ability to *match* insulin output to a glucose load is at its peak in the morning, even as peripheral sensitivity changes in the opposite direction. The net glucose-tolerance curve therefore reflects the *product* of two opposing rhythms ([Saad et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22751690/); [Qian et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29862620/)).

#### The breakfast–dinner asymmetry: clock effect, "second-meal" effect, and pre-load confounds

A persistent confound in the diurnal-glucose-tolerance literature is that the morning meal is the first meal after an overnight fast, while the evening meal is preceded by several hours of feeding, ambulation, and prior insulin exposure. The Staub-Traugott effect (the "second-meal phenomenon"; improved glucose tolerance after a preceding meal, mediated by residual hepatic insulin signalling and reduced FFA) — partially *opposes* the circadian deterioration in evening tolerance ([Gonzalez, 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24986552/)). Studies that compare an isolated breakfast to an isolated dinner after matched 8–10 h pre-loads tend to underestimate true circadian variation; studies that allow normal daytime feeding before the evening test meal tend to overestimate it. The cleanest evidence comes from forced-desynchrony designs in which breakfast and dinner are delivered in counterbalanced order across multiple days and circadian phases, isolating the endogenous circadian contribution from the meal-history contribution ([Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/); [Qian et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29862620/)). These designs converge on a ~17% higher postprandial glucose at biological evening (~20:00) than biological morning (~08:00) at matched pre-load, with circadian misalignment adding a further ~6%.

#### Shift work and circadian misalignment

In simulated shift-work protocols where the behavioural cycle is inverted by 12 h relative to the endogenous circadian phase, healthy young adults show a 6% rise in postprandial glucose despite a 22% rise in postprandial insulin — i.e. acutely induced peripheral insulin resistance ([Scheer et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2657421/)). Three of eight subjects in that study reached a postprandial glucose response in the prediabetic range during misalignment, despite normal tolerance at baseline. Long-term observational data are consistent: meta-analyses of cohort studies estimate a 9–37% relative excess of incident type 2 diabetes in rotating-shift workers, with a dose-response relation to years of exposure ([Gao et al., 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31684766/)). Mechanistic interpretation is constrained by the fact that shift work is bundled with sleep restriction, late-night eating, and altered physical activity — but laboratory misalignment studies that hold sleep duration constant still demonstrate independent metabolic effects, supporting a primary circadian contribution.

### Quantitative characterization

|---|---|---|---|
| Postprandial glucose: evening vs morning (healthy, matched meal, controlled pre-load) | ~17% higher in evening | 8 healthy adults, forced-desynchrony, isolated circadian effect | [Morris et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4418873/) |
| Glucose rise above morning levels under continuous enteral nutrition | +17 ± 5% glucose; +49 ± 8% calculated insulin secretion | Sleep-isolated circadian rhythmicity; healthy adults | [Van Cauter et al., 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1885778/) |
| Postprandial glucose AUC: dinner vs breakfast (healthy, isocaloric) | ~30% higher at dinner | Reproducible in multiple meal-test cohorts; partly confounded by pre-load | [Saad et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22751690/); [Owens et al., 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8974189/) |
| Disposition index (β-cell function ÷ insulin sensitivity), breakfast vs dinner | Highest at breakfast, ~25–35% lower at dinner | Healthy adults, triple-tracer mixed-meal | [Saad et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22751690/) |
| Postprandial glucose during 12-h circadian misalignment vs alignment | +6% glucose, +22% insulin | Forced-desynchrony, simulated shift work | [Scheer et al., 2009](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2657421/) |
| Type 2 diabetes risk in rotating-shift workers | RR ~1.09–1.37 vs day workers | Dose-response with years of shift exposure | [Gao et al., 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31684766/) |
| Fraction of muscle transcriptome under circadian control | ~5–20% | Includes GLUT4, glycogen-handling, mitochondrial biogenesis | [Harfmann et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25512305/); [Dyar et al., 2014](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24567902/) |
| Effect of muscle-specific Bmal1 KO | Abolished diurnal muscle glucose uptake; whole-body glucose intolerance with normal insulin secretion | Mouse model; identifies intrinsic muscle clock as causal | [Harfmann et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27486508/) |
| Population-level diurnal SI curve in T1D adults | Not statistically significant on average; large inter-individual variability | n=19 adults, triple-tracer; morning EGP suppression weaker than later in day | [Hinshaw et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3712033/) |
| Real-world ISF in T1D children/adolescents on ultra-long-acting basal | Lower morning ISF, higher evening ISF | Single-centre paediatric cohort; not generalised to adults | [Hegab, 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8957904/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Two overlapping morning effects, not one.** Early-morning hyperglycaemia in T1D reflects (a) the §12 dawn phenomenon — acute cortisol/GH-driven hepatic glucose output rising from ~04:00 — and (b) the trough of the broader 24-h ISF rhythm in muscle, liver, and adipose. Clinically, the two are difficult to separate from CGM alone; pump basal-rate profiling typically captures the *summed* effect rather than parsing it mechanistically.
2. **Population-level evidence in T1D is weaker than in healthy adults.** Hinshaw and colleagues studied 19 adults with long-standing T1D under triple-tracer mixed-meal protocols and did not find a statistically significant population-wide diurnal SI rhythm; the more robust signal was that endogenous glucose production was less suppressed at breakfast despite higher insulin concentrations, supporting a hepatic component rather than a clean peripheral SI curve in adult T1D ([Hinshaw et al., 2013](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3712033/)). Paediatric real-world data show clearer time-of-day ISF differences (lower morning, higher evening), but external validity to adults is limited ([Hegab, 2022](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8957904/)).
3. **Why the T1D signal is harder to detect.** Subcutaneous basal insulin pharmacokinetics impose their own time-varying plasma insulin profile that is convolved with the underlying circadian SI rhythm; missing endogenous β-cell pulsatility removes a feedback loop that healthy individuals use to absorb part of the rhythm; and inter-individual variability in pump basal-rate profiling and meal habits adds noise that obscures population-level patterns.
4. **Practical implications.** Time-segmented basal rates, breakfast-specific carb ratios, and dinner-specific bolus advancement are standard pump-therapy responses to the joint dawn + ISF effect. Closed-loop and hybrid closed-loop systems can compensate reactively for a fraction of the rhythm, but pre-emptive feed-forward correction (e.g., temporary basal increases before breakfast) is still commonly used and reflects the persistent residual error of purely reactive control.
5. **Shift work in T1D.** People with T1D who do rotating shifts experience compounded difficulty: their basal-rate schedule, their meal-time circadian rhythm, and their ambient cortisol axis all desynchronise. Quantitative T1D shift-work data are sparse and dominated by case series; the healthy-adult misalignment data give an upper bound on the magnitude of metabolic disruption (~6% extra postprandial glucose, ~22% extra insulin demand) that is biologically plausible to extrapolate, with the caveat of sparse direct evidence.

### Controversies and limits of the evidence

The field is in a curious position: the *qualitative* picture (breakfast > dinner glucose tolerance in healthy adults; intrinsic muscle clock gates diurnal disposal in mice) is robust, but the *quantitative* T1D-specific 24-h ISF curve that pump algorithms or closed-loop systems should embed is not yet definitively established. Three open issues are worth flagging. (a) Pre-load confounds are pervasive: many morning-vs-evening tolerance studies do not match prior caloric intake, and the second-meal effect partly cancels the circadian effect. (b) Many model-based diurnal-ISF curves used in algorithm design (e.g. Toffanin and colleagues) are *synthetic constructions* fitted within virtual-patient simulators and validated by hypoglycaemia reduction in those same simulators — useful as engineering heuristics but not direct human validation of the underlying physiological curve ([Toffanin et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23911174/)). (c) Inter-individual variability in T1D appears to be at least as large as the population-mean rhythm itself, meaning a fixed time-of-day ISF correction is unlikely to capture the relevant dynamics for any given patient — chronotype, sleep schedule, meal timing, and basal-insulin choice all modulate the effective curve.

The simulator uses a ~50% peak-to-trough ISF amplitude (1.20 → 0.70 → 1.20) based on the Toffanin (2013) virtual-patient curve, dampened relative to algorithm-design references. This is an engineering heuristic for educational realism — it is *not* a population-validated T1D physiological curve (Hinshaw 2013 found no statistically significant population-wide diurnal SI rhythm in T1D adults). The choice should be revisited if better quantitative human T1D data become available.

<a name="menstrual-cycle"></a>
## 15. Menstrual cycle and insulin requirements

The hypothalamic-pituitary-ovarian axis imposes a recurring 21-35 day rhythm of estradiol and progesterone fluctuation onto every other physiological system, and glucose-insulin homeostasis is no exception. In women with type 1 diabetes (T1D), this rhythm overlays the already considerable day-to-day variability of exogenous insulin pharmacokinetics, food, exercise, sleep and stress, occasionally producing reproducible cyclic glycemic patterns and more often producing noise that is difficult to disentangle from background variability. The clinical literature is unusually mixed: some women experience luteal-phase insulin requirement increases of 30-50% with marked premenstrual hyperglycemia, others show no detectable cycle effect at all, and modern hybrid closed-loop cohorts find the average effect to be small. The dominant message of recent systematic reviews is that inter-individual variation eclipses any group-level signal and that population-level dose adjustments are not justified ([Gamarra & Trimboli, 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36836608/)).

### Endocrinology of the cycle and metabolic correlates

The menstrual cycle is conventionally divided into four functional phases. The **menses** (cycle days 1-5) coincide with the lowest steroid levels: estradiol typically <100 pmol/L, progesterone <2 nmol/L. The **follicular phase** (days 1-13) is dominated by rising estradiol from the developing dominant follicle, peaking at 800-1500 pmol/L just before the LH surge (luteinizing hormone surge triggering ovulation at cycle day ~14). **Ovulation** (around day 14) is followed by formation of the corpus luteum (post-ovulatory ovarian structure secreting progesterone throughout the luteal phase), which during the **luteal phase** (days 15-28) produces progesterone in concentrations 10-40 nmol/L (a 10- to 30-fold rise over follicular baseline) along with a secondary estradiol peak of 400-800 pmol/L. If implantation does not occur the corpus luteum involutes, steroid concentrations collapse over 24-48 h, and menstruation ensues.

Each steroid has at least one biologically plausible mechanism for altering insulin action. **Progesterone** in supraphysiologic doses produces peripheral insulin resistance in animal models and humans, attributed to (i) impaired post-receptor insulin signaling in skeletal muscle, (ii) increased lipolysis with elevated circulating free fatty acids and consequent FFA-induced insulin resistance via the Randle cycle, and (iii) reduced GLUT4 translocation. **Estradiol** has bidirectional effects: physiologic concentrations enhance hepatic and peripheral insulin sensitivity, but supraphysiologic levels (as in pharmacological combined oral contraceptives) impair it. The luteal phase combines high progesterone with moderately elevated estradiol — net effect is typically a small reduction in whole-body insulin-mediated glucose disposal.

### Quantitative evidence in healthy women

Euglycemic-hyperinsulinemic clamp data in non-diabetic women are remarkably consistent in showing **little or no whole-body insulin sensitivity change** across the cycle. Yki-Järvinen ([1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6376531/)) clamped seven healthy women at follicular and luteal phases and found glucose disposal of 9.0 ± 1.3 vs. 9.2 ± 1.6 mg/kg/min (NS), despite progesterone rising from 1.2 to 24.2 nmol/L. Diamond and colleagues likewise reported no significant change with hyperinsulinemic clamps in non-diabetic women. The BioCycle longitudinal study using HOMA-IR (homeostatic model assessment of insulin resistance, fasting-based surrogate) across multiple cycles in 257 healthy women detected statistically significant but small variation, with HOMA-IR peaking around the late follicular phase and the magnitude of change <10% — well within day-to-day biological noise. The take-home is that in metabolically intact women, ovarian counter-regulation, beta-cell adaptation and hepatic insulin clearance buffer most of the steroidal effect on circulating glucose. *(Caveat: clamp studies have small samples — typically n = 5-15 — and are powered to detect only large effects; subtle phase effects in subgroups would be missed.)*

### T1D-specific evidence: clamp and pump-based studies

Because women with T1D lack endogenous insulin and beta-cell counter-regulation, any phase-dependent change in insulin sensitivity translates more directly into glucose excursions. The literature here is genuinely heterogeneous.

The most-cited mechanistic study is **Widom, Diamond & Simonson ([1992](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1547678/))**, who performed hyperglycemic-hyperinsulinemic clamps (glucose 11.7 mmol/L, insulin ~24 mU/L) in 16 IDDM women at follicular and luteal phases. Seven subjects ("deteriorators") showed luteal-phase glucose disposal falling from ~30.2 to 24.5 µmol/kg/min — a ~19% reduction — and this group also had a larger luteal-phase rise in estradiol (164 → 352 pmol/L). The remaining nine subjects ("non-deteriorators") had unchanged or improved disposal. This bimodality has been the dominant interpretive frame ever since: a sizable subgroup (perhaps 30-50% of cycling women with T1D) is sensitive to cyclic steroid fluctuation while others are not.

**Trout ([2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17425444/))** used frequently-sampled IVGTT (intravenous glucose tolerance test with minimal-model analysis) in five T1D pump users and found three of five with reduced luteal-phase insulin sensitivity, with no significant group-level effect. **Goldner ([2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15321002/))** used early-generation CGM over three consecutive cycles in four T1D women and found two with reproducible luteal-phase hyperglycemia patterns and two without — again a roughly 50-50 split. **Brown ([2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4667305/))** estimated nocturnal insulin sensitivity using Kalman-filtered CGM and pump data in 12 T1D women across 3 cycles each (33/36 ovulatory). Group-mean insulin sensitivity index fell from 4.6 ± 0.5 (early follicular) to 4.0 ± 0.9 (early-mid luteal, P = 0.05) and 4.0 ± 1.0 (late luteal, P = 0.03) — a ~13% reduction. Across the 36 cycles, 42% showed >5% luteal sensitivity reduction, 36% showed increased sensitivity, and 22% were unchanged; 9/12 subjects had a *consistent* pattern across at least two of their cycles. The latter is the strongest evidence to date that cyclic effects are individually reproducible even when the population mean is modest.

### CGM-era observational data

Cross-sectional CGM studies confirm a small but consistent group-level luteal-phase deterioration. **Tatulashvili ([2022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35869507/))** prospectively monitored 24 T1D women over 62 cycles and observed time-in-range falling progressively from 61 ± 18% (early follicular) to 55 ± 20% (late luteal, P < 0.001), with significantly higher time-above-range late-luteal vs early-follicular (P = 0.003). The absolute TIR decrease (~6 percentage points) is real but small compared with intra-subject day-to-day variability of 10-20 percentage points. **Closed-loop cohorts attenuate this further:** Levy ([2022](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35099294/)) studied 16 T1D women across ≥3 cycles each on the Tandem Control-IQ system and found insulin delivery and CGM metrics statistically unchanged across phases. Monroy and colleagues ([2025, "780MENS"](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39898554/)) reported 12 T1D women on the MiniMed 780G with total daily insulin rising from 33.6 ± 12.2 IU (early follicular) to 37.2 ± 11.9 IU (late luteal, +11%, P < 0.001) and mean glucose rising from 7.3 to 7.7 mmol/L (P = 0.002), but with TIR essentially preserved (85% vs 83%, NS). The implication is that hybrid closed-loop algorithms compensate adequately for typical luteal increases in insulin demand.

### Perimenstrual hypoglycemia risk

Less well documented but clinically important is the **abrupt steroid withdrawal at menstruation**, when insulin demand can fall by 10-20% over 24-48 h as progesterone-induced resistance dissipates. Women maintaining a luteal-phase upward titration of basal or bolus doses are then at risk for hypoglycemia on cycle day 1-2 if the titration is not promptly reversed. Tatulashvili's data showed a small but significant rise in time-below-range during the mid-follicular vs early-follicular phase, consistent with this mechanism. The phenomenon argues against fixed luteal up-titration without an equally reliable taper at menses onset.

### PCOS, contraception and pregnancy interactions

**Polycystic ovary syndrome** (PCOS) is markedly over-represented in T1D, with prevalence depending on criteria: 12-18% by NIH 1990, 31% by Androgen Excess Society, and up to 40% by Rotterdam ([Codner & Escobar-Morreale, 2007](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17284617/)). Mechanistically the supraphysiologic peripheral insulin doses required in T1D drive ovarian theca-cell androgen production, mimicking the hyperinsulinemia of T2D-PCOS. Clinical features (oligomenorrhea, anovulation, hyperandrogenism) are typically milder than in T2D-PCOS but confound any cycle-tracking attempt: anovulatory cycles lack the luteal progesterone surge entirely, while irregular cycles produce timing uncertainty that defeats phase-based dose adjustments.

**Combined oral contraceptives** (estrogen + progestin) impose a relatively constant daily steroid dose across the active-pill phase and substantially **blunt or abolish endogenous cyclic variation** in insulin requirements. The pill-free week typically produces a steroid-withdrawal response analogous to natural menses. Progestin-only contraceptives (depot medroxyprogesterone, levonorgestrel IUS, etonogestrel implant) do *not* produce reliably cyclical hormone profiles and may cause sustained mild insulin resistance without cyclic variation. T1D women considering hormonal contraception with cycle-related glycemic instability often tolerate combined OCPs better, though absolute insulin requirements may rise modestly.

The **transition into pregnancy** is signaled by failure of the corpus luteum to involute, sustained progesterone elevation and exponentially rising hCG, with insulin requirements typically rising 50-100% by mid-second trimester (covered separately).

### Inter-individual variability and clinical implications

Synthesizing the evidence: roughly 40-50% of T1D women appear to have a clinically meaningful cyclic pattern, of which perhaps 20-30% experience luteal increases in insulin requirement of 20-50%; the remainder show smaller, inconsistent or absent effects. Group-mean luteal-phase changes in clamp- or CGM-based insulin sensitivity cluster around 10-20% reduction, but the variance around that mean is enormous. *(Caveat: every published study has n ≤ 24 T1D subjects, follows ≤6 cycles per subject, and applies different phase definitions; meta-analytic pooling has not been feasible. T1D-specific evidence is genuinely sparse, and the existence of "non-cyclers" is robust across studies.)* Personalized cycle tracking — ideally combining CGM time-series with self-reported cycle phase or salivary progesterone — remains the only validated approach; population-level dose templates based on phase do not improve outcomes and risk inducing perimenstrual hypoglycemia in non-cyclers.
