<a name="seasonal-variation"></a>
## 16. Seasonal variation in glycemia and insulin requirements

Glycemic control in T1D is not a stationary signal. Repeated cross-sectional and longitudinal analyses of large registries demonstrate that mean HbA1c oscillates predictably with the calendar, with a winter peak and a late-summer trough in the northern hemisphere. The amplitude is small in absolute terms (≈0.2–0.7 percentage points in most cohorts) but exceeds the typical month-to-month within-subject coefficient of variation in stable adult T1D and is therefore detectable at population level. The phenomenon matters for three reasons: (i) it is a confounder in clinical trials and HbA1c-based diagnostic thresholds when enrollment or assay timing is not balanced across seasons; (ii) it shifts the operating point of automated insulin delivery (AID) algorithms and predictive bolus advisors that assume stationary insulin needs; (iii) it points to modifiable upstream drivers — temperature, activity, daylight, infections — each with mechanistic links covered elsewhere in this document.

### 16.1 Magnitude and timing in T1D registries

The most rigorous T1D-specific dataset is from the Lodz pediatric clinic (central Poland, 51°N): 3,935 HbA1c measurements in 677 children showed a sine-wave with peak in February (and secondary peaks in November–December) and trough in August–September; the winter–summer difference was 0.44–0.67 percentage points across consecutive years (max single-month delta 0.72 pp), against a cohort mean HbA1c of 7.65 ± 1.12% [Mianowska 2011]. The signal was confined to schoolchildren (≥7 y); preschoolers showed white-noise variability, attributable to caregiver-controlled rather than child-driven activity and meal patterns. Swedish pediatric registry data (n ≈ 8,000) reproduce a seasonal HbA1c pattern with similar phasing [Hanberger 2014]. Adult T1D data are sparser; a Japanese tertiary-care series (n = 88 with severe-hypoglycemia events) reported median HbA1c 9.1% (winter) vs 7.7% (summer), though the comparison did not reach significance given small subgroup sizes (P = 0.13) [Tsujimoto 2014]. As a working figure for adult T1D, an amplitude of ≈0.2–0.5 pp is consistent with the published data, with the larger end of that range applying to high-latitude, lifestyle-active subgroups; pediatric amplitudes are typically larger.

### 16.2 Mechanism 1 — temperature and insulin absorption

Ambient temperature correlated negatively with HbA1c in Lodz schoolchildren at R = −0.56 (p = 0.0002), and hours of sunshine and solar irradiance similarly with R ≈ −0.52 [Mianowska 2011]. Mechanistically, warmer ambient and skin temperature raises subcutaneous blood flow and accelerates insulin absorption from the depot; this is treated quantitatively in §21 (temperature/climate effects on insulin absorption). The same physics produces the paradoxical Tsujimoto finding of *more* severe hypoglycemia in summer in T1D (35.2% vs 18.2% of cases, p = 0.01) despite *lower* mean HbA1c [Tsujimoto 2014]: a faster, taller insulin-action curve sharpens both the average effect and the tail risk of overshoot, particularly when meal-bolus timing or basal rates set in cooler months are not retitrated. *Caveat:* the temperature contribution cannot be cleanly separated from concurrent changes in physical activity and clothing-mediated heat exposure at the injection site, and most observational studies do not adjudicate these.

### 16.3 Mechanism 2 — physical activity seasonality

Daylight hours and ambient temperature jointly drive an outdoor-activity gradient that is steep in temperate latitudes. Pedometer and accelerometer data in non-diabetic populations show 20–40% higher daily step counts in summer than winter at latitudes >40°. Exercise increases insulin sensitivity acutely (next 24–48 h) and chronically (training adaptation), and this is the dominant pathway by which behavior couples to seasonal HbA1c (mechanism quantified in §10, exercise). The pediatric school-vs-preschool dissociation in [Mianowska 2011] is the strongest single piece of evidence that activity, not photoperiod or vitamin D *per se*, drives the bulk of the signal: preschoolers experience the same temperature, daylight and vitamin D as schoolchildren but lack the autonomous outdoor-play exposure, and they show no seasonal HbA1c pattern. *Caveat:* activity is partially confounded with school-holiday meal patterns (different carbohydrate loads, snacking frequency), which the registry data cannot resolve.

### 16.4 Mechanism 3 — vitamin D and insulin sensitivity

25-hydroxyvitamin D follows a strong seasonal cycle at temperate latitudes (winter trough roughly 30–50% below summer peak), and observational studies link low 25(OH)D to higher HbA1c and lower insulin sensitivity. However, Mendelian randomization in 443,734 Europeans found no causal effect of genetically lowered vitamin D on T1D risk [Manousaki 2021], and randomized supplementation trials in established T1D have not produced clinically meaningful HbA1c reductions. The most defensible position is that vitamin D is a *marker* of light exposure and outdoor activity rather than a major independent driver of seasonal glycemia in T1D; any direct β-cell or muscle insulin-signaling effect is small relative to the activity and absorption mechanisms above.

### 16.5 Mechanism 4 — winter respiratory infections

Winter respiratory virus seasons (influenza, RSV, rhinovirus, since 2020 SARS-CoV-2) drive transient insulin resistance via cytokine release (TNF-α, IL-6) and counter-regulatory hormone activation (mechanism in §17, illness/infection). Infection episodes are typically days-to-weeks long, which is short relative to the 8–12 week HbA1c integration window, but their winter clustering biases the integrated signal upward. The SWEET registry showed that pandemic-era disruption changed not only T1D incidence seasonality but the timing of glycemic control nadirs, supporting infection load as a real contributor [SWEET 2022]. *Caveat:* corticosteroid prescriptions for respiratory illness amplify the effect and are not consistently captured in registry data.

### 16.6 Mechanism 5 — diet, light exposure and circadian alignment

Northern-hemisphere winter is associated with higher caloric and carbohydrate intake (holidays, comfort foods, reduced fresh produce), shorter photoperiods, and circadian phase-delay in late chronotypes. Each of these has plausible mechanistic links to insulin sensitivity (circadian misalignment lowers peripheral insulin sensitivity by 10–15% in controlled lab studies), but the specific contribution to seasonal HbA1c in T1D has not been quantified. Holiday-period (Dec–early Jan) glucose excursions are clearly visible in CGM cohort data but represent a minority of the integrated winter excess.

### 16.7 Latitude, hemisphere asymmetry and equatorial cohorts

Seasonal amplitude is not universal. The T1D incidence-seasonality literature, which is methodologically simpler than HbA1c-seasonality, found that of 105 worldwide centers only 42 showed significant seasonality in incidence; latitude largely accounted for the difference, with southern-hemisphere centers showing inverted (July–September peak) patterns consistent with their winter [Moltchanova 2009]. For HbA1c specifically, Scandinavian, German, Polish and UK studies show clear winter peaks; equatorial and low-latitude cohorts show attenuated or absent patterns; a North Indian study found a *monsoon* peak rather than a thermal-winter peak, attributed to monsoon-related activity restriction and festival-period dietary patterns [Sapra 2024]. The takeaway: the *direction* and *magnitude* of seasonal HbA1c variation in any given clinic depends on local climate amplitude, cultural activity patterns, and infection ecology — temperate-zone Northern Hemisphere data should not be extrapolated to equatorial care.

### 16.8 Open questions and climate-change implications

Whether climate-change-driven warming and milder winters will compress the seasonal HbA1c amplitude in temperate latitudes is unresolved. A naive prediction from the Mianowska temperature correlation (R = −0.56) is that winter HbA1c would fall as winters warm, narrowing the seasonal swing; this is plausible but unproven and ignores compensating shifts in infection seasonality and indoor heating practices. Whether AID systems should incorporate seasonal priors on basal rate and ICR/ISF is an open algorithmic question — current commercial systems adapt within days-to-weeks but do not encode an annual cycle.

# Part 6: Lifestyle and External Factors

<a name="illness-infection"></a>
## 17. Acute illness and infection — sick-day physiology

Acute intercurrent illness is one of the most common precipitants of diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) in established type 1 diabetes (T1D). In international pediatric registries combining data from England, Wales, the U.S., Austria, and Germany, intercurrent infection is consistently among the leading identifiable triggers of DKA episodes, alongside missed insulin and pump failure ([Maahs 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26055086/); [Wolfsdorf 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29900641/)). The mechanistic problem is that infection simultaneously raises insulin requirements and disrupts the patient's ability to deliver, eat, and absorb predictably — a tight-rope walk between hyperglycemia/ketonemia on one side and hypoglycemia/dehydration on the other. Holt and colleagues' 2024 review estimates that people with diabetes carry a 1.5- to 4-fold increased risk of infection, with hyperglycemia itself impairing neutrophil function, complement activation, and adaptive responses, creating a feed-forward loop where poorly controlled BG worsens the very infection driving the loss of control ([Holt 2024 — Diabetologia, OA](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11153295/)).

### Cytokine biology of acute infection

Within hours of pathogen recognition, innate immune cells (macrophages, monocytes, dendritic cells) and tissue-resident sentinels release a coordinated wave of pro-inflammatory cytokines: TNF-α, IL-1β, and IL-6. IL-6 is the dominant systemic signal — its plasma concentration tracks the magnitude and severity of infection more reliably than TNF-α or IL-1β, both of which act largely paracrine and have shorter half-lives. In moderate viral or bacterial infection (e.g. influenza, community-acquired pneumonia, pyelonephritis), circulating IL-6 typically rises from a baseline of <5 pg/mL to 20–200 pg/mL, with peaks of 1000+ pg/mL in severe sepsis. TNF-α rises more modestly in plasma (often only 2- to 10-fold) but exerts strong local effects on insulin-target tissues. Caveat: cytokine concentrations vary widely between infections, between individuals, and between assay platforms — the absolute numbers above are order-of-magnitude clinical orientation, not precise thresholds.

### Cytokine-induced insulin resistance — molecular mechanism

The signaling pathway by which TNF-α, IL-1β, and IL-6 blunt insulin action is the same set of serine kinase cascades described in detail in [§10 Exercise-induced inflammation](#exercise-inflammation): cytokine receptor engagement activates JNK and IKK-β, both of which phosphorylate IRS-1 on inhibitory residues including Ser307, while IL-6 in particular induces SOCS3, which directly competes with IRS-1 for receptor binding and targets it for proteasomal degradation. The downstream consequence is impaired tyrosine phosphorylation of IRS-1, blunted PI3K/Akt signaling, reduced GLUT4 translocation in skeletal muscle, and attenuated insulin suppression of hepatic glucose production. The acute-illness version of this pathway is essentially the same biology operating with steeper kinetics — hours rather than days — and with a stronger systemic component because IL-6 spillover from the infection site reaches all insulin-target tissues simultaneously ([Senn 2002 — Diabetes](https://diabetesjournals.org/diabetes/article/51/12/3391/34282/); [Senn 2003 — JBC, SOCS3](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12560330/); [de Alvaro 2004 — JBC](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15199052/); [Pickup 2004 — Diabetologia](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s00125-004-1447-y)). Caveat: IL-6 has dual roles — chronic elevation drives insulin resistance, while transient exercise-induced IL-6 from contracting muscle is insulin-sensitizing; the difference appears to lie in source, duration, and co-secretion of IL-1ra versus TNF-α.

### Counterregulatory hormone activation

Pro-inflammatory cytokines do not act in isolation. IL-6, IL-1β, and TNF-α each independently stimulate the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis, with synergistic effects when combined; IL-6 is the most potent of the three and can drive ACTH and cortisol release even when CRH signaling is blocked, indicating direct pituitary action ([Bethin 2000 — PNAS](https://www.pnas.org/doi/10.1073/pnas.97.16.9317)). Plasma cortisol typically doubles to triples during moderate febrile illness, with loss of the normal diurnal rhythm — sustained elevation through the night is part of why nocturnal hyperglycemia is a hallmark of sick days. Glucagon also rises, partly via cytokine-stimulated α-cell secretion and partly via baroreflex/dehydration-driven sympathetic activation. The combined cortisol + glucagon + catecholamine signature drives hepatic gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis, increases lipolysis (raising FFA delivery to liver), and antagonizes peripheral insulin action — the same counterregulatory profile described in [§11 Counterregulatory hormones](#counterregulatory-hormones), now layered on top of cytokine-induced IR.

### Fever as multiplier of metabolic demand

Fever is energetically expensive. Classical indirect calorimetry studies (DuBois; Roe & Kinney; reviewed in [Manthous 1995](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3621073/)) show that resting metabolic rate rises approximately 10–13% per °C above 37 °C, driven by Q10 effects on enzyme kinetics and the energetic cost of generating and defending the new hypothalamic setpoint (shivering thermogenesis at fever onset, then increased substrate cycling). A 2 °C fever therefore lifts basal energy demand by ~20–25%. In T1D this matters because the substrate fueling that demand must come from somewhere — and with insulin signaling already blunted by cytokines, the body increasingly mobilizes hepatic glycogen and fatty acids rather than glucose uptake into peripheral tissues. Caveat: fever's direct contribution to hyperglycemia is smaller than the cytokine + counterregulatory hormone effect; the dominant clinical signal is insulin resistance, not heat-driven gluconeogenesis.

### The gastrointestinal-illness paradox

Gastroenteritis (rotavirus, norovirus, bacterial enteritis, foodborne toxins) creates a uniquely difficult sick-day scenario in T1D. Insulin requirements are elevated by the cytokine storm and counterregulatory drive, but oral carbohydrate intake collapses because of nausea, vomiting, anorexia, or diarrheal malabsorption. The naive response — "I'm not eating, so I'll skip insulin" — is precisely the failure mode that drives gastroenteritis-associated DKA, because basal insulin requirements *rise* during the illness and continued endogenous glucose production from cytokine-stimulated hepatic gluconeogenesis means that withholding basal allows ketogenesis to accelerate unopposed. ISPAD sick-day guidance emphasizes that basal insulin must *never* be omitted during illness, even during anorexia or vomiting — the dose may need adjustment, but full omission is one of the highest-risk decisions a family or patient can make ([ISPAD 2022 Sick-day chapter](https://www.ispad.org/resource/chapter-13-sick-day-management.html)). Caveat: pediatric DKA admission rates during gastroenteritis episodes are substantially higher than in adults with similar illness severity, reflecting smaller hepatic glycogen reserves, faster ketogenic flux, and greater dependence on caregiver decision-making.

### Accelerated ketogenesis in T1D illness

Under physiological insulinemia, ketogenesis is restrained by insulin's suppression of adipose tissue lipolysis and hepatic carnitine palmitoyltransferase-1 (CPT-1; rate-limiting enzyme for fatty acid entry into mitochondria, the committed step for ketogenesis) activity. During acute illness in T1D, three forces converge to break this restraint: (1) cytokine + counterregulatory hormone-driven insulin resistance reduces the *effective* insulin signal at adipocyte and hepatocyte level, even when the same exogenous dose is delivered; (2) glucagon/cortisol directly upregulate hepatic ketogenic enzymes; (3) elevated plasma FFA from accelerated lipolysis increases substrate flux into mitochondrial β-oxidation, where the malonyl-CoA brake is already lifted by glucagon's suppression of acetyl-CoA carboxylase. The result is that β-hydroxybutyrate (BOHB; primary circulating ketone body, the dominant acid in DKA) can rise into the 1–3 mmol/L range within hours even when blood glucose is only modestly elevated — so-called "euglycemic" or "near-euglycemic" DKA, especially in patients who maintain insulin delivery but at a dose that has become subtherapeutic relative to the new requirement ([Wolfsdorf 2018 — ISPAD DKA, OA](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/pedi.12701); [Glaser 2022 — ISPAD DKA, OA](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36250645/)). The ISPAD-endorsed action threshold is BOHB ≥1.5 mmol/L → administer correction insulin and rapid-acting carbohydrate together, repeat ketone measurement every 1–2 h, escalate to medical contact if BOHB rises despite correction.

### Specific infection profiles

- **Influenza and other respiratory viruses.** Strong systemic IL-6 response, prolonged fever (3–7 d), often modest GI involvement. Insulin requirements typically up 20–50% during the febrile phase, declining over the subsequent week. Influenza vaccination is recommended annually for T1D, and observational data suggest reduced hospitalization rates in vaccinated individuals.
- **COVID-19 / SARS-CoV-2.** Particular concern in T1D because of (a) ACE2 expression on β-cells raising theoretical risk of further islet injury, (b) profound IL-6 storm in severe cases, and (c) early pandemic data showing increased presentation of new-onset T1D in DKA. The Unsworth multicenter U.K. cohort reported 70% DKA at presentation in new-onset pediatric T1D during the first COVID-19 wave (vs ~30–40% baseline), with 52% of DKA cases severe ([Unsworth 2020 — Diabetes Care, OA](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/43/11/e170/35903/)). Causality for new-onset T1D induction by SARS-CoV-2 remains debated; what is clear is that the pandemic's healthcare-access disruption raised DKA-at-diagnosis rates substantially.
- **Gastroenteritis.** As above — high pediatric DKA risk despite often modest fever, due to the eating/vomiting/insulin paradox.
- **Urinary tract infection (UTI).** Particularly relevant in adults with longer-duration T1D; glycosuria provides bacterial substrate and partial immune impairment in poorly-controlled diabetes raises pyelonephritis risk. UTI is a classical "occult" precipitant of DKA in patients without overt febrile symptoms.
- **Dental abscess and skin/soft-tissue infection.** Often underestimated as DKA precipitants because patients may not recognize a dental or cellulitic source as systemic illness. Clinically important to ask explicitly during DKA workup.

### Sick-day rules — ISPAD 2022 framing

The ISPAD 2022 consensus sick-day chapter ([Chapter 13](https://www.ispad.org/resource/chapter-13-sick-day-management.html)) operationalizes the physiology above into actionable rules. The key principles, with the caveat that all dose adjustments are personalized and the ranges below are starting points, not prescriptions:

- **Never omit basal insulin** — even during vomiting or anorexia. Reduce only if BG is trending low *and* ketones are negative; otherwise hold the basal dose.
- **Increase frequency of monitoring** — BG every 1–2 h, blood ketones (BOHB) every 2–4 h while BG > 14 mmol/L or symptoms persist. Urine ketones are a less timely fallback.
- **Correction-dose insulin scaling.** When BOHB ≥0.6 mmol/L with hyperglycemia, give an additional 5–10% of total daily dose (TDD; sum of all basal and bolus insulin delivered in a 24-hour period) as rapid-acting; when BOHB ≥1.5 mmol/L, 10–20% of TDD with simultaneous rapid-acting carbohydrate to allow continued insulin action without inducing hypoglycemia.
- **Maintain hydration.** Sugar-free fluids when BG > 14 mmol/L, sugar-containing when BG < 8 mmol/L. Dehydration accelerates the ketoacidotic spiral by reducing renal glucose and ketone clearance.
- **Trigger medical contact** when BOHB rises despite correction, persistent vomiting prevents oral intake, BG remains > 14 mmol/L for 6+ hours, or any altered mental status / abdominal pain develops.

Aggregate insulin requirement during acute febrile illness in T1D typically increases 20–100% above pre-illness baseline, with the upper end seen in severe sepsis, post-surgical infection, or burn-pattern hypermetabolism. The lower end is more typical of common viral illness in well-controlled T1D ([Brink 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26343445/); [Laffel 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30079506/)).

### Controversies and uncertainties

- **Pediatric vs adult differences.** Children show faster ketogenic flux per unit illness severity, smaller glycogen buffer, and higher dependence on caregiver decisions — combining to give substantially higher gastroenteritis-associated DKA rates than adults. Adult sick-day rules drawn directly from pediatric ISPAD frameworks may under-emphasize the role of comorbid conditions (cardiovascular disease, renal impairment) that matter more in older T1D populations.
- **Fixed multipliers vs glucose-driven titration.** Older sick-day frameworks specified fixed percentage increases (e.g. "+20% basal during illness"). Modern ISPAD guidance moves toward ketone- and glucose-driven titration because the insulin-resistance magnitude varies enormously between infections and patients — a fixed multiplier under-doses severe sepsis and over-doses mild rhinovirus.
- **COVID-era data.** The 2020–2022 COVID-19 pandemic generated a large but heterogeneous body of data on T1D-infection interaction. Genuine signals (DKA-at-diagnosis spike, IL-6 storm in severe cases) coexist with confounded signals (healthcare-access disruption, delayed presentation, behavioral change) that are difficult to disentangle. Long-term incidence data for whether SARS-CoV-2 itself accelerates T1D onset are still maturing.
- **Sick-day BOHB thresholds.** The 1.5 mmol/L action threshold is operationally validated but mechanistically it is a continuum — ketogenesis is already accelerated at 0.6 mmol/L. Some frameworks (notably newer pump-loop algorithms) initiate intervention at lower thresholds.

<a name="sleep-deprivation"></a>
## 18. Sleep and sleep deprivation

Sleep is an active neuroendocrine state, not a passive interlude, and its architecture is tightly coupled to glucose homeostasis. A normal adult night cycles four to six times through ~90-minute periods of non-rapid-eye-movement (NREM) sleep — divided into N1, N2, and slow-wave sleep (SWS, N3) — alternating with rapid-eye-movement (REM) sleep. SWS dominates the first third of the night and coincides with the nocturnal nadir of cortisol, the sympathetic nervous system, and core body temperature, together with the largest spontaneous growth-hormone (GH) pulse of the 24-hour cycle. REM dominates the last third and is accompanied by autonomic instability, rising adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)/cortisol secretion, and a partial uncoupling of cerebral glucose utilisation from systemic glycaemia. Disturbance of either the duration or the internal architecture of sleep alters the magnitude and timing of these endocrine signals, with quantifiable downstream effects on insulin sensitivity, hepatic glucose output, appetite hormones, and counterregulation. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), the picture is layered: the same physiology applies, but the night is also when most exogenous-insulin overdoses, fear-of-hypoglycaemia, CGM/pump alarm awakenings, and nocturia from osmotic diuresis converge to fragment sleep in ways healthy controls do not experience. Sleep biology is therefore a clinically and physiologically relevant axis for T1D — and one of the most under-studied.

### Normal sleep architecture and its endocrine choreography

A consolidated 7–9 h adult night opens with a rapid descent through N1 and N2 into SWS within ~15–25 min. The first NREM-REM cycle ends ~90 min after sleep onset and is followed by progressively shorter SWS episodes and longer, denser REM episodes towards morning. Two endocrine systems are anchored to this architecture. First, the somatotropic axis: ~70–80 % of the daily GH pulse mass is released during the first SWS episode in young adults, and SWS suppression abolishes this pulse without compensatory daytime release. Second, the hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal (HPA) axis: cortisol secretion is actively inhibited during early-night SWS, reaches its nadir around sleep onset, then rises through the late night to peak shortly after waking. Sympathetic outflow shows a similar pattern — low during SWS, rising during REM and the second half of the night. (*Caveat:* these patterns are best characterised in young, healthy men sleeping in laboratory conditions; women, older adults, and individuals with chronic disease show attenuated SWS and a flatter cortisol nadir, complicating extrapolation.)

### The Spiegel–Van Cauter sleep-restriction series

The classical evidence base for "sleep loss as a metabolic stressor" comes from a tightly controlled series at the University of Chicago. In the landmark 1999 *Lancet* report, 11 healthy young men were studied across 6 nights of severe restriction (4 h time-in-bed) followed by 6 nights of recovery (12 h time-in-bed). After the restriction phase, intravenous glucose-tolerance-test (ivGTT) glucose clearance fell ~40 % and the acute insulin response to glucose fell ~30 %, yielding a glucose-tolerance pattern resembling impaired glucose tolerance in older adults. Evening cortisol concentrations rose markedly (P = 0.0001) — a "flattening" of the diurnal cortisol slope rather than a uniform increase — sympathetic heart-rate-variability indices increased (P < 0.02), and thyrotropin fell (P < 0.01). A 2004 *Annals of Internal Medicine* brief communication extended the work to appetite regulation: 2 nights of 4 h sleep versus 2 nights of 10 h, in a controlled-feeding crossover, reduced circulating leptin by ~18 %, increased ghrelin by ~28 %, and increased self-rated hunger and appetite by ~24 % and ~23 %, respectively, with the largest effect on calorie-dense, carbohydrate-rich foods (+33–45 %). (*Caveat:* the Spiegel cohorts were small (n = 11–12), exclusively young, lean men, studied under fixed isocaloric feeding and bed-rest conditions; the effect sizes are likely upper bounds, and free-living adults compensate partially through behaviour.)

### Replication and dose–response in independent cohorts

Subsequent independent groups have largely replicated the direction of effect with smaller magnitudes under more moderate restriction. Buxton et al. (2010, *Diabetes*) restricted 20 healthy men to 5 h/night for 7 nights after an 8-night 10 h baseline and observed a 20 ± 24 % fall in ivGTT-derived insulin sensitivity (P = 0.001) and an 11 ± 5.5 % fall in clamp-measured insulin sensitivity (P < 0.04), without proportionate changes in salivary cortisol or catecholamines — implicating mechanisms beyond classical stress hormones. Nedeltcheva et al. (2009, *J Clin Endocrinol Metab*) showed that even in middle-aged adults, 14 nights of 5.5 h time-in-bed (versus 8.5 h) reduced glucose tolerance and the disposition index, raised evening cortisol, and produced sustained increases in hunger ratings. Selective SWS suppression — sparing total sleep time but eliminating slow-wave activity using acoustic stimuli — independently impaired insulin sensitivity by ~25 % over only 3 nights (Tasali 2008, *PNAS*), demonstrating that sleep *quality*, not just total duration, is metabolically relevant. The dose–response across studies suggests roughly a 5–15 % reduction in insulin sensitivity per hour of chronic sleep curtailment below ~7 h, but the curve is non-linear and confounded by adiposity, age, and baseline sleep need.

### Molecular and circadian mechanisms

Several non-mutually-exclusive pathways connect sleep loss to insulin resistance. (i) **Sympathetic overdrive:** restriction shifts heart-rate variability towards low-frequency (sympathetic) dominance and raises plasma noradrenaline, suppressing β-cell insulin secretion and stimulating hepatic glucose output. (ii) **HPA-axis flattening:** the loss of the early-night cortisol nadir produces a sustained mild hypercortisolaemia that, over days, induces hepatic and adipose insulin resistance through classical glucocorticoid-receptor signalling. (iii) **Adipokine perturbation:** the leptin fall and ghrelin rise increase energy intake and shift substrate selection towards carbohydrate, exacerbating postprandial glycaemia. (iv) **Peripheral clock disruption:** the molecular clock genes BMAL1, CLOCK, PER1–3, and CRY1–2 oscillate in liver, muscle, adipose, and pancreatic β-cells; misalignment between the central suprachiasmatic clock and peripheral oscillators (as in shift work, jet lag, or social jet lag) reduces β-cell insulin response and skeletal-muscle GLUT4 translocation independently of sleep duration. (v) **Mitochondrial and inflammatory signalling:** sleep restriction raises IL-6, TNF-α, and CRP and impairs muscle mitochondrial respiration, contributing to insulin resistance through the JNK/IKK-β pathway. Cross-link [§12 (dawn phenomenon)](#dawn): the cortisol-rise that produces dawn hyperglycaemia in T1D is the *normal* end of the sleep-locked cortisol curve, and sleep loss broadens and amplifies it — but the two phenomena are mechanistically distinct (dawn = circadian cortisol/GH timing; sleep loss = sustained HPA/SNS activation).

### Sleep restriction in T1D specifically

The only direct experimental data come from Donga et al. (2010, *Diabetes Care*). Seven adults with T1D (age 44 ± 7 y, mean HbA1c well-controlled) underwent hyperinsulinaemic-euglycaemic clamps with [6,6-²H₂]glucose tracer after a normal night (~469 min sleep) and after a single night of restriction (~222 min). Peripheral glucose disposal fell from 25.5 ± 2.6 to 22.0 ± 2.1 µmol·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ (P = 0.04, ~14–21 % reduction), and the steady-state glucose-infusion rate fell ~21 %. Endogenous glucose production was *not* significantly different (6.2 ± 0.8 vs 6.9 ± 0.6 µmol·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹, NS) — a notable contrast to non-diabetic literature, although the small sample limits power to detect a hepatic component. (*Caveat:* a single-night, single-cohort, n = 7 study cannot establish chronic-restriction kinetics in T1D; claims of "sleep loss universally raises hepatic glucose production in T1D" overreach the evidence. Reduced peripheral disposal is the robust finding.)

### Counterregulatory blunting during sleep

Healthy sleep itself attenuates the counterregulatory response to hypoglycaemia. In landmark clamp work by Jones et al. (1998) and Banarer & Cryer (2003), nocturnal hypoglycaemia during sleep produced markedly lower epinephrine and symptom responses than identical hypoglycaemia while awake, and crucially failed to wake most subjects. In T1D this defect is compounded by the broader hypoglycaemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF) phenomenon (cross-link [§11](#hypoglycemia-haaf)): a single antecedent nocturnal hypoglycaemic event blunts daytime symptom recognition and adrenergic response for 24–48 h, creating the well-described vicious cycle of nocturnal hypoglycaemia → impaired awareness → further nocturnal events. Sleep is thus both a permissive state for prolonged hypoglycaemia and a trigger for next-day HAAF.

### Real-world overnight glycaemia and sleep fragmentation in T1D

CGM-era observational data describe a heterogeneous picture. Well-controlled adult T1D cohorts on hybrid closed-loop or sensor-augmented pumps achieve ~50–70 % overnight time-in-range (3.9–10 mmol/L), with progressively higher overnight TIR than daytime TIR as algorithms have matured; multiple-daily-injection cohorts perform less well. Reutrakul et al.'s 2016 systematic review and meta-analysis (22 studies) found that adults with T1D self-report poorer subjective sleep quality than controls, that those sleeping > 6 h have lower HbA1c than those sleeping ≤ 6 h, and that children with T1D sleep ~26 min less per night than controls. CGM alarm awakenings are a substantial and under-quantified contributor to fragmentation: published series report median 1–4 alarm-driven awakenings per night in unoptimised settings, with high-tone glucose alarms producing sleep-stage transitions even when the patient does not consciously wake. Fear of nocturnal hypoglycaemia is reported by 50–75 % of T1D adults and parents of children with T1D, and predicts intentional pre-sleep hyperglycaemia targeting that elevates HbA1c independently of objective glycaemic exposure. (*Caveat:* these are observational, with strong selection bias towards motivated CGM users; the "fragmentation burden" of T1D in unselected populations is largely unmeasured.)

### Obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA) in diabetes

OSA — recurrent upper-airway collapse producing intermittent hypoxia and arousals — is causally linked to insulin resistance through sympathetic activation, oxidative stress, and HPA-axis dysregulation. The prevalence in type 2 diabetes is well-characterised at 50–70 %. In T1D the data are sparser but increasingly suggest a similar burden: Reutrakul's meta-analysis estimated a pooled OSA prevalence of ~52 % in adults with T1D, far above general-population rates of ~10–25 %, with moderate-to-severe OSA trending towards higher HbA1c. Mechanisms include obesity (increasingly common in T1D), autonomic neuropathy (which may impair upper-airway tone and ventilatory drive), and possibly diabetes-specific changes in pharyngeal soft-tissue glycation. (*Caveat:* heterogeneity in diagnostic methods (polysomnography vs home testing vs questionnaires) inflates the confidence interval, and selection bias in sleep-clinic cohorts is large.)

### Shift work, social jet lag, and chronic circadian misalignment

Forced desynchrony protocols — in which subjects live on a 28-h "day" so their behavioural schedule rotates against the central clock — show that circadian misalignment alone, with adequate total sleep, raises postprandial glucose ~6 % and reduces insulin response to a meal ~32 % (Scheer 2009, *PNAS*; Buxton 2012, *Sci Transl Med*). Real-world shift workers have a ~40 % relative-risk increase for type 2 diabetes; in T1D, observational data link rotating shifts and large weekday–weekend bedtime shifts ("social jet lag" (chronic misalignment between social schedule and internal circadian clock) > 2 h) to higher HbA1c and more glycaemic variability (Larcher 2016, *Eur J Endocrinol*). Mechanistically, peripheral-clock misalignment with the central clock is the leading hypothesis, with direct β-cell clock disruption demonstrated in murine models.

### Sleep extension as an intervention

Pilot trials of sleep extension (typically increasing time-in-bed by 60–90 min/night for 1–6 weeks) in habitually short-sleeping adults without diabetes show modest improvements in fasting glucose, HOMA-IR, and appetite-hormone profiles, and meaningful reductions in spontaneous energy intake (~270 kcal/day in So-ngern 2019). Direct extension trials in T1D are essentially absent; the available evidence supports sleep hygiene and the optimisation of CGM alarm thresholds (e.g. wider hypo-alert bands, smart predictive alarms, partner-shared alerts) as plausible — but unproven — adjuncts to glycaemic management.

<a name="alcohol"></a>
## 19. Alcohol -- ethanol metabolism, gluconeogenesis, and delayed hypoglycemia

Acute and especially delayed hypoglycemia after alcohol intake is one of the most clinically important and disproportionately fatal complications of T1D. Population-based registry and case-series data identify alcohol as a contributing factor in 19-25% of severe hypoglycemia events in adults with T1D, and as the dominant risk factor for nocturnal hypoglycemia-associated death (so-called "dead-in-bed" syndrome) in young adults ([Tetzschner et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29931823/); [Richardson et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16306564/); [Turner et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11679452/)). The danger is not the acute pharmacological action of ethanol itself but a temporally extended pharmacodynamic shadow: ethanol selectively suppresses one of the two arms of hepatic glucose production (gluconeogenesis), blunts adrenergic and growth-hormone counterregulation, and impairs the cortical recognition of neuroglycopenic symptoms -- a triad that converges 6-12 h after the drinking episode, typically during sleep, and that operates orthogonally to insulin pharmacokinetics so that nominal "correct" insulin dosing remains profoundly hypoglycemic in the post-alcohol state ([Avogaro et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15189193/); [Kerr et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2227124/)).

### Mechanism

#### Hepatic ethanol metabolism and the NADH/NAD+ ratio

Ingested ethanol is absorbed nearly completely from the proximal small intestine and reaches the hepatocyte via the portal circulation. Two oxidative pathways dominate hepatic clearance: (i) cytosolic alcohol dehydrogenase (ADH; primarily ADH1B and ADH1C in humans) converts ethanol to acetaldehyde with reduction of NAD+ to NADH, and (ii) the microsomal ethanol-oxidising system, principally cytochrome P450 2E1 (CYP2E1) in the smooth endoplasmic reticulum, which contributes ~10% of clearance at moderate doses but is induced 5-10-fold during chronic intake. Acetaldehyde is then oxidised to acetate by mitochondrial aldehyde dehydrogenase (ALDH2), again reducing NAD+ to NADH. Acetate exits the liver and is oxidised peripherally via acetyl-CoA. Each ethanol molecule oxidised therefore generates two NADH equivalents in the hepatocyte cytosol and mitochondrial matrix, while consuming oxygen and producing no net ATP gain to the gluconeogenic precursor pool ([Lieber, 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15303622/); [Steiner et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4693236/)).

The cytosolic NADH/NAD+ ratio in the hepatocyte rises by a factor of approximately 2-5 during active ethanol oxidation at moderate doses (0.5-0.8 g/kg), with mitochondrial-matrix NADH/NAD+ rising more modestly. This redox shift is the proximate biochemical lesion responsible for the gluconeogenic blockade described next, and its magnitude scales near-linearly with hepatic ethanol flux up to the saturation of ADH at venous alcohol concentrations of ~10 mmol/L (~46 mg/dL; well below typical recreational intoxication levels), at which point hepatic ethanol clearance becomes near-zero-order at ~0.1-0.15 g/kg/h ([Lieber, 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15303622/)).

#### Inhibition of hepatic gluconeogenesis

The cytosolic step that controls flux from three-carbon precursors into hepatic glucose output is the LDH-catalysed equilibrium **lactate + NAD+ ⇌ pyruvate + NADH**. With NADH chronically elevated by ethanol oxidation, the equilibrium is driven toward lactate; pyruvate availability for entry into mitochondrial pyruvate carboxylase (the gateway to gluconeogenesis) collapses. The same redox shift drives the **malate ⇌ oxaloacetate** and **glycerol-3-phosphate ⇌ dihydroxyacetone phosphate** equilibria toward their reduced forms, removing oxaloacetate (the PEPCK substrate) and DHAP (a triose-phosphate gluconeogenic intermediate) from the pool. The net effect is a near-complete blockade of gluconeogenesis from lactate, alanine, glycerol, and glutamine, even though the upstream proteolytic and lipolytic precursor flux is preserved ([Siler et al., 1998](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9519750/); [van de Wiel, 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15233823/)).

In a tracer-clamped study of healthy adults receiving an intravenous ethanol infusion to a steady plasma concentration of 22 mmol/L (~100 mg/dL), endogenous glucose production fell from 2.05 ± 0.12 to 1.05 ± 0.09 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ (a ~50% suppression) within 90 min, while gluconeogenic flux estimated by deuterium incorporation fell to <30% of baseline ([Siler et al., 1998](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9519750/)). Comparable suppression of EGP (endogenous glucose production, the sum of hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis) (~45-55%) has been replicated in T1D adults at moderate oral doses (0.6-0.9 g/kg as wine or vodka) using stable-isotope tracer methods, with an exposure-time of 4-12 h depending on the carbohydrate content of the beverage and concomitant feeding ([Avogaro et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15189193/); [Turner et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11679452/)).

#### Glycogenolysis is preserved -- the fed-state vs glycogen-depleted distinction

Ethanol does *not* inhibit glycogen phosphorylase or the hepatic glucose-6-phosphatase step. Hepatic glucose output from glycogen breakdown therefore proceeds normally during ethanol oxidation. This is the single most important clinical-pharmacology distinction in alcohol-related T1D hypoglycemia: in the postprandial / well-fed state with replete hepatic glycogen (~70-100 g in a healthy adult liver), glycogenolysis can sustain whole-body glucose flux for several hours despite gluconeogenic blockade, and ethanol intake is rarely acutely hypoglycemic. In the post-absorptive, fasted, or post-exercise state with depleted glycogen (<20-30 g hepatic glycogen, characteristic after an overnight fast or prolonged exertion), glucose production becomes increasingly gluconeogenesis-dependent within 12-18 h of fasting; ethanol intake under these conditions removes the dominant source of EGP and produces severe and sustained hypoglycemia ([Siler et al., 1998](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9519750/); [Steiner et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4693236/)). The 6-12 h delayed nocturnal hypoglycemia after evening alcohol in T1D reflects exactly this transition: glycogen reserves built up from the evening meal cover the early-night hours; once those are depleted (typically 04:00-08:00 in adults retiring at 23:00-01:00), the gluconeogenically-blocked liver can no longer compensate for circulating basal insulin, and BG falls precipitously ([Turner et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11679452/); [Richardson et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16306564/)).

#### Counterregulatory hormone blunting

Ethanol acutely impairs the hormonal response to falling glucose in non-diabetic and T1D adults alike. Under controlled hyperinsulinemic-hypoglycemic clamp conditions (target plasma glucose 2.8-3.0 mmol/L) with prior moderate ethanol intake (~0.75 g/kg), the adrenaline response is reduced by 35-55%, the growth-hormone response by 50-70%, and the glucagon response by ~30-40%, while the cortisol response is approximately preserved ([Kerr et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2227124/); [Avogaro et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15189193/)). The mechanism is incompletely resolved: a central effect on hypothalamic glucose-sensing GABA-ergic and glutamatergic interneurons (potentiated by ethanol-enhanced GABA-A receptor activity) is the leading hypothesis, with additional contributions from ethanol-induced suppression of pancreatic α-cell glucagon secretion and impaired adrenal medullary responsiveness ([Steiner et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4693236/)). The net effect in T1D is twofold: the residual glucagon response (already markedly impaired by autoimmune α-cell dysfunction in established T1D) is further suppressed, and the catecholamine-mediated hepatic glycogenolysis that would normally rescue falling glucose is reduced -- compounding the gluconeogenic blockade.

Critically, ethanol also blunts the recognition of neuroglycopenic and autonomic warning symptoms. In T1D adults under clamp conditions, prior ethanol intake reduced symptom-score increases at PG 2.8 mmol/L by 40-60% versus placebo, even though objective cognitive deficits (reaction time, four-choice tasks) were unchanged or worsened ([Kerr et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2227124/)). This represents a pharmacological superposition of acquired hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure (HAAF) on top of the structural hypoglycemia unawareness already characteristic of long-standing T1D, and it operates regardless of whether the patient feels intoxicated.

#### Mixed and sweetened beverages -- the biphasic glycemic profile

Beer (carb content 10-20 g per 330 mL serving), sweet wines, sugar-containing mixers (cola, tonic, fruit juice), and cocktails produce a characteristic biphasic CGM trace in T1D: an early postprandial hyperglycemic excursion 30-90 min after intake driven by the carbohydrate component (often inadequately covered by mealtime insulin because patients reduce dosing in anticipation of "alcohol-induced hypoglycemia"), followed by a late hypoglycemic excursion 6-12 h later from the unopposed sustained ethanol-induced gluconeogenic blockade ([Turner et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11679452/); [Richardson et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16306564/)). Pure-distillate spirits (vodka, gin, whisky) without carbohydrate mixers produce only the late hypoglycemic limb. The clinical implication is that the apparent early adequacy or even hyperglycemia of the BG trace gives no information about the late risk: by the time the gluconeogenic crisis develops, the patient is asleep, ethanol is largely cleared from circulation, the mealtime insulin is gone, and only basal insulin is acting -- against a liver that cannot mount a counterregulatory response.

#### Exercise + alcohol

Aerobic and resistance exercise produce a sustained insulin-sensitising and glycogen-depleting state lasting 24-48 h ([§8](#aerobic-exercise)). When alcohol intake follows exercise (the common "post-game" or "post-run" social pattern), the gluconeogenic blockade is superimposed on already-depleted hepatic glycogen, while the post-exercise insulin sensitisation amplifies the effect of any circulating insulin. The risks are quantitatively additive at minimum and may be supra-additive: small case-series in exercising T1D adults report nocturnal severe hypoglycemia (PG <2.5 mmol/L or requiring third-party assistance) in 30-60% of evening-alcohol nights following same-day moderate-vigorous exercise ([Richardson et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16306564/); [Steiner et al., 2015](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4693236/)).

### Quantitative characterisation

| Quantity | Value | Notes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hepatic ethanol clearance, saturated (above ~10 mmol/L) | ~0.10-0.15 g·kg⁻¹·h⁻¹ (zero-order kinetics) | ADH-saturated; CYP2E1 contribution rises with chronic intake | [Lieber, 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15303622/) |
| Cytosolic hepatocyte NADH/NAD+ ratio shift during oxidation | ~2-5x baseline at 0.5-0.8 g/kg | Drives lactate, malate, glycerol-3-P equilibria toward reduced forms | [Lieber, 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15303622/) |
| Suppression of EGP, IV ethanol clamp at PEth ~22 mmol/L (~100 mg/dL) | ~50% (2.05 -> 1.05 mg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹) | Healthy adults; tracer methods | [Siler et al., 1998](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9519750/) |
| Suppression of gluconeogenesis (deuterium-incorporation), same protocol | >70% suppression of GNG flux | Glycogenolysis preserved | [Siler et al., 1998](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9519750/) |
| EGP suppression in T1D, oral 0.6-0.9 g/kg | ~45-55%, lasting 4-12 h | Tracer methods; varies with beverage and feeding | [Avogaro et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15189193/) |
| Time-to-trough BG after evening alcohol, T1D (CGM-detected) | 6-12 h post-intake; typically 04:00-08:00 nadir | Glycogen-depletion-gated transition | [Turner et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11679452/); [Richardson et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16306564/) |
| Adrenaline response to clamp hypoglycemia, post-ethanol | -35 to -55% vs placebo | Hyperinsulinemic-hypoglycemic clamp, 0.75 g/kg ethanol | [Kerr et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2227124/) |
| Growth-hormone response, same protocol | -50 to -70% vs placebo | Mechanism includes hypothalamic GABA-A potentiation | [Kerr et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2227124/) |
| Glucagon response, same protocol | -30 to -40% vs placebo | T1D α-cell dysfunction further compounded | [Avogaro et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15189193/) |
| Hypoglycemia-symptom score reduction, post-ethanol | -40 to -60% at PG 2.8 mmol/L | Cognitive deficits unchanged or worse | [Kerr et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2227124/) |
| Severe nocturnal hypoglycemia incidence, evening alcohol + same-day exercise | ~30-60% of nights | Small case-series; supra-additive interaction | [Richardson et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16306564/) |
| Alcohol-attributable fraction, severe T1D hypoglycemia events (registry data) | ~19-25% | Includes acute and delayed events | [Tetzschner et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29931823/) |

### T1D-specific aspects

1. **Loss of glucagon counterregulation is multiplicative with ethanol effect.** Established T1D is characterised by absent or markedly impaired glucagon secretion in response to hypoglycemia, secondary to autoimmune α-cell dysfunction and loss of the paracrine β-cell-to-α-cell switch-off signal. Ethanol further suppresses the residual glucagon response by 30-40%; in patients with long-duration T1D the practical effect is near-complete loss of the glucagon arm during alcohol exposure, leaving adrenaline as the sole rapid counterregulatory pathway -- which ethanol then blunts by 35-55% ([Avogaro et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15189193/)).

2. **Sweetened beverages produce false reassurance.** The early hyperglycemic excursion from carbohydrate-containing alcoholic drinks is often interpreted by patients as evidence that "the alcohol is not lowering my BG", leading to upward correction with rapid-acting insulin in the late evening. The corrective bolus then peaks in plasma 1-2 h later, exactly when hepatic glycogen reserves are depleting and gluconeogenesis is blocked -- a common antecedent of severe nocturnal hypoglycemia ([Turner et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11679452/)).

3. **Symptom misattribution.** Neuroglycopenic symptoms (confusion, ataxia, dysarthria, visual disturbance) overlap exactly with intoxication, and ethanol-blunted autonomic warning symptoms remove the early autonomic warning. Both patient and bystander attribute observed signs to drunkenness; treatment is delayed; severe hypoglycemia or fatality results. This pattern has been repeatedly identified in coronial reviews of "dead-in-bed" cases in young T1D adults ([Tetzschner et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29931823/)).

4. **Inter-individual variability in ADH/ALDH genotype shifts the risk profile.** Polymorphisms in *ADH1B*, *ADH1C*, and (most importantly) *ALDH2* alter hepatic ethanol kinetics and acetaldehyde accumulation. The ALDH2*2 (rs671) loss-of-function allele, present at ~30-50% allele frequency in East Asian populations, causes acetaldehyde accumulation, the cutaneous flushing reaction, and clinically lower self-limited alcohol intake -- and may therefore reduce population-level hypoglycemia exposure even though per-gram ethanol the gluconeogenic blockade and counterregulatory blunting are unchanged or amplified. ADH1B*2 (high-activity) similarly accelerates acetaldehyde accumulation. Pharmacokinetic and gluconeogenic data have been generated almost entirely in European-ancestry cohorts; extrapolation to populations with high ALDH2*2 prevalence requires caution ([van de Wiel, 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15233823/)).

5. **Chronic moderate alcohol intake and HbA1c.** Cross-sectional data in T1D adults show no consistent HbA1c effect of low-to-moderate habitual intake (≤2 standard drinks/day for men, ≤1 for women), but increased glycemic variability and increased reported severe hypoglycemia frequency ([Hirst et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22825066/); [Christiansen et al., 2003](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12881456/)). Heavy intake is associated with worse mean HbA1c, more diabetic ketoacidosis admissions, and pancreatic exocrine insufficiency. The clinical-pharmacology data summarised above derive almost entirely from acute and short-term exposure paradigms -- binge-drinking effects (>0.10 g/dL peak BAC, common in young-adult T1D populations) are mechanistically expected to be more severe but have been less systematically studied for ethical reasons.

<a name="psychological-stress"></a>
## 20. Psychological stress

Psychological stress -- examinations, work conflict, anticipatory anxiety, public speaking, bereavement -- engages the same neuroendocrine effectors that defend against hypoglycaemia (sympathoadrenal medulla, hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis), and an effect on circulating glucose is therefore mechanistically anticipated in type 1 diabetes (T1D), where exogenous insulin cannot dynamically compensate. The clinical reality is more nuanced than the popular shorthand "stress raises blood glucose": ambulatory studies in T1D consistently show heterogeneous, individual-specific glycaemic reactivity, with positive responders (BG rises with hassles) outnumbering negative responders but a large fraction showing no detectable association ([Riazi et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15536020/); [Gonder-Frederick et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2226381/)). The chronic-stress and behavioural pathways -- diabetes distress, depression, sleep disruption, missed boluses, comfort eating -- are at least as relevant for HbA1c trajectories as direct hormonal effects, and they interact bidirectionally with glycaemic control ([Snoek, Bremmer & Hermanns, 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25995123/)).

### Acute stress biology

#### Sympathoadrenal axis

A salient psychogenic stimulus activates the locus coeruleus and rostral ventrolateral medulla, driving preganglionic sympathetic outflow to the adrenal medulla and to peripheral postganglionic terminals. Within seconds to minutes, plasma adrenaline (epinephrine) rises 5- to 10-fold above resting (~30-50 pg/mL → 200-500 pg/mL during a moderately intense laboratory stressor) and noradrenaline (norepinephrine) rises 2- to 4-fold ([Goldstein, 2010 -- review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19698447/)). Adrenaline binds β2-adrenoceptors on hepatocytes (activating glycogen phosphorylase via cAMP/PKA) and α-adrenoceptors on pancreatic islets, simultaneously stimulating hepatic glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis and -- in non-diabetic subjects -- suppressing insulin secretion. In skeletal muscle, β2 stimulation acutely activates glycogen phosphorylase locally but reduces insulin-mediated glucose uptake. The integrated effect in healthy subjects is a transient 1-2 mmol/L rise in plasma glucose blunted within 30-60 min by a counter-regulatory insulin response (see §11). In T1D the brake on glucose efflux is missing, so the same catecholamine surge produces a larger and more sustained glycaemic excursion.

#### HPA axis

Within the same minutes-to-hours window, hypothalamic paraventricular nucleus neurons release corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH); CRH stimulates anterior-pituitary corticotrophs to secrete adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH); ACTH drives adrenal-cortical synthesis of cortisol. Salivary or plasma cortisol typically rises 30-50% above baseline 20-40 min after a standardised psychosocial stressor, with the Trier Social Stress Test as the canonical paradigm ([Kirschbaum, Pirke & Hellhammer, 1993](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8255414/); [Dickerson & Kemeny, 2004 -- meta-analysis](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15122924/)). Cortisol acts genomically through the glucocorticoid receptor at glucocorticoid-response elements in the *PCK1* and *G6PC* promoters (PEPCK and glucose-6-phosphatase induction; cf. §2), increasing hepatic gluconeogenic capacity over 30-120 min, and post-receptor in muscle and adipose tissue to attenuate insulin signalling. The cortisol contribution to a single stress episode is therefore delayed relative to catecholamines but more durable.

### Acute glycaemic effects in T1D

Wiesli et al. (2005) administered the Trier Social Stress Test to 20 T1D patients in two states. In the **fasting state**, neither plasma glucose nor counter-regulatory hormones changed significantly during stress despite robust cardiovascular activation (heart rate 80 → 99 bpm, systolic blood pressure 122 → 152 mmHg). In the **postprandial state** (test administered after a standardised meal and matched insulin bolus), the same stressor produced a clinically meaningful 45-min delay in postprandial glucose decline, with a cumulative incremental AUC ~30% higher than the non-stress condition ([Wiesli et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16043731/)). The differential is consistent with adrenaline's known suppression of insulin-mediated glucose disposal acting on top of an already-elevated nutrient load. The same mechanism is implicated in stress-related delayed gastric emptying and in transient β-adrenergic insulin resistance ([Goldstein, 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19698447/)).

Earlier laboratory work ([Gonder-Frederick et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2226381/); [Halford, Cuddihy & Mortimer, 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2226382/)) found inconsistent direct stress-glucose effects in basal conditions, mirroring the fasting arm of Wiesli. The empirical pattern most defensible from these studies is: in T1D, acute psychological stress reliably perturbs glycaemia mainly when it coincides with active nutrient absorption or active insulin action, where the catecholamine-mediated reduction in tissue insulin sensitivity has a substrate to act on.

### Variable directionality -- the autonomic-profile caveat

The cliché "stress raises BG" is wrong often enough in T1D that it should not be relied on for self-management heuristics. Riazi, Pickup and Bradley (2004) followed 54 T1D adults over 21 days with daily hassle reports and four-times-daily capillary glucose. Within-subject stress-glucose correlations spanned r = -0.79 to +0.58, with about 20% of participants showing a positive same-day association (BG rises with hassles), about 4% a negative association, and the majority no detectable signal. "Stress-reactive" individuals (significant within-person correlation in either direction) had significantly higher HbA1c (P < 0.05) than non-reactive participants ([Riazi et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15536020/)). Direction and magnitude of stress-reactivity track autonomic phenotype ("fight" vs "freeze" responder profiles), coping style, and likely the hormonal balance between catecholamines (hyperglycaemic in T1D) and stress-induced behavioural changes -- reduced food intake, restlessness, increased physical activity -- some of which lower BG. The clinical implication is that stress-glycaemia must be characterised individually before any management response is justified.

A separate confound is **reverse causality**: hyperglycaemia itself induces irritability, fatigue, cognitive slowing, and low mood, while hypoglycaemia produces anxiety and dysphoria ([Sommerfield et al., 2004](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15226212/)). Cross-sectional studies that report a BG-mood correlation cannot distinguish the direction of effect, and longitudinal CGM-mood studies that adjust for activity, meals and time-of-day generally find smaller stress-glycaemia coefficients than naive analyses suggest.

### Chronic stress and T1D

#### Hormonal pathway

Sustained activation of the HPA axis -- weeks to months of work overload, caregiving burden, chronic illness, financial strain -- produces moderately elevated daytime cortisol, flattened diurnal rhythm, and (in some phenotypes) eventual hypocortisolism. Chronically elevated cortisol increases hepatic gluconeogenic capacity and post-receptor insulin resistance (see §2 and §11), shifts visceral adipose distribution, and elevates fasting glucose and HbA1c. In T1D the dominant glucose-lowering signal remains exogenous insulin, so the practical effect of chronic cortisol elevation is an upward drift in basal-insulin and total-daily-insulin requirements during prolonged stress periods ([Surwit, Schneider & Feinglos, 1992 -- review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1425110/)).

#### Behavioural pathway

The behavioural arm is empirically larger than the direct hormonal arm. Chronic stress disrupts sleep (see §18), erodes adherence to bolus timing and dose calculation, increases reliance on convenience foods with poor postprandial profiles, and reduces planned physical activity. In adolescents and emerging adults the loss of structure is particularly costly: missed boluses are the single largest modifiable contributor to elevated HbA1c in this group ([Hagger et al., 2016 -- review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26748793/)).

### Diabetes distress and burnout

**Diabetes distress** describes the affective burden specifically arising from living with diabetes -- the unrelenting demands of carbohydrate counting, dosing decisions, fear of hypoglycaemia, fear of long-term complications, perceived clinician unsupportiveness, and family or interpersonal frictions. The 17-item Diabetes Distress Scale (DDS-17) operationalises four subscales: emotional burden, physician-related distress, regimen-related distress, and interpersonal distress ([Polonsky et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15735199/)). In adult T1D cohorts, ~40% report moderate diabetes distress (DDS-17 mean ≥2) and ~20% report high distress (≥3) at any given time, with substantial within-person variation across the disease course ([Fisher et al., 2016 -- T1D longitudinal study](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27118163/)). Distress is statistically and conceptually distinct from clinical depression: in the 3D study, 84% of patients with moderate-to-high diabetes distress did not meet criteria for major depressive disorder, and 67% of those with major depressive disorder also had diabetes distress ([Fisher, Gonzalez & Polonsky, 2014 -- review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24606397/)).

Diabetes distress correlates with HbA1c at small-to-moderate effect sizes (r ≈ 0.2-0.3 cross-sectionally) and longitudinally predicts HbA1c trajectory more reliably than depression scores do, even at sub-clinical distress levels ([Fisher et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19837786/)). "Diabetes burnout" -- a clinical descriptor partially overlapping high distress, characterised by detachment, exhaustion, and disengagement from self-care -- is increasingly recognised but lacks a fully validated separate instrument; current measurement leans on the high end of the DDS-17 and qualitative criteria ([Hilliard et al., 2016 -- T1D burnout review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27086134/)).

### Depression and the bidirectional T1D-depression link

Adults with diabetes have approximately twice the odds of clinical depression compared with non-diabetic adults (OR ≈ 2.0, 95% CI 1.8-2.2; Anderson et al. 2001 meta-analysis of 42 studies, mixed T1D and T2D; [PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11375373/)); estimates restricted to T1D suggest a 2- to 3-fold elevation versus background population, though prevalence figures vary widely with the assessment instrument used ([Roy & Lloyd, 2012 -- T1D depression review](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22858441/)). A persistent measurement caveat is that several Diagnostic and Statistical Manual depression items (fatigue, weight change, sleep disturbance, concentration deficit) overlap with symptoms of unrecognised hyperglycaemia or hypoglycaemia, leading to over-diagnosis when self-report instruments are used without glycaemic context ([Snoek, Bremmer & Hermanns, 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25995123/)).

The depression-glycaemia link is bidirectional. Depression predicts subsequent worsening of glycaemic control via reduced self-care, missed appointments, and altered eating and activity patterns; glycaemic deterioration in turn predicts subsequent worsening of depression scores via direct mood effects of dysglycaemia and via the demoralising experience of perceived treatment failure. Severe hypoglycaemic episodes are independently associated with subsequent depressive symptoms and with fear-of-hypoglycaemia anxiety that constrains insulin dosing.

### CGM-era observational data: small effect sizes after adjustment

Continuous glucose monitoring combined with ecological momentary assessment of mood and stress has refined the older cross-sectional picture. When meals, insulin doses, physical activity and time-of-day are adjusted for, the residual within-person association between perceived stress and CGM-derived glucose is small (typical effect size corresponding to a ~0.2-0.5 mmol/L shift per standard-deviation increase in same-hour stress), and is not consistently positive across individuals. The take-away is that population-level "stress raises BG" coefficients in T1D are real but quantitatively modest once the confounders that travel with stress (skipped meals, comfort eating, missed boluses, sedentary behaviour) are accounted for. Most of the clinical impact of stress on T1D glycaemia operates through these behavioural channels rather than through unmediated hormonal flux.

### Interventions

Behavioural interventions targeting the cognitive-emotional layer of T1D self-management have small-to-moderate effects on HbA1c and larger effects on distress and quality-of-life outcomes. Cognitive-behavioural therapy adapted for diabetes reduces distress and depressive symptoms (mean reduction ~0.4-0.6 SD) and produces small HbA1c reductions (~0.3 percentage points) sustained at 6-12 months ([Uchendu & Blake, 2017 -- meta-analysis](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27933671/)). Mindfulness-based interventions reduce salivary cortisol output and perceived stress at moderate effect size, with small HbA1c effects in mixed T1D/T2D samples; T1D-specific evidence is sparse and is summarised under [§10b -- Relaxation, yoga, and meditation](#relaxation-yoga). Structured problem-solving and motivational-interviewing protocols, peer-support and group-based approaches, and integrated behavioural-medicine co-management within diabetes clinics are increasingly recommended in international guidelines.

<a name="temperature-climate"></a>
## 21. Temperature and climate

Ambient and tissue temperature is one of the most clinically conspicuous yet under-quantified modifiers of subcutaneous (SC) insulin pharmacokinetics in T1D. Patients routinely report patterns that are reproducible across decades of physiology: a hot shower or sauna shortly after a meal bolus precipitates hypoglycemia within 30-60 min; injecting insulin into a chilled abdomen on a winter morning blunts and delays peak action; a febrile illness amplifies the unpredictability of basal-bolus dosing; and an insulin pen left on a car dashboard at 45 °C silently loses potency, presenting later as unexplained hyperglycemia or even ketoacidosis. The physiological substrate is simple - SC insulin must traverse interstitium, dissociate from hexameric/dimeric storage forms, and reach capillary beds before entering the systemic circulation - and every step is sensitive to local blood flow, tissue temperature, and the chemical stability of the depot. *Caveat: Most quantitative human evidence dates from regular human insulin and NPH (1979-1995); modern rapid analogs (lispro, aspart, glulisine, faster aspart) and ultra-long basals (degludec, glargine U300) carry smaller absolute temperature effects because their kinetics are less limited by hexamer dissociation and SC blood flow, but the directionality and clinical relevance persist ([Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/); [Pitt et al., 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33193089/)).*

### Subcutaneous blood flow as the rate-limiting step

Healthy human skin and SC adipose tissue exhibit one of the body's widest dynamic ranges in regional perfusion. Dermal blood flow can vary roughly 10-fold between full vasoconstriction (~0.1-0.5 mL/100 g/min in cold-stressed acral skin) and full thermoregulatory vasodilation (4-7 mL/100 g/min during heat stress, with peaks of >150 mL/100 g/min in glabrous skin via arteriovenous anastomoses). SC adipose perfusion has a narrower but still meaningful range (1-10 mL/100 g/min). Because the absorption half-time of soluble (regular) insulin in steady-state conditions is ~50-90 min and is dominated by transport from interstitium to capillary, even modest perfusion changes translate directly into changes in plasma insulin onset, peak, and duration ([Berger et al., 1979](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/488572/); [Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/)). Rapid analogs are less perfusion-limited because their dissociation from hexameric form is faster, but at the population level absorption variability remains 15-25 % CV (coefficient of variation, intra-individual variability %) intra-individually and >50 % inter-individually for any given SC insulin in any given site - and acute thermal perturbations can exceed that baseline noise.

Cold exposure adds two further mechanisms beyond vasoconstriction. First, local tissue temperature directly slows insulin diffusion and dissociation kinetics (a typical Arrhenius effect of ~2-3 % per °C over the physiological range). Second, shivering thermogenesis recruits skeletal-muscle glucose uptake (insulin-independent but additive with circulating insulin) and elevates catecholamines, producing a paradoxical mixed glycemic response in which a cold-induced absorption delay can be partly offset by enhanced peripheral glucose disposal once the insulin finally arrives.

### Heat → vasodilation → faster absorption: the sauna paradigm

The classical demonstration is [Koivisto's 1980 sauna study](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7000239/) (BMJ 280:1411-1413), in which ten insulin-dependent patients received their morning soluble (regular) insulin and were then exposed to two 25-min sessions in a Finnish sauna at 85 °C dry heat. The mean insulin disappearance rate from the SC depot rose by ~110 % vs. the room-temperature control day (i.e., approximately 2-fold faster, p<0.01), and plasma glucose 2-3 h after injection was 3.0-3.3 mmol/L (54-60 mg/dL) lower on the sauna day (p<0.05). The fall in glucose was tightly proportional to the increase in absorption, implicating a pharmacokinetic - not pharmacodynamic - mechanism. A confirmatory chamber study at moderate heat ([Koivisto et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7010077/), Metabolism 30:402-405) found that simply raising ambient temperature from 20 °C to 35 °C accelerated SC insulin disappearance by 50-60 % over 4 h, with plasma glucose 1-2 mmol/L lower at the warmer temperature - a dose-response relationship between skin/SC temperature and absorption rate. Local heating without systemic heat stress (e.g., warm packs over the injection site, warm-water immersion of the limb) reproduces the effect, and modern infusion-set studies confirm a ~30-60 % acceleration of basal insulin delivery from CSII (continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion, i.e., insulin pump) pumps with skin warming. Hot showers and warm baths within 30-60 min of a bolus are therefore a real, reproducible hypoglycemia trigger.

### Cold injection and reduced ambient temperature

The cold side of the curve is less well-quantified but consistent in direction. Reducing skin temperature from 32 °C to ~22 °C decreases SC insulin disappearance by ~30-50 % in human and porcine models ([Berger et al., 1979](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/488572/); reviewed by [Heinemann, 2002](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12450450/)), prolongs time-to-peak, and lowers peak plasma concentration with a corresponding loss of postprandial control. A second practical phenomenon is "cold-pen sting": insulin from a refrigerator-fresh pen (4-8 °C) is biologically intact but the cold bolus locally vasoconstricts the depot, transiently delays absorption, and is more painful at injection. Letting pens reach room temperature before use (~20 min) eliminates this effect. In outdoor winter exposure, peripheral skin in the abdomen or arm can drop to 18-25 °C even under clothing during prolonged cold; switching the rotation from a chilled limb to a warmer covered site (e.g., abdomen vs. exposed thigh) is the simplest mitigation.

### Fever as endogenous heat - amplifying §17 illness risk

Febrile illness combines two independent BG perturbations. The illness itself drives counterregulatory hormones, ketogenesis, and insulin resistance (see §17). Independently, the rise in core and SC temperature accelerates SC insulin absorption: a 1-2 °C fever produces a measurable (~10-25 %) increase in disappearance rate, narrowing the time window between insulin onset and peak. The clinical consequence is that the same dose-correction algorithm that worked at baseline can now both peak earlier (raising hypo risk in the 1-2 h post-bolus window) and run out faster (raising hyper risk later). This is one mechanism by which sick-day glycemia becomes "all-or-nothing": small dose changes are amplified through faster, sharper kinetics on top of an already hormonally perturbed background.

### Insulin product thermal stability and storage

Insulin is a small protein (5.8 kDa) stabilized by hexameric Zn²⁺ coordination and a small number of disulfide bonds, and its in-vial chemistry is sensitive to both heat and freeze damage. Manufacturer labeling for unopened cartridges and vials is 2-8 °C until the printed expiry; once in use, all major rapid analogs (lispro, aspart, glulisine, faster aspart) are labeled stable for ≤28 days (some up to 42 days for specific products) at room temperature ≤30 °C. Above ~30-32 °C, both potency and chemical purity deteriorate progressively. The Vimalavathini-Gitanjali human-insulin study ([Indian J Med Res, 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19797814/), 130:166-169) showed a 14-18 % loss of potency by HPLC after 28 days at 32-37 °C across three formulations. The 2023 Cochrane systematic review ([Richter et al., Cochrane Database Syst Rev, 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37930742/), CD015385) consolidated manufacturer and independent data and concluded that unopened vials and cartridges retain clinically acceptable potency for up to 6 months at 25 °C and up to 2 months at 37 °C, with oscillation between 25 °C and 37 °C tolerated for up to 3 months - findings highly relevant to storage in low-resource and tropical settings (e.g., the unglazed-clay-pot work). Freezing irreversibly disrupts hexameric structure: a single freeze-thaw cycle can produce visible turbidity and substantial activity loss, and any insulin that has frozen must be discarded. The functional clinical signature of degraded insulin is unpredictable, often blunted glycemic response despite apparently correct dosing - a pattern that should prompt a fresh vial/pen before a major dose escalation.

### Exercise-induced muscle warmth and absorption from active limbs

Local hyperthermia is also an automatic byproduct of exercise. Working muscle generates heat at 5-15 W/kg, raising muscle and overlying SC temperature by 1-3 °C within 20-30 min and increasing local SC blood flow several-fold. The classic injection-site work from [Koivisto and Felig, 1980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6444267/) (Ann Intern Med 92:59-61) showed that under resting conditions, abdominal absorption of regular insulin was ~86 % faster than thigh and ~30 % faster than arm; running converts the resting-thigh depot into the body's fastest depot, and arm exercise does the same for an arm depot. Reported absorption acceleration from exercise of the limb containing the depot is in the range of ~50-100 % above the resting rate for soluble insulins, with a smaller but still significant effect (~20-50 %) for rapid analogs. This is one component of the widely observed hypoglycemia risk in the 60-90 min after a meal bolus in patients who exercise the injected limb (see §10).

### Hot weather, sweating, and dehydration

Beyond the depot, sustained hot-weather exposure drives behavioral and circulatory changes that distort BG. Heat-induced sweat losses of 0.5-1.5 L/h reduce plasma volume; the resulting hemoconcentration produces an apparent rise in measured glucose for a fixed glucose mass and concentrates ketones if insulin is also under-delivered. Heat exposure also reduces appetite, alters meal timing, and increases the appeal of sugar-containing rehydration fluids - all hard for fixed insulin algorithms to track. People with T1D have measurably impaired thermoregulation: lower skin blood flow and sweating responses during heat exposure, especially with longer disease duration, autonomic neuropathy, or poor glycemic control ([Kenny et al., Temperature, 2016](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4861190/)). The combination raises both heat-illness risk (impaired heat dissipation) and BG instability (faster SC absorption + dehydration concentration effect + behavioral disruption).

### Climate adaptation - tropical and arctic acclimatization

Chronic acclimatization to heat (≥10-14 days) increases plasma volume by ~10-15 %, lowers the sweating threshold, and improves cardiovascular tolerance, partly normalizing the acute heat response. Cold acclimatization is more modest in humans but includes reduced shivering threshold and improved peripheral vasoconstrictor tone. Long-stay travelers and migrants typically require an iterative basal/ICR/ISF re-tune over 1-3 weeks rather than a single day-1 adjustment; the evidence base is largely observational and case-series.

### CGM and pump hardware in extreme climates

Sensor and infusion hardware add a further layer of climate sensitivity. Common CGM platforms (Dexcom G6/G7, Libre 2/3) carry an operating range of approximately 10-42 °C; below ~10 °C the electrochemistry of the sensor wire slows and reported glucose lags or reads low, and above ~42 °C drift and signal loss become common. Adhesive failure is the dominant practical mode of failure in heat: sweat and SC oil reduce hold time from 7-14 days to 2-4 days, and overpatching, antiperspirant skin-prep, or cyanoacrylate-style adhesives are necessary for endurance, tropical, or military use. Insulin pump reservoirs are subject to the same thermal stability constraints as vials and pens; in addition, insulin in tubing exposed to direct sunlight on a beach can exceed 40 °C within minutes, with both potency loss and air-bubble formation that disrupts delivery. Cold has the inverse profile: sub-zero exposure can freeze the insulin in pump tubing and sensor connectors, and operating below ~5 °C is outside specification.
