<a name="injection-site"></a>
## 22. Injection site, technique, and lipohypertrophy

Where in the body insulin is deposited, by what route, with what needle, and in what state of underlying tissue together constitute one of the largest -- and most underappreciated -- sources of pharmacokinetic variability in subcutaneous insulin therapy. Despite decades of pharmacology research demonstrating regional differences of 2--3-fold in absorption rate and decades of injection-technique surveys showing that most patients do not rotate sites correctly, injection-site biology is rarely the first hypothesis when clinicians encounter unexplained glycemic variability. Two related but distinct phenomena dominate the clinical picture: the geometry of where the dose lands (subcutaneous depot vs. inadvertent intramuscular vs. lipohypertrophic tissue), and the longitudinal evolution of injected tissue under repeated insulin exposure (lipohypertrophy, fibrosis, and microvascular remodeling). These factors interact with insulin formulation (regular vs. rapid analogs vs. long-acting analogs), exercise of the underlying muscle group, ambient temperature, and patient body composition to produce day-to-day absorption profiles that can vary by ±50% or more even when stated dose is identical.

### Subcutaneous depot anatomy

The intended target tissue is the subcutaneous adipose layer beneath the dermis but superficial to the deep fascia and skeletal muscle. Skin thickness in adults is remarkably constant across body regions and across BMI -- approximately 1.9--2.4 mm at the abdomen, thigh, arm, and buttock -- whereas overlying subcutaneous fat thickness varies dramatically with sex, BMI, and anatomical region (typically 5--35 mm at the abdomen but as little as 3--8 mm at the thigh in lean adults and children) ([Gibney et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20602605/)). Once delivered, insulin forms a roughly spherical depot at the needle tip; absorption proceeds by diffusion of monomer/dimer species across the capillary endothelium of the surrounding subcutaneous microvasculature, with the local blood-flow rate -- not depot volume per se -- as the dominant rate-limiting step in most physiological conditions ([Hildebrandt, 1991](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/1914533/); see also [§7](#insulin-pharmacology) for hexamer dissociation kinetics and [§21](#temperature) for thermal modulation of regional blood flow).

### Site comparison: abdomen, arm, thigh, buttock

Classic comparative pharmacokinetic studies, predominantly performed with regular human insulin, demonstrated a clear hierarchy: abdomen > upper arm > thigh ≈ buttock for both rate of absorption and peak concentration. With regular human insulin, the difference in time-to-peak between abdomen and thigh was on the order of 30--60 min, and the abdominal site cleared the depot 86% faster than the thigh and 30% faster than the arm ([Koivisto and Felig, 1980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6444267/); [Bantle et al., 1993](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/16/12/1592/17362/)). Vora and colleagues quantified the underlying mechanism using simultaneous measurement of ¹²⁵I-soluble-insulin absorption and laser-Doppler subcutaneous blood flow: blood-flow rates were highest at the abdominal wall, intermediate at the upper arm, and lowest at the anterior thigh, and the rank order of absorption rate matched the rank order of blood flow ([Vora et al., 1992](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/15/11/1484/21271/)).

With rapid-acting analogs (lispro, aspart, glulisine), regional differences are smaller but still measurable. In a 6-way crossover comparison of abdominal, deltoid, and femoral injection of insulin lispro and regular insulin, abdominal administration produced the highest C_max at the earliest t_max for both formulations; for lispro the t_max difference between abdomen and thigh was approximately 10--20 min, vs. ~45 min for regular insulin in the same study ([Ter Braak et al., 1996](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8941480/)). The flatter absorption curves of analogs partly reflect their faster monomerization, which makes the rate of absorption less dependent on local blood flow. Long-acting analogs (glargine, detemir, degludec) show minimal regional dependence in registration studies, consistent with their depot stability being driven by formulation-specific microprecipitation or albumin binding rather than vascular kinetics. *Caveat:* a substantial fraction of widely-cited site-comparison data was generated with regular human insulin, which is now rarely used for prandial dosing; readers should not extrapolate the magnitude of "abdomen vs. thigh" effects from 1980s literature directly to modern analog therapy. The clinical implication remains, however: for prandial boluses, the abdomen offers the most rapid and reproducible onset; for basal injections where slow, even absorption is desired, thigh or buttock are reasonable.

### Inadvertent intramuscular injection

Because subcutaneous fat thickness is highly variable but skin thickness is not, needles longer than the local subcutis depth deliver insulin into skeletal muscle. Intramuscular insulin is absorbed substantially faster than subcutaneous insulin (NPH T_50% 5.3 h IM vs. 10.3 h SC, with day-to-day coefficient of variation rising from 18% to 30%) and is associated with hypoglycemia, particularly when followed by exercise of the injected limb ([Vora et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2404719/); [Frid et al., 1990](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2190773/)). Ultrasound studies confirm that with an 8 mm needle inserted at 90° without a skin fold, IM injection rates are ~15--20% in lean adult men and rise to ~30--40% in lean children, especially in the thigh and male deltoid; with a 12.7 mm needle the IM rate in lean children reaches 86% ([Tubiana-Rufi et al., 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10526725/)). The 4--5 mm pen needle, inserted at 90° without a skin fold in adults, reduces IM injection rates to ≤2% across BMI strata while maintaining backflow rates equivalent to longer needles ([Hofman et al., 2010](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/33/9/1940/27008/); [Birkebaek et al., 2008](https://diabetesjournals.org/care/article/31/9/e65/28003/)). Current Forum for Injection Technique and Therapy Expert Recommendations (FITTER) consequently endorse 4 mm pen needles as first-line for nearly all patients regardless of age or BMI ([Frid et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27594187/)). *Caveat:* in children with very thin thighs (subcutis <4 mm by ultrasound) even a 4 mm needle inserted at 90° can reach muscle, and an angled (45°) approach with a pinched skin fold remains preferred for the pediatric thigh.

### Lipohypertrophy: biology

Repeated injection of insulin into the same focal area drives both adipocyte hypertrophy and adipocyte hyperplasia mediated by the lipogenic, anti-lipolytic, and insulin-like growth factor 1 receptor cross-reactivity of insulin signalling, compounded by a low-grade fibrotic response to mechanical needle trauma ([Barola et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6218430/)). The resulting lipohypertrophic (LH) lesion is clinically detectable as a thickened, rubbery, often hypoesthetic subcutaneous nodule -- frequently more conspicuous on palpation than on inspection, and consistently underdiagnosed when palpation is omitted from routine review. Ultrasound visualizes LH as hyperechoic dermal-subcutaneous thickening with disorganized fat-lobule architecture and reduced microvascular density. Because LH tissue is hypoesthetic and absorbs unpredictably (i.e., often less painfully but less effectively), patients preferentially re-inject into established lesions, perpetuating a self-reinforcing cycle.

### Lipohypertrophy and absorption variability

A pooled prevalence of LH in insulin-treated diabetes is approximately 38% (95% CI 29--46%), with type 1 diabetes estimates clustering around 30--65% in long-duration cohorts ([Deng et al., 2018](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5934253/); [Blanco et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23886784/)). Mechanistic clamp studies demonstrate that lispro injected into LH tissue produces a ~25% lower AUC, ~25% lower C_max, and -- most importantly -- a ~5-fold increase in the within-subject coefficient of variation of insulin AUC (52% vs. 11% for normal subcutaneous tissue), with corresponding deterioration of postprandial glucose control (5-h glucose AUC ≥26% higher) ([Famulla et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27411698/)). The clinical signature is unexplained hypoglycemia (39% of patients with LH vs. 6% without), unexplained hyperglycemia, glycemic variability on CGM, and elevated total daily insulin requirements (~37% higher in the Blanco cohort: 56 vs. 41 IU/day) that often regress when patients shift to lesion-free skin. *Caveat:* the prevalence range across studies (1.9--73%) reflects substantial heterogeneity in detection method (palpation alone vs. ultrasound vs. self-report); ultrasound consistently detects more lesions than palpation, suggesting that clinic-based prevalence estimates underestimate true burden.

### Rotation strategy

The injection-technique evidence base supports a hierarchical rotation strategy: keep the same anatomic region for the same time-of-day injection (e.g., abdomen for breakfast, thigh for bedtime basal) to minimize within-day pharmacokinetic variability that would otherwise be introduced by switching between fast-absorbing and slow-absorbing sites; within each region, rotate systematically across a grid of injection points spaced ≥1 cm apart to prevent local overuse. Blanco and colleagues found that only 5% of patients who rotated correctly developed LH, vs. ~98% of those with LH having rotated incorrectly or not at all ([Blanco et al., 2013](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23886784/)). FITTER recommends a "clean-zone-of-the-week" approach in which the patient mentally divides each region into quadrants and rotates through them weekly, with a ≥1 cm minimum spacing between consecutive injection points ([Frid et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27594187/)).

### Pump cannula sites

Continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion (CSII) shares the same regional absorption hierarchy as multiple-daily injection but adds the complication of an indwelling Teflon or steel cannula resident for 48--72 h. Cannula sites develop inflammation, low-grade fibrosis, and -- with extended dwell or mechanical irritation -- silent occlusion or "set failures" producing unexpected hyperglycemia and ketosis. Absorption from cannula sites declines progressively over the dwell period (typically 10--30% lower AUC by day 3 vs. day 1), and the same focal accumulation mechanism that drives MDI (multiple daily injection) lipohypertrophy can also produce CSII-site lipohypertrophy in patients who fail to rotate cannula locations.

### Other modulators

Massage of the injection site, exercise of the underlying muscle group (e.g., running after a thigh injection), and elevated ambient or local skin temperature each accelerate absorption via increased local blood flow (cross-link [§21](#temperature) for thermal effects); cold skin and dehydration slow it. Insulin formulations vary in injection-site pain, which patients may misattribute to "bad sites" -- low-pH formulations (historically glargine U100, pH 4) cause more transient stinging than neutral-pH analogs.

### Modern guidance and controversies

The two pillars of modern injection-technique guidance are the FIT (Forum for Injection Technique) recommendations of [Frid et al., 2010](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20933208/) and the FITTER consensus of [Frid et al., 2016](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27594187/), both endorsed by 100+ national diabetes associations. Open controversies include: (i) the optimal rotation pattern (clock-face vs. grid vs. randomized), where evidence is sparse; (ii) the timeline and degree of LH reversibility once a lesion is rested, with case series suggesting partial regression over 6--12 months but no controlled trials; (iii) whether routine ultrasound screening is cost-effective in long-duration T1D; and (iv) the optimal CSII set-change interval, where 48 h reduces variability but increases cost and infusion-set burden.

# Part 7: Complications and Warning Signs

<a name="ketones-dka"></a>
## 23. Ketone bodies and diabetic ketoacidosis

Ketogenesis (mitochondrial production of ketone bodies from acetyl-CoA when beta-oxidation flux exceeds the capacity of the tricarboxylic acid cycle to oxidize acetyl-CoA) is a conserved mammalian survival mechanism that provides an oxidizable fuel for the brain and periphery during carbohydrate scarcity. Under physiological conditions, circulating beta-hydroxybutyrate (beta-OHB; the predominant blood ketone body) rarely exceeds 0.3 mmol/L in the fed state and rises to 1--3 mmol/L during prolonged fasting. In type 1 diabetes (T1D), absolute or near-absolute insulin deficiency abolishes the principal brake on lipolysis and hepatic ketogenesis, enabling an unregulated cascade that can raise beta-OHB to 3--25 mmol/L, depress blood pH below 7.10, and produce a life-threatening syndrome -- diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) -- that remains the leading cause of diabetes-related death in children and young adults ([Maahs et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26180108/); [Dhatariya, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32546826/)). Approximately 20--30% of new-onset T1D in children presents as DKA at diagnosis, and recurrent DKA affects roughly 10--15% of those with established disease ([Wolfsdorf et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29956180/)).

### Ketogenesis biochemistry

Three chemically distinct ketone bodies are produced in hepatic mitochondria. Acetoacetate (AcAc; the primary synthetic product) is reduced to beta-OHB by beta-hydroxybutyrate dehydrogenase in a reaction coupled to the NADH/NAD+ ratio; at the high NADH:NAD+ ratio prevailing during heavy fatty acid oxidation this equilibrium strongly favors beta-OHB, explaining why beta-OHB constitutes approximately 75--80% of total circulating ketones in early DKA and the plasma beta-OHB:AcAc ratio rises from the physiological ~1:1 to approximately 3:1 (and up to 7:1 in severe DKA) ([Laffel, 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10634967/)). AcAc spontaneously decarboxylates to acetone -- the volatile ketone responsible for the characteristic fruity breath odour of DKA -- but acetone is metabolically inert and contributes negligibly to acid load.

The rate-limiting step in hepatic ketogenesis is carnitine palmitoyltransferase-1 (CPT-1; the mitochondrial outer-membrane enzyme that transfers long-chain acyl groups onto carnitine for import into the mitochondrial matrix). CPT-1 is allosterically inhibited by malonyl-CoA, the first committed intermediate of de novo fatty acid synthesis. In the fed, insulin-replete state, high malonyl-CoA maintains CPT-1 in a largely inhibited state, limiting beta-oxidation and confining most incoming acetyl-CoA to the TCA cycle. During insulin deficiency, AMPK-mediated phosphoinositide signalling suppresses acetyl-CoA carboxylase activity, malonyl-CoA falls, and CPT-1 is disinhibited -- a permissive gate through which the massively elevated free fatty acid (FFA) flux generated by adipose lipolysis can flood the mitochondrial matrix ([McGarry & Foster, 1980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6998753/)). Excess acetyl-CoA that cannot be oxidised via the TCA cycle condenses two molecules to form HMG-CoA (3-hydroxy-3-methylglutaryl-CoA, central intermediate in the HMG-CoA pathway) via HMG-CoA synthase; HMG-CoA lyase then cleaves HMG-CoA to yield AcAc and acetyl-CoA, completing the ketogenic cycle.

Glucogenic amino acid catabolism during insulin deficiency simultaneously consumes hepatic oxaloacetate (OAA) via gluconeogenesis, depleting the TCA-cycle intermediate required to condense with acetyl-CoA to form citrate. This OAA deficiency further diverts acetyl-CoA toward HMG-CoA and AcAc, making gluconeogenesis and ketogenesis mutually reinforcing parallel processes -- a biochemical feature that accelerates the DKA cascade once it is established.

### Hepatic fatty acid sources for ketogenesis: lipolysis, chylomicron remnants, de novo lipogenesis, and medium-chain triglycerides

The CPT-1 / malonyl-CoA gate controls the entry of all activated long-chain acyl-CoA species into the mitochondrial matrix, irrespective of the upstream source of the underlying long-chain fatty acid. Once a long-chain FFA reaches the cytosolic acyl-CoA pool of the hepatocyte it is metabolically indistinguishable from any other LCFA of the same chain length, and its disposition between re-esterification to triglyceride, secretion as very-low-density lipoprotein triglyceride (VLDL-TG), and beta-oxidation to acetyl-CoA is determined by the prevailing malonyl-CoA, glucagon, and insulin signal -- not by whether the FFA originated in adipose-tissue lipolysis, intravascular lipoprotein lipase (LPL) action on circulating triglyceride-rich particles, hepatic uptake of intact chylomicron remnants, or hepatic de novo lipogenesis from carbohydrate carbon ([McGarry & Foster, 1980](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6998753/); [Cooper, 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9392416/) — full text on paywalled wishlist). Modelling the ketogenic pathway as fed solely by adipose lipolysis-derived FFA therefore omits two pathways that can contribute substantially in the carbohydrate-restricted or high-fat postprandial state.

**Adipose-lipolysis-derived FFA.** In the postabsorptive and fasting state, adipose-tissue lipolysis is the dominant source of hepatic LCFA, supplying approximately 60% of liver triglyceride fatty acids in cohorts with non-alcoholic fatty liver disease and a larger fraction in healthy fasted subjects ([Donnelly et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15864352/)). Plasma FFA in the post-absorptive state is largely (>70-80%) of adipose-lipolytic origin, with the residual contributed by spillover from intravascular LPL action on the residual triglyceride-rich lipoprotein pool. Insulin suppresses adipose lipolysis with an EC50 of ~44-68 pmol/L, making this source the most insulin-sensitive of the three pathways (§25, Rizza dose-response hierarchy).

**Chylomicron-derived dietary fatty acids: LPL spillover and remnant uptake.** Long-chain dietary triglycerides are absorbed from the small intestine, re-esterified in the enterocyte, packaged into chylomicrons, and secreted via the thoracic duct into the systemic circulation. Stable-isotope studies in healthy adults consistently identify two routes by which dietary fatty acids reach the hepatocyte:

- *Intravascular LPL hydrolysis and spillover.* LPL anchored at endothelial surfaces hydrolyzes chylomicron-TG to monoacylglycerol and free fatty acids; a fraction is taken up locally (predominantly adipose tissue, with smaller contributions from skeletal muscle and heart), and the remainder "spills over" into the systemic plasma FFA pool, where it becomes operationally indistinguishable from adipose-derived FFA. Whole-body chylomicron-FFA spillover, quantified by deuterated triolein meal labelling, averages ~15-25% in lean healthy adults with regional values reaching ~30% in subcutaneous abdominal adipose tissue in women ([Pich&eacute; et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29099975/)). At higher meal fat loads or with reduced adipose LPL capacity, the spillover fraction rises further, in some studies approaching 50% of systemic plasma FFA flux during the 2-4 h postprandial window. Spillover-derived FFA reaching the liver via hepatic artery and portal vein is subject to the same CPT-1 / malonyl-CoA gating as adipose-derived FFA.

- *Hepatic remnant uptake.* After LPL has hydrolyzed approximately 70-90% of the chylomicron-TG core, the residual cholesteryl-ester-enriched chylomicron remnant is cleared from plasma predominantly by the liver via apoE-mediated binding to the LDL receptor (LDLR) and the LDL-receptor-related protein (LRP). Remnant plasma residence time is short (apparent half-life ~15-30 min in healthy adults; clearance largely complete by 4-6 h post-meal) ([Cooper, 1997](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9392416/) — based on abstract; full text on paywalled wishlist). Quantitatively, ~8-12% of meal TG is taken up by the liver in lean healthy subjects, rising to 10-16% in subjects with type 2 diabetes or NAFLD ([Lambert & Parks, 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22281699/)). Tracer studies in NAFLD demonstrate that "dietary" fatty acids account for ~15% of hepatic TG in habitual mixed-diet conditions and that the dietary contribution in VLDL-TG exceeds what spillover alone would predict, implying a quantitatively meaningful contribution from direct remnant uptake ([Donnelly et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15864352/)). On a high-fat low-carbohydrate diet -- where adipose lipolysis remains active *and* chylomicron-TG flux is large -- the dietary contribution to hepatic acyl-CoA flux scales approximately with absolute fat intake, although no isotope-tracer studies have directly quantified this in the keto-diet setting.

**De novo lipogenesis (DNL).** Hepatic synthesis of palmitate from carbohydrate-derived acetyl-CoA contributes ~5% of liver TG in healthy lean subjects and ~25% in NAFLD ([Donnelly et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15864352/)). DNL is insulin- and ChREBP-stimulated and is therefore suppressed in carbohydrate-restricted and insulin-deficient states. In the keto-diet / T1D ketogenic context, DNL contributes negligibly to the hepatic acyl-CoA pool.

**Medium-chain triglycerides (MCT): the CPT-1-independent shortcut.** Medium-chain fatty acids (C6-C12, principally octanoate C8 and decanoate C10) are absorbed directly into the portal vein bound to albumin rather than packaged into chylomicrons, deliver intact to the hepatocyte, and -- critically -- enter the hepatic mitochondrial matrix independently of the carnitine acyltransferase system. MCT thus bypass the CPT-1 / malonyl-CoA gate entirely and are obligately oxidized via the medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase pathway, generating acetyl-CoA at a rate that is essentially substrate-limited. The resulting acetyl-CoA flux exceeds the local TCA capacity (which is reduced by gluconeogenic OAA consumption in the carbohydrate-restricted state) and is condensed to ketone bodies. Acute MCT-feeding studies in healthy adults provide some of the most direct evidence that dietary fat can rapidly raise plasma ketones in the absence of fasting or insulin deficiency:

- 20 mL tricaprylin (C8, ~17 g) raises plasma beta-OHB to peak ~0.6-0.8 mmol/L within 1-1.5 h, with peak plasma octanoate ~280 micromol/L at 2 h ([St-Pierre et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31058159/)).
- 30 g MCT in mixed adult cohorts produces plasma beta-OHB peaks of ~0.6-1.0 mmol/L at 1.5-3 h.
- C8 is approximately 3-fold more ketogenic than C10 and 6-fold more than C12 on a gram-for-gram basis, reflecting both chain-length dependence of MCT-CoA carnitine-independence and post-absorptive partitioning to the systemic pool for the longer medium-chain species.
- Co-ingested glucose attenuates but does not abolish the MCT-induced BHB rise; the area-under-the-curve falls approximately twofold when MCT is consumed with a mixed meal versus alone ([St-Pierre et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31058159/)).

The acute MCT response demonstrates that dietary fat -- when it bypasses the CPT-1 gate -- can drive transient ketogenesis even in metabolically healthy, insulin-replete adults, and provides a useful upper bound on the magnitude of dietary-fat-driven hepatic ketogenic flux. Long-chain dietary triglycerides do not bypass CPT-1, but they do reach the hepatic acyl-CoA pool via remnant uptake and spillover and therefore contribute to ketogenesis in proportion to the prevailing CPT-1 open fraction.

**Quantitative summary: hepatic fatty acid sources by metabolic state.**

| Metabolic state | Adipose lipolysis (% of hepatic acyl-CoA flux) | Chylomicron-spillover + remnant uptake (%) | DNL (%) | Plasma BHB (mmol/L) | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Overnight-fasted lean healthy | ~70-80 | ~5-10 (residual TRL pool) | ~5 | 0.1-0.3 | [Lambert & Parks, 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22281699/); [Donnelly et al., 2005](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/15864352/) |
| Postprandial mixed meal, lean | ~40-60 | ~30-40 (peak 3-4 h) | ~5-10 | 0.05-0.15 (suppressed by insulin) | [Pich&eacute; et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29099975/); [Lambert & Parks, 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22281699/) |
| 72-h fast, healthy | ~85-95 | minimal (no dietary input) | minimal (substrate-limited) | 2-5 | [Cahill, 2006](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16848698/) |
| Keto-diet steady state, T1D w. adequate basal insulin | ~50-70 | ~25-40 (sustained dietary fat input) | minimal | 0.3-1.2 (LCD), 1.0-1.5 (VLCD) | [Ozoran et al., 2023](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37454371/) |
| Acute MCT load (30 g) in fed lean healthy | n/a (FFA flux normal) | MCT bypasses CPT-1 | minimal | peak 0.6-1.0 at 1.5-3 h | [St-Pierre et al., 2019](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31058159/) |
| DKA (insulin-deficient, mixed substrate) | ~70-85 (maximal flux) | ~10-20 if recent fat meal | minimal | 5-25 | [Laffel, 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10634967/) |

**Modeling implications and unresolved questions.** Two conclusions follow for quantitative models of ketogenesis. First, the rate of hepatic beta-oxidation -- and therefore the rate of ketone-body production for any given CPT-1 open fraction -- is proportional to the *total* hepatic acyl-CoA pool, not to the lipolytic component alone. In a high-fat postprandial state, dietary-fat-derived FA (via spillover + remnant uptake) can contribute approximately 30-40% of hepatic acyl-CoA input in healthy subjects (calculated from the spillover and remnant-uptake fractions above), rising further in keto-adapted T1D where adipose lipolysis is partially suppressed by basal insulin while dietary fat input is sustained. Second, MCT acts as a near-pure CPT-1-bypass tracer of ketogenic capacity: the magnitude of the BHB rise after a graded MCT dose can be used to bound the maximal hepatic ketogenic flux for a given hormonal milieu.

Several quantitative gaps remain. The fraction of hepatic acyl-CoA derived from dietary fat on a sustained ketogenic diet has not been directly measured by stable-isotope tracer in T1D; existing Donnelly-style data are confined to habitual mixed-diet and NAFLD cohorts. The dose-response of plasma BHB to long-chain dietary fat in carbohydrate-restricted T1D with adequate basal insulin -- the operating condition for keto-diet T1D adults -- has been characterised observationally (BHB 0.3-1.5 mmol/L cross-sectionally) but not by controlled feeding trials with paired meal-tracer methodology. The kinetic time constant for postprandial chylomicron-TG to hepatic acyl-CoA -- relevant for modelling the meal-locked component of dietary-fat-driven ketogenesis -- is bounded by chylomicron peak time (~3-4 h) and remnant residence time (~15-30 min) but the integrated transfer-rate constant has not been formally identified in vivo.

### The hormonal trigger: insulin deficiency and glucagon excess

The primary metabolic regulator of ketogenesis is circulating free insulin, not blood glucose. Insulin suppresses adipose lipolysis with an EC50 of approximately 44--68 pmol/L (~8--11 muU/mL), far lower than the EC50 for skeletal muscle glucose uptake (~330 pmol/L) ([Rizza et al., 1981](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7014664/)). Consequently, even modest reductions in portal insulin (as during deliberate carbohydrate restriction) activate lipolysis proportionally, while the full pathway to DKA requires near-complete insulin deficiency (typically portal insulin <10--20 pmol/L). Glucagon excess is a necessary co-driver: glucagon activates hepatic cAMP signalling, suppresses fructose-2,6-bisphosphate (shifting hepatic metabolism toward gluconeogenesis), reduces malonyl-CoA via AMPK, and directly upregulates HMG-CoA synthase transcription. In the insulin-deficient state the normal glucagon-to-insulin ratio is inverted -- glucagon rises two- to threefold above basal while insulin is nearly absent -- producing a "double-hit" on hepatic ketogenesis ([Foster, 1984](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6424550/)). Counter-regulatory hormones (epinephrine, cortisol, growth hormone) amplify lipolysis further and contribute to the hepatic metabolic shift, but glucagon excess is considered the dominant intrahepatic driver. *Caveat:* individuals with long-standing T1D and concurrent alpha-cell failure (rare) may have blunted glucagon responses that attenuate ketogenesis -- a clinically relevant exception in older cohorts with autonomic neuropathy.

### Physiological ketosis versus DKA

Physiological ketosis and DKA occupy entirely different biochemical regimes. During therapeutic carbohydrate restriction or prolonged fasting in a person with functional insulin secretion (or insulin-treated T1D with adequate exogenous basal), controlled lipolysis releases FFA at a rate that is matched by hepatic uptake and oxidative capacity, yielding steady-state beta-OHB of 0.5--3 mmol/L; blood pH is maintained by normal renal bicarbonate reabsorption and respiratory compensation. Because insulin -- even at low physiological concentrations -- maintains partial CPT-1 inhibition and limits FFA flux, the system is self-regulating.

In T1D DKA, the constraint is lost: adipose lipolysis is unrestrained, FFA delivery to the liver is maximal, CPT-1 is fully disinhibited, and ketone production rates (estimated at 100--200 mumol/kg/h in severe DKA vs. 10--30 mumol/kg/h in prolonged fasting) overwhelm the combined clearance capacity of peripheral tissues, renal excretion, and respiratory elimination of acetone ([Laffel, 1999](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/10634967/)). Simultaneously, the osmotic diuresis driven by hyperglycaemia (glucose >10--14 mmol/L filtered load exceeding renal reabsorptive capacity) produces obligate water and electrolyte losses that impair renal acid excretion and augment acidosis.

| Parameter | Physiological ketosis | DKA (moderate--severe) |
|---|---|---|
| Insulin | Present (reduced but functional) | Near-absent |
| beta-OHB (mmol/L) | 0.5--3 (up to 5--7 in 72-h fast) | 3--25+ |
| Blood pH | >7.35 | <7.30 (<7.10 severe) |
| Bicarbonate (mmol/L) | >22 | <18 (<10 severe) |
| Anion gap | Normal (8--12) | Elevated (>16, often >20) |
| Life-threatening | No | Yes |

### Anion gap acidosis and electrolyte derangements

The metabolic acidosis of DKA is a high anion gap (AG) acidosis (anion gap = Na+ - [Cl- + HCO3-], elevated when unmeasured anions such as beta-OHB and AcAc accumulate in plasma). AcAc (pKa 3.58) and beta-OHB (pKa 4.70) are almost completely dissociated at physiological pH; their accumulation consumes bicarbonate and raises the AG proportionally. In moderate DKA the AG characteristically exceeds 16 mmol/L (normal 8--12), and values of 25--40 are common in severe cases. A "delta-delta" analysis (comparing the rise in AG to the fall in HCO3-) approximates the pure ketoacid contribution vs. any co-existing hyperchloraemic component that may emerge during treatment with chloride-rich fluids.

Dehydration is typically 5--10% of body weight at presentation (3--8 litres in adults), driven by osmotic diuresis from glucosuria and ketonuria plus insensible losses from the compensatory hyperventilation (Kussmaul breathing: deep, rapid respirations that provide respiratory compensation by blowing off CO2, partially raising pH but increasing water loss). Total body potassium (K+) depletion is invariably present (200--500 mmol deficit in adults) even when serum K+ is normal or elevated at presentation; the apparent normokalemia reflects extracellular K+ shift from transcellular exchange with H+ (acidosis drives K+ out of cells). Insulin therapy and pH correction rapidly return K+ to cells, precipitating potentially fatal hypokalaemia if replacement is not initiated promptly -- the most common cause of iatrogenic mortality in DKA management. Phosphate, magnesium, and sodium deficits also occur but their clinical significance is less acutely critical; phosphate replacement is not routinely recommended in most guidelines due to lack of outcome benefit ([Wolfsdorf et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29956180/)).

### Diagnostic criteria

**ISPAD 2018 criteria** (paediatric; [Wolfsdorf et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29956180/)):
- Blood glucose >11 mmol/L (200 mg/dL) or known T1D
- Blood beta-OHB ≥3.0 mmol/L (or urinary ketones ≥2+)
- Venous pH <7.30 or bicarbonate <15 mmol/L

Severity stratification (paediatric ISPAD):
- **Mild:** pH 7.20--7.29, HCO3- 10--14.9 mmol/L
- **Moderate:** pH 7.10--7.19, HCO3- 5--9.9 mmol/L
- **Severe:** pH <7.10, HCO3- <5 mmol/L

**ADA 2009 criteria** (adults; [Kitabchi et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19564476/)):
- Blood glucose >13.9 mmol/L (250 mg/dL); or euglycaemic DKA in specific contexts
- Arterial pH <7.30; venous pH <7.35
- Bicarbonate <18 mmol/L
- Anion gap >10 mmol/L

*Caveat:* Point-of-care blood beta-OHB measurement (electrochemical strip; measuring beta-OHB directly) is now strongly preferred over urine ketone measurement (urinary AcAc detected by nitroprusside reaction, which does not detect beta-OHB and systematically underestimates ketone burden, particularly early in DKA before ketonuria is established and paradoxically during early treatment when AcAc is regenerated from beta-OHB as NADH:NAD+ falls).

### Paediatric-specific considerations

Children with T1D are at substantially higher risk of DKA at onset and recurrence than adults. Global registry data from the T1D Exchange indicate a DKA-at-onset rate of 30--40% in children under 5 years, declining to 20--25% in adolescents ([Maahs et al., 2015](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26180108/)). Recurrent DKA (≥2 episodes/year) affects approximately 1--10% of paediatric T1D, concentrated in adolescent females with socioeconomic disadvantage or psychological comorbidity (including insulin omission for weight management -- "diabulimia").

The most feared complication of paediatric DKA is cerebral edema (CE): symptomatic CE occurs in approximately 0.5--1.0% of paediatric DKA episodes (essentially absent in adults), with mortality 20--25% and permanent neurological deficit in a further 15--35% of survivors ([Glaser et al., 2001](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11419433/)). The pathophysiology remains debated: the "vasogenic" hypothesis (failure of cerebrovascular autoregulation with osmotic fluid shifts during rapid rehydration) and the "cytotoxic" hypothesis (ischemia-reperfusion injury during cerebral hypoperfusion in DKA) both have supporting data. A landmark NEJM case-control study by Glaser and colleagues (2001) identified three independent risk factors for CE: attenuated rise in serum sodium during treatment (i.e., falling corrected Na+, suggesting excessive hypotonic fluid movement into brain), bicarbonate therapy (OR 4.2, 95% CI 1.5--12.1), and high initial BUN (marker of severe dehydration and renal hypoperfusion). *Caveat:* retrospective design and case-control methodology limit causal inference; prospective trials of fluid replacement strategies have not conclusively identified an optimal rate or tonicity. The PECARN-funded FLUID trial (2018) found no neurological benefit of slower fluid rates or higher-tonicity fluids vs. current practice, but was powered for neurological outcomes at 24h rather than CE incidence ([Kuppermann et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30088006/)).

### Euglycaemic DKA

Euglycaemic DKA (euDKA) -- DKA with blood glucose <11 mmol/L -- is recognised in three clinical contexts in T1D: (i) SGLT2 inhibitor use (glucose filtered by SGLT2 inhibitors is excreted, blunting hyperglycaemia while ketone accumulation continues unchecked -- potentially masking DKA); (ii) partial insulin delivery (pump underdelivery, missed basal with residual effect); and (iii) prolonged starvation or low-carbohydrate dieting with reduced but not absent insulin dosing. In all three scenarios, the common pathway is reduced insulin-mediated suppression of lipolysis and ketogenesis in the setting of relatively normal glycaemic control, producing a presentation that may be delayed until ketoacidosis is advanced. SGLT2 inhibitor-associated euDKA carries estimated risk of approximately 2 per 1,000 patient-years in T1D and is now a class labelling concern, partly explaining why SGLT2 inhibitors are currently approved as adjunct therapy in T1D in only a subset of jurisdictions ([Dhatariya, 2020](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32546826/)).

### DKA mortality and epidemiology

DKA mortality in high-income countries has declined substantially over the 20th century with improved access to insulin and critical-care medicine but has plateaued since approximately 1980. Current estimates: case fatality rate approximately 0.15--0.30% in adults in high-income settings ([Kitabchi et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19564476/)), approximately 0.15--0.30% in paediatric series (excluding CE) ([Wolfsdorf et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29956180/)). Global DKA mortality remains high in low- and middle-income countries due to delayed diagnosis, insulin access barriers, and limited critical-care capacity; in sub-Saharan Africa case fatality rates of 5--30% are reported. In high-income paediatric cohorts, the most common causes of DKA death are CE, hypokalaemia-related cardiac arrhythmia, and aspiration pneumonia.

### Treatment principles

Standard DKA management follows three concurrent pillars ([Wolfsdorf et al., 2018](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29956180/); [Kitabchi et al., 2009](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19564476/)):

**1. Fluid resuscitation.** Initial bolus of 10--20 mL/kg isotonic saline (0.9% NaCl) over 1 hour for circulatory compromise; subsequent rehydration with 0.9% or 0.45% NaCl titrated to replace estimated deficit over 24--48 h. In paediatrics, total fluid volumes are generally more conservative than in adults to limit CE risk (maintenance + deficit replacement rather than aggressive bolusing). Dextrose 5--10% is added to IV fluid when blood glucose falls below approximately 14 mmol/L to allow continuation of insulin without producing hypoglycaemia.

**2. Insulin infusion.** Low-dose intravenous insulin (0.05--0.1 U/kg/h in children; 0.1 U/kg/h in adults) suppresses lipolysis and ketogenesis, enabling bicarbonate regeneration and anion gap closure. The therapeutic target is anion gap normalisation and ketone clearance -- not glucose normalisation. Importantly, insulin must not be started before adequate fluid resuscitation because insulin-driven cellular K+ uptake in a fluid-depleted, hyperkalaemic patient risks precipitous hypokalaemia. The "sugar and insulin together" treatment logic -- adding IV dextrose once glucose drops to prevent hypoglycaemia while maintaining insulin to close the anion gap -- is the central therapeutic principle that distinguishes DKA reversal from simple hyperglycaemia correction.

**3. Potassium replacement.** Commenced with insulin initiation if serum K+ <5.5 mmol/L; typically 20--40 mEq/h KCl (adult), guided by continuous ECG monitoring and 2-hourly electrolytes. If K+ <3.5 mmol/L at presentation, insulin should be withheld until K+ is ≥3.5 mmol/L. Bicarbonate is not recommended in most paediatric or adult guidelines except for pH <6.9 with haemodynamic compromise, as bicarbonate therapy may paradoxically worsen cerebral acidosis by increasing CO2 crossing the blood-brain barrier while HCO3- equilibrates slowly.

### Controversies and open questions

Several areas remain mechanistically or clinically unsettled. (i) **CE pathophysiology:** Despite Glaser's influential risk-factor analysis, the causal mechanism remains unresolved and no RCT has definitively identified a safer fluid composition or rate. (ii) **Bicarbonate in severe adult DKA:** The ADA 2009 guideline cautiously permits bicarbonate at pH <6.9, but the supporting evidence is low-quality and several observational studies show no benefit or harm; a prospective pilot trial (Chua et al., 2012) suggested possible delay in ketone clearance. (iii) **Optimal ketone monitoring modality:** Blood beta-OHB is pharmacokinetically superior to urine AcAc, but global guideline adoption remains incomplete and strip costs limit access in low-income settings. (iv) **Subcutaneous insulin protocols for mild DKA:** Meta-analyses (e.g., Andrade-Castellanos et al., 2016) suggest rapid-acting subcutaneous analogs may be non-inferior to IV insulin infusion in mild-to-moderate adult DKA (pH >7.20), potentially enabling non-ICU management, but this approach is not yet endorsed in paediatric guidelines. (v) **SGLT2 inhibitors in T1D:** The risk-benefit balance of adjunct SGLT2 inhibitor therapy in T1D, specifically the euDKA risk, continues to be evaluated in post-marketing registries.

### Ketone body kinetics and clearance

Beta-OHB plasma half-life under normal conditions is approximately 0.8--3.1 hours, but clearance is saturable: at DKA-range concentrations (>5 mmol/L), Michaelis-Menten kinetics apply and apparent clearance declines. Clarke and colleagues (2012) characterised a two-compartment model of exogenous ketone pharmacokinetics with a total clearance of approximately 10.9 L/h plus a saturable Vmax component ([Clarke et al., 2012](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22561291/)). During clinical DKA resolution with insulin infusion, the anion gap typically closes within 8--16 hours (reflecting ketone clearance and bicarbonate regeneration) while pH lags by 2--4 hours. The beta-OHB:AcAc ratio normalises as NADH:NAD+ falls with improved mitochondrial function, and urinary ketone loss (ketonuria) typically persists 4--8 hours after blood ketones have normalised, reflecting the time constant of renal tubular clearance from interstitial space.

The chain-length-specific medium-chain triglyceride (MCT) pathway, which bypasses CPT-1 entirely, remains unmodeled and is the residual documented gap relative to the reference physiology.

<a name="insulin-overdose"></a>
## 24. Insulin overdose and the limitations of counterregulation

Exogenous insulin overdose encompasses a clinical spectrum from accidental dosing errors — miscalculated correction boluses, decimal-place mistakes with concentrated insulin (U-500), double-dosing, or pump programming errors — to factitious hypoglycemia and intentional self-poisoning. Epidemiological data from poison control registries and emergency departments consistently identify insulin as one of the most common causes of medication-induced death; a 10-year retrospective analysis of intentional overdoses (Mégarbane et al. 2007, *Critical Care*) reported a 16% in-hospital mortality despite intensive care management, substantially higher than most oral overdoses. Severity scales from mild (BG 2.5–3.9 mmol/L, self-treatable) to catastrophic (BG < 1 mmol/L, coma, seizures, massive glucose infusion requirements) depend on: (1) total insulin dose relative to body mass, (2) formulation (soluble regular vs. long-acting analogue), (3) injection depot characteristics, (4) pre-overdose BG and hepatic glycogen stores, (5) the integrity of the counterregulatory hierarchy, and (6) speed of clinical intervention. In T1D specifically, the glucagon limb of counterregulation is absent after a median of 2–5 years of disease duration (Bolli et al. 1983, *N Engl J Med*), making even moderate overdoses potentially life-threatening.

### Pharmacokinetics of overdose: saturable absorption and prolonged tails

Under normal therapeutic dosing, subcutaneous insulin absorption follows first-order kinetics with half-lives of 1–2 h (rapid analogues) to 12–24 h (glargine, degludec). During massive overdose, this kinetics changes qualitatively. Large subcutaneous depots (> 100 IU in a single site) create a semi-solid precipitate that dissolves slowly; absorption becomes zero-order (rate-limited by depot surface area) rather than first-order, and the effective duration extends dramatically. Case series document insulin-induced hypoglycemia persisting for 24–120 h after a single large injection: von Mach et al. (2004, *J Clin Pharmacol*) described survivors of insulin overdoses of 300–4,500 IU (the latter in a 63 kg patient with type 2 diabetes) where continuous intravenous glucose infusion was required for 3–5 days; serum insulin concentrations in the thousands of μU/mL were documented at 24 h, confirming ongoing depot absorption. Brvar et al. (2007, *Eur J Clin Pharmacol*) measured insulin pharmacokinetics in 14 overdose patients (median dose 400 IU lispro) and found terminal half-lives of 8–19 h — 4–10 times longer than the therapeutic value — consistent with depot-limited, saturable absorption kinetics. Importantly, attempted aspiration or lavage of subcutaneous depots is generally unsuccessful; direct excision of the injection depot has been employed as a rescue measure in life-threatening cases refractory to glucose infusion (Fasano et al. 2008, *Endocr Pract*; Levine et al. 2007, *J Med Toxicol*), though data supporting efficacy are limited to case reports.

### Physiologic counterregulation: the Cryer hierarchy and its ceiling

Normal counterregulation to hypoglycemia operates through a hierarchical sequence (Cryer 2013, *N Engl J Med*): (1) suppression of endogenous insulin secretion (plasma insulin falls below ~42 pmol/L), (2) glucagon release from alpha cells (BG threshold ~3.8 mmol/L), (3) sympathoadrenal activation including adrenaline secretion (threshold ~3.7 mmol/L), and (4) cortisol and GH release (threshold ~3.6–3.7 mmol/L). The key effector of acute counterregulation is EGP (endogenous glucose production), which at baseline approximates 2 mg/kg/min (roughly 10–12 g/h in a 70 kg adult). Glucagon is the primary driver of acute EGP stimulation via glycogenolysis (breakdown of stored hepatic glycogen to glucose) and, secondarily, gluconeogenesis. Under maximal glucagon stimulation, EGP can increase approximately 3–5-fold above basal (Rizza et al. 1979, *Am J Physiol*; Taborsky et al. 1998, *Diabetologia*), equating to a maximum EGP increment of ~20–40 mg/min in a 70 kg individual — approximately 1.2–2.4 g glucose/h above baseline. At supraphysiologic insulin concentrations (> 1,000 μU/mL), portal insulin concentrations suppress hepatic gluconeogenic enzymes (phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase, glucose-6-phosphatase) within minutes to hours (Pilkis & Granner 1992, *Annu Rev Physiol*), so that gluconeogenesis is essentially abolished even if glucagon is present. EGP is therefore constrained to the glycogenolytic rate, which itself is limited by hepatic glycogen content.

### Hepatic glycogen: the hard substrate limit

The adult liver stores approximately 75–100 g of glycogen under fed conditions (Nilsson & Hultman 1973, *Scand J Clin Lab Invest*; Taylor et al. 1996, *Proc Natl Acad Sci* — MRS-derived estimates in vivo). At a maximal glycogenolytic EGP increment of 2 mg/kg/min (= ~2 g glucose/min in a 70 kg person above basal), the hepatic glycogen store is fully depleted in roughly 50–100 minutes if EGP were to operate at its ceiling with no concurrent gluconeogenesis. Under realistic overdose conditions — where insulin suppresses gluconeogenesis but glycogenolysis proceeds against declining substrate — stores are functionally depleted in 4–8 h of sustained hypoglycaemic stress (Petersen et al. 2001, *J Clin Invest* — hepatic glycogen dynamics quantified by 13C MRS). Beyond this window, EGP cannot be meaningfully sustained from the liver regardless of glucagon dose; muscle glycogenolysis, though substantial (~400 g total glycogen in a 70 kg adult), does not contribute directly to blood glucose because muscle lacks glucose-6-phosphatase. The practical consequence is that in a massive insulin overdose with a depot still absorbing at t = 6–12 h, the liver has exhausted its primary rescue capacity, and even high-dose glucagon administration produces negligible BG elevation. This has been confirmed in clinical case series: glucagon (1 mg IM) restores BG transiently in mild overdose but provides minimal and short-lived benefit in severe or prolonged cases (Marks & Teale 1999, *Endocrinol Metab Clin North Am*).

### HAAF in the overdose context

HAAF (hypoglycemia-associated autonomic failure) — the progressive attenuation of sympathoadrenal counterregulatory responses consequent to recurrent antecedent hypoglycemia — amplifies overdose risk by two mechanisms. First, it shifts the glycemic threshold for adrenaline secretion and symptom onset downward from ~3.6–3.8 mmol/L toward 2.5–2.8 mmol/L or lower, delaying recognition of overdose-related hypoglycemia. Second, it reduces the absolute amplitude of adrenaline secretion even after the threshold is crossed, diminishing the sympathoadrenal contribution to EGP (which accounts for ~40–60% of the counterregulatory EGP response when glucagon is absent; Cryer 2013). Dagogo-Jack et al. (1993, *J Clin Invest*) demonstrated that even a single episode of hypoglycemia the preceding day blunted adrenaline responses by ~50% during a subsequent hyperinsulinemic clamp; patients with established HAAF had ~25-fold greater risk of severe hypoglycemia episodes. In overdose, this means a patient with HAAF may not become symptomatic until BG < 2 mmol/L, by which time hepatic glycogen may already be depleted. HAAF is partially reversible with 2–3 weeks of strict hypoglycemia avoidance (Cranston et al. 1994, *Lancet*; Dagogo-Jack et al. 1994), but the alpha-cell glucagon deficit in established T1D is permanent and is not restored by avoidance protocols (Bolli et al. 1983).

### Case series and recovery dynamics

Documented survivors of extreme insulin overdose challenge the intuitive expectation that large doses are uniformly fatal. Roberge et al. (1993, *Crit Care Med*) reported survival after 4,500 IU human regular insulin in a 63 kg adult, requiring continuous 50% dextrose infusion (approximately 25 g glucose/h) for 72 h — total glucose administered exceeded 1,800 g. Mégarbane et al. (2007) analysed 30 consecutive intentional overdose admissions; serum insulin peaked at 3,800–26,000 μU/mL (normal fasting ~5–15 μU/mL); mean glucose infusion requirement was 7.5 g/h with a maximum of 35 g/h; six patients required depot excision and two died. Tofade & Lesar (2004, *Endocr Pract*) reviewed 57 published overdose cases and identified that time-to-treatment initiation, rather than absolute dose, was the strongest predictor of morbidity: all deaths occurred in patients with a > 6 h delay to glucose infusion. Recovery of euglycemia typically required 12–120 h depending on formulation; long-acting analogues (glargine, NPH) were consistently associated with durations > 48 h. Fasano et al. (2008) documented a unique case where surgical excision of a glargine depot at 18 h post-injection — combined with octreotide — terminated hypoglycemia that had required > 150 g glucose/h for 12 h. Neurological sequelae (memory impairment, cortical atrophy) from prolonged severe hypoglycemia have been reported in survivors after BG < 1.5 mmol/L lasting > 2 h (Strachan et al. 2000, *Diabetes Care*).

### Treatment principles

The cornerstone of treatment is continuous intravenous glucose infusion titrated to maintain BG 5–10 mmol/L, with frequent monitoring (every 15–30 min during acute phase). Oral glucose and sucrose are appropriate for mild, conscious overdose but inadequate as sole therapy for large depots or long-acting formulations. Glucagon (1 mg IM or IV) is rational as first-aid in T1D if the patient is unconscious and IV access is unavailable, but its efficacy diminishes rapidly with hepatic glycogen depletion and is unreliable beyond 1–2 doses; it is not a substitute for IV glucose. Octreotide — a somatostatin analogue that suppresses residual endogenous insulin secretion — is specifically beneficial in sulfonylurea-augmented overdose (it has no effect on exogenous insulin kinetics), administered at 50–100 µg subcutaneously every 6–8 h (Carr 2006, *Emerg Med Australas*; Fasano et al. 2008); its use in pure exogenous insulin overdose is pharmacologically questionable but has been employed in combination regimens for very high-dose cases. Surgical excision of the subcutaneous depot should be considered when glucose requirements exceed 25–30 g/h despite maximal medical therapy and imaging (ultrasound or CT) confirms a large residual depot; the evidence base is limited to approximately 20 published cases (Levine et al. 2007; Fasano et al. 2008). Hypokalemia is a consistent complication requiring active replacement; insulin drives potassium into cells, and massive glucose infusions potentiate this. Continuous cardiac monitoring is mandatory. Controversies include the optimal glucose infusion concentration (10% vs. 50% dextrose — the latter delivers more glucose per volume but risks phlebitis and hyperosmolar complications), and the appropriate endpoint for depot excision (BG requirement threshold is not established in guidelines).

<a name="nonlinear-insulin"></a>
## 25. Non-linearity in insulin action — threshold effects and dose-response saturation

Insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios (ICR) and insulin sensitivity factors (ISF) are central dosing parameters in T1D management, yet both are treated as constants in clinical practice despite strong evidence that insulin's glucose-lowering action is fundamentally non-linear. A given unit of exogenous insulin does not produce a fixed decrement in blood glucose across the physiological dose range: the relationship between plasma insulin concentration and biological effect follows a sigmoidal, saturable curve whose slope, threshold, and ceiling differ across target tissues. The practical consequence is that small correction doses may produce disproportionately hepatic effects with little peripheral glucose disposal, while supraphysiological concentrations produce diminishing additional benefit at the cost of increased hypoglycemia risk. Understanding this non-linearity is essential for interpreting the apparently paradoxical clinical phenomena of "insulin stacking dead zones," dose-dependent changes in apparent ISF, and the differential impact of insulin resistance at liver versus muscle.

### Insulin receptor pharmacology: molecular basis for sigmoidal dose-response

Insulin binds the insulin receptor (IR), a heterotetrameric receptor tyrosine kinase (alpha-2/beta-2), at two distinct binding surfaces on the alpha subunits that together confer negative cooperativity: occupation of the first binding site reduces affinity at the second (De Meyts 2004). This negative cooperativity is the molecular origin of the sigmoidal shape observed in whole-body insulin dose-response curves, because rising concentrations progressively overcome reduced affinity at a rate that is non-linear with respect to total receptor occupancy.

Receptor binding triggers autophosphorylation of multiple tyrosine residues on the intracellular beta subunits, creating docking sites for IRS-1 (insulin receptor substrate 1, primary docking platform downstream of insulin receptor). IRS-1 recruits phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase (PI3K), generating phosphatidylinositol 3,4,5-trisphosphate (PIP3), which in turn activates 3-phosphoinositide-dependent kinase-1 (PDK1) and Akt/PKB. Akt phosphorylation then bifurcates into metabolic branches: (i) GLUT4 vesicle translocation to the plasma membrane (glucose uptake in muscle and adipose), (ii) phosphorylation and nuclear exclusion of FOXO1 (suppressing gluconeogenic gene transcription), and (iii) activation of glycogen synthase kinase-3 (GSK3) inhibition (glycogen synthesis) (Saltiel & Kahn 2001; Manning & Cantley 2007). Critically, these downstream branches have different amplification factors and activation thresholds within the signaling cascade, so identical receptor occupancy does not yield proportionally identical biological outputs across pathways.

At the whole-organ level, an additional layer of non-linearity arises from plasma-to-interstitial insulin transport. Insulin must traverse the capillary endothelium to reach receptor-bearing cells; this transport step is itself concentration-dependent and saturable, adding a further delay and non-linearity to the tissue dose-response relationship (Sjöstrand et al. 2002). Computational analyses by Bradley et al. (1993) suggest that hepatic and peripheral insulin effects, despite markedly different EC50 values at the level of glucose fluxes, may share a common rate-limiting transcapillary transport step — though the kinetic parameters remain model-dependent and subject to ongoing debate.

### Hill kinetics framework for insulin dose-response

The sigmoidal insulin dose-response relationship is canonically represented by the Hill equation (Hill kinetics — sigmoidal dose-response curve parameterized by EC50 and Hill coefficient n):

```
E(I) = E_max × I^n / (EC50^n + I^n)
```

where E is the fractional effect (0–1 relative to maximal), I is the plasma insulin concentration (typically µU/mL or pmol/L), EC50 (insulin concentration producing half-maximal effect) is the concentration at half-maximum effect, n is the Hill coefficient governing curve steepness (dimensionless), and E_max is the tissue-specific maximal biological effect. For n = 1 the equation reduces to a Michaelis-Menten hyperbola; values of n > 1 produce progressively steeper sigmoidal transitions. For whole-body insulin action on hepatic EGP (endogenous glucose production) and peripheral Rd (rate of glucose disposal), published estimates typically fall in the range n ≈ 1.5–2.5, reflecting a moderately cooperative system (Rizza et al. 1981; Natali et al. 2000).

The Hill framework captures three pharmacologically distinct dose regimes that are physiologically important:

- **Sub-threshold zone (I << EC50):** Effect increases slowly and approximately linearly with dose; tissue-specific responses have not yet reached their steeply rising region.
- **Transition zone (I ≈ EC50):** The steepest portion of the dose-response curve; small concentration changes produce large effect changes. This is the clinically relevant operating range for most T1D correction boluses.
- **Saturation zone (I >> EC50):** Effect plateaus near E_max; additional insulin provides diminishing marginal benefit. Supraphysiological doses during insulin overdose or aggressive correction dwell in this region.

Because EC50 differs across tissues, each organ enters and exits these regimes at different absolute insulin concentrations, creating the well-documented sequential hierarchy of insulin effects.

### Tissue-specific EC50 values: the Rizza 1981 clamp dataset

The most rigorously characterized tissue-specific insulin dose-response data in humans derive from a landmark study by Rizza, Mandarino, and Gerich (1981), who performed 8-hour sequential insulin infusion clamps at six steady-state plasma insulin concentrations (ranging from ~35 to ~1,400 pmol/L) in 15 healthy subjects. By measuring endogenous glucose production (via tritiated glucose isotope dilution) and whole-body glucose disposal (Rd) simultaneously at each insulin plateau, they generated complete sigmoidal dose-response curves for both hepatic and peripheral effects in the same subjects.

**Table 25-1: Tissue-specific insulin dose-response parameters (Rizza et al. 1981, healthy subjects, n = 15)**

| Target tissue / effect | EC50 (pmol/L) | EC50 (µU/mL) | Half-maximal receptor occupancy | Hill coefficient (n) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Adipose: suppression of lipolysis (FFA flux) | ~44–68 | ~8–11 | < 5% | ~1.5 |
| Liver: suppression of EGP (hepatic glucose production) | ~174 | ~29 | ~11% | ~1.7 |
| Muscle/whole-body: stimulation of Rd (glucose disposal) | ~330 | ~55 | ~49% | ~1.5 |
| Liver: full EGP suppression (≥ 90%) | ~360 | ~60 | — | — |
| Muscle: near-maximal Rd (≥ 80% E_max) | ~1,200–4,200 | ~200–700 | — | — |

*Conversion: 1 µU/mL ≈ 6.0 pmol/L (based on WHO international standard). EC50 values from Rizza et al. (1981) Table 2 and Fig. 3–4. Hill coefficients estimated from graphical dose-response slopes reported therein.*

The ~3-fold difference in EC50 between hepatic EGP suppression (~29 µU/mL) and peripheral Rd stimulation (~55 µU/mL) is the quantitative cornerstone of tissue-specific insulin pharmacology. Adipose tissue responds at even lower concentrations, with half-maximal suppression of lipolysis achieved at portal concentrations that barely begin to suppress EGP, reflecting the extreme sensitivity of hormone-sensitive lipase to insulin signaling.

An important anatomical consideration modulates these figures: insulin secreted by the pancreatic beta cells is delivered via the portal vein directly to the liver before reaching systemic circulation, creating a portal-to-peripheral plasma insulin gradient of approximately 2–3:1 under physiological conditions (Polonsky et al. 1988; Meier et al. 2005). Consequently, the liver is exposed to substantially higher insulin concentrations than peripheral tissues at any given systemic plasma insulin level. In T1D, where all insulin is administered subcutaneously and enters the systemic circulation before reaching the liver, this gradient is reversed or abolished — peripheral concentrations equal or exceed hepatic concentrations. This "hepatic insulin deficiency" relative to the physiological state may contribute to the higher total exogenous insulin requirements observed in T1D and is mechanistically relevant to the incomplete EGP suppression seen in some T1D patients even with intensive therapy (Woerle et al. 2003).

### HGP suppression versus Rd activation as separable dose-response curves

EGP suppression and Rd stimulation are not merely quantitatively different — they represent qualitatively distinct physiological responses mediated by partially distinct intracellular pathways, and their dose-response relationships are therefore separable and independently modulatable.

**Hepatic EGP suppression** is mediated principally through (i) suppression of glycogenolysis (rapid, via phosphorylase kinase inhibition) and (ii) suppression of gluconeogenesis (slower, via FOXO1 nuclear exclusion and reduced expression of PEPCK and glucose-6-phosphatase). At basal insulin concentrations (~50–100 pmol/L systemic, ~150–300 pmol/L portal), glycogenolysis is already substantially suppressed; gluconeogenesis continues and accounts for ~50% of basal EGP. Near-complete EGP suppression (~90%) requires systemic insulin of ~360 pmol/L (~60 µU/mL) (Rizza et al. 1981). Because hepatic insulin action operates near the lower end of the sigmoidal curve under physiological conditions, small corrections in portal insulin exert large proportional effects on EGP.

**Peripheral Rd stimulation** requires insulin concentrations approximately 2–10 times higher than for EGP suppression to reach equivalent fractional effects. Rd is dominated (~80%) by skeletal muscle, which expresses GLUT4 as its primary glucose transporter; GLUT4 translocation is the key rate-limiting step for insulin-stimulated muscle glucose uptake (Zierath et al. 2000). Because GLUT4 translocation requires high Akt activity and is subject to post-receptor signal attenuation, skeletal muscle operates on the steep portion of its dose-response curve at insulin concentrations corresponding to the late post-meal (or high-dose correction) range. This explains why Thiebaud et al. (1982), using graded insulin infusions in a separate stepped-clamp design, found that whole-body glucose oxidation and glucose storage increased progressively only above ~200 pmol/L (~33 µU/mL) and were near-maximal only above ~700 pmol/L (~117 µU/mL).

Adipose tissue represents a third, more sensitive effector: Stumvoll et al. (2000) demonstrated that suppression of systemic, intramuscular, and subcutaneous lipolysis reaches 80–90% at insulin concentrations of ~100–200 pmol/L, well below concentrations required for substantial Rd stimulation. This creates the physiologically logical hierarchy: at low insulin levels, energy substrate mobilization is arrested first (protecting against ketogenesis), EGP is suppressed next (limiting hepatic glucose output), and peripheral disposal is recruited last (the most energetically demanding step).

### T1D-specific implications: variable ICR, the small-dose paradox, and post-meal dynamics

These dose-response non-linearities have several underappreciated clinical consequences specific to T1D:

**Variable apparent ICR and ISF by dose.** Because the bolus dose determines which portion of the Rd dose-response curve is activated, the apparent ISF and ICR calculated from a correction or meal response are dose-dependent. A 1-unit correction from a low baseline (I ≈ 80 pmol/L systemic) operates near the inflection of the hepatic curve and primarily suppresses residual EGP — producing a glucose-lowering effect driven predominantly by hepatic action. A 4-unit correction from the same baseline pushes systemic insulin into the steep and eventually saturating portion of the muscle Rd curve, recruiting substantially more peripheral disposal per unit insulin. The apparent ISF from the small dose thus reflects mainly hepatic sensitivity; the apparent ISF from the large dose reflects the integrated hepatic + peripheral response. ICR estimated from small mixed meals may therefore systematically underestimate the insulin requirement for larger meals (where muscle recruitment is disproportionately larger), particularly in individuals with peripheral insulin resistance.

**The "small-dose paradox" and sub-threshold dosing.** In the transition zone between adipose lipolysis suppression and hepatic EGP suppression (systemic insulin ~70–174 pmol/L), small exogenous doses produce measurable anti-lipolytic effects and modestly reduced EGP but negligible peripheral Rd stimulation. A patient administering a small correction bolus who observes "no effect on blood glucose" over 2 hours may in fact have substantially suppressed lipolysis and partially reduced EGP — effects that do not manifest as a visible BG decline if residual EGP still exceeds current Rd. The clinical pitfall is administering a second correction dose that together with the first finally recruits peripheral Rd; the combined glucose-lowering effect then appears disproportionately large. This phenomenon has been described qualitatively by clinicians but lacks systematic quantitative characterization specific to T1D.

**Post-meal dual-process dynamics.** After a mixed meal, insulin rises from basal to post-meal concentrations over 60–120 minutes (depending on insulin type and absorption kinetics). The shape of the glucose response curve reflects the sequential recruitment of tissue responses: early post-meal EGP suppression dominates, followed by Rd stimulation as insulin rises further. In T1D without any residual beta-cell function, the entire post-meal insulin profile is externally determined and depends on bolus timing, formulation, and absorption rate. Errors in bolus timing that shift the plasma insulin peak into the ascending versus descending phase of the meal glucose excursion will differentially engage hepatic versus peripheral effects, altering both the peak and the late post-meal nadir.

**Portal insulin deficit in T1D.** As noted above, subcutaneous insulin delivery eliminates the physiological portal-to-peripheral gradient (~2–3:1). The liver in T1D is therefore exposed to substantially lower insulin concentrations than it would receive from equivalent endogenous secretion. Published quantitative estimates suggest that portal insulin from subcutaneous delivery is approximately 30–50% lower than what the same systemic concentration would imply under physiological delivery (Woerle et al. 2003). This contributes to the elevated basal and post-meal EGP seen in T1D relative to non-diabetic controls at comparable systemic insulin levels.

### Modulators of EC50: exercise, FFA, and glucotoxicity

The EC50 values reported by Rizza et al. (1981) are population means in healthy, euglycemic, non-exercising subjects. Several physiologically important conditions shift the dose-response curves in T1D-relevant ways:

**Exercise.** Aerobic exercise at moderate intensity (40–65% VO2max) recruits GLUT4 translocation via an AMP-kinase (AMPK) pathway that is independent of Akt, effectively adding a dose-independent component to Rd that shifts the effective Rd dose-response curve leftward — i.e., reduces the apparent EC50 for insulin-stimulated disposal (Zierath et al. 2000; Richter & Hargreaves 2013). Functionally, post-exercise peripheral insulin sensitivity is substantially elevated, meaning that the same plasma insulin concentration drives more Rd, and a lower insulin concentration can fully suppress EGP (which requires similar absolute insulin to suppress regardless of exercise state). The net effect is that post-exercise, both curves shift, but the muscle Rd curve shifts more, narrowing the gap between EC50-liver and EC50-muscle and reducing the "dead zone" described above. This underpins the clinical recommendation for reduced correction doses post-exercise.

**Free fatty acids (FFA).** Elevated circulating FFA from adipose lipolysis (either insufficient insulin suppression of lipolysis or dietary fat absorption) drives diacylglycerol (DAG) and ceramide accumulation, activating PKC-theta, which phosphorylates IRS-1 at serine residues, impairing PI3K recruitment and downstream Akt activation (Boden & Shulman 2002; Szendroedi et al. 2014). This post-receptor signaling defect predominantly affects the muscle Rd arm of the dose-response relationship, shifting the EC50-muscle rightward while leaving EC50-liver less affected (hepatic FOXO1 suppression is relatively resistant to DAG/PKC-theta because hepatocytes rely more on IRS-2 than IRS-1). The consequence is a selective widening of the hepatic-to-muscle EC50 gap — precisely the condition that amplifies the "dead zone" phenomenon described above. Quantitatively, sustained FFA elevation of ~0.8 mmol/L has been shown to reduce whole-body insulin-stimulated glucose disposal by ~25–40% in clamp conditions (Boden 1996; Roden et al. 1996), corresponding to a rightward EC50 shift of a similar magnitude.

**Glucotoxicity.** Sustained hyperglycemia independently reduces GLUT4 expression and membrane trafficking via ROS-mediated oxidative stress and hexosamine pathway activation (Brownlee 2001). As with FFA-induced resistance, this predominantly affects the peripheral Rd curve, since GLUT4 is the primary target of glucotoxic down-regulation. EC50-liver may also shift rightward via reduced hepatic FOXO1 sensitivity, but the magnitude is smaller and more variable across studies (Kraegen et al. 1991).

**Table 25-2: Approximate effects of physiological modulators on tissue-specific EC50 (relative to euglycemic resting baseline; directional estimates based on cited studies)**

| Modulator | EC50-liver (EGP) | EC50-muscle (Rd) | Net gap change |
|---|---|---|---|
| Baseline (Rizza 1981, healthy) | ~29 µU/mL | ~55 µU/mL | ~26 µU/mL |
| Aerobic exercise (moderate) | Minimal change | Leftward (↓ ~20–40%) | Gap narrows |
| FFA elevation (~0.8 mmol/L) | Mild rightward (↑ ~10–20%) | Rightward (↑ ~25–40%) | Gap widens |
| Glucotoxicity (sustained > 15 mmol/L) | Mild rightward | Rightward (↑ ~20–30%) | Gap widens |
| Insulin resistance (illness, T2D) | Rightward (↑ ~20–50%) | Rightward (↑ ~50–100%) | Gap widens markedly |

### Controversies and limitations

Several important caveats surround the quantitative use of published EC50 estimates:

**Population vs. individual EC50 variability.** Rizza et al. (1981) reported substantial inter-individual variability in EC50-muscle (coefficient of variation ~30–40% across the 15 subjects). Published values should be treated as population central estimates; individual EC50 likely varies by ~2-fold around the mean, depending on fitness, body composition, and metabolic state. No large-scale characterization of individual EC50 variability in T1D populations exists.

**Healthy-to-T1D generalizability.** The canonical Rizza dataset was obtained in metabolically healthy subjects under euglycemic clamp conditions. Generalization to T1D introduces two complications: (1) absent portal-to-peripheral gradient alters effective hepatic exposure; (2) variable insulin resistance from disease duration, exercise habits, and glycemic control shifts EC50 in unpredictable directions. Basu et al. (2004) showed that the hepatic dose-response curve is similarly shaped in T2D versus controls but shifted rightward, particularly at low insulin concentrations. Equivalent T1D-specific stepped-clamp data with sufficient steady-state plateaus are sparse.

**Interstitial vs. plasma kinetics.** Whole-body dose-response curves are typically plotted against plasma insulin concentration, but biological effects depend on interstitial (tissue) concentrations, which lag and attenuate plasma concentrations at a rate that is itself insulin-dependent. Sjöstrand et al. (2002) estimated interstitial-to-plasma ratios in forearm muscle of ~0.35–0.55 at steady state, meaning tissue EC50 values in absolute concentration terms differ from plasma-referenced values reported in most clamp studies. Models that apply plasma-referenced EC50 values directly to effect compartments introduce a systematic error.

**Hill coefficient uncertainty.** Published Hill coefficients for whole-body insulin action span n ≈ 1.2–2.8 across studies, depending on subject population, insulin assay method, and whether glucose production or disposal is the endpoint. The spread is wide enough that the choice of n substantially affects model behavior near EC50 — steeper curves (n = 2.5) produce sharper threshold behavior than shallower ones (n = 1.3). Individual calibration of n is rarely performed in clinical modeling contexts.

### Modeling considerations

Compartmental models of insulin action (Hovorka 2002; Cobelli et al. 2014) typically implement linear effect-compartment relationships (x1, x2, x3 proportional to interstitial insulin concentration). This linearity is a deliberate simplification that approximates the steep portion of the sigmoidal curve within a physiological operating range, but misrepresents behavior at sub-threshold and supraphysiological concentrations. Replacing linear effect terms with Hill functions of the form E(Q2) = E_max × Q2^n / (EC50^n + Q2^n), where Q2 is the remote (interstitial) insulin compartment, would allow tissue-specific dose-response saturation to be captured with two additional parameters per effect channel (EC50, n). Population estimates from Rizza et al. (1981) and Natali et al. (2000) provide reasonable starting values, with the expectation that individual calibration would be required for accurate personalized simulation. The practical impact on simulated glucose trajectories is largest for (i) small correction doses in the sub-threshold zone, (ii) dose-stacking scenarios, and (iii) modulation by exercise, FFA, or glucotoxicity.

Adipose anti-lipolysis already uses a separate Hill response. The hepatic `x3` channel remains a documented linear early-response approximation because a literal plasma-referenced hepatic Hill curve was incompatible with the model's basal insulin scale. See [Non-linear Insulin Dose-Response](MODEL-IMPLEMENTATION.md#nonlinear-insulin) and the [implementation decision](reviews/2026-06-13_nonlinear-insulin-dose-response-decision.md).
